# A20. Executing Entity

* **Agence Française de Développement (AFD):** is a financing company and public institution acting as financier, advisor, partner and implementer to provide finance and expertise for development projects and developing countries.
* **PROPARCO:** is the subsidiary of AFD focused on private sector development. It provides funding, technical assistance, equity, and quasi-equity support (technical assistance and expertise) to business in Africa, Asia Pacific, Latin America and the Middle East. The infrastructure of finance projects represents 80% of its portfolio.
* **Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ):** is a public limited company with a sole shareholder being the Federal Ministry for Economic Affairs and Energy (BMWi). GIZ is headquartered in Bonn and Eschborn but operates from about 150 countries around the globe. 70% of its 26,000 employees are national staff.

# A21. Executive summary

(maximum 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

## Climate Context

Transport contributes almost one-quarter of current global energy-related greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions and is the fastest-growing sector. The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) projects that a temperature increase to two degrees Celsius requires changing this transport emissions trajectory, which involves the electrification of vehicles and infrastructure. This must be coupled with the low-carbon production of electricity and hydrogen (Paris Declaration on Electric Mobility - 2018). The International Energy Agency (IEA) modeling says EVs need to represent 35% of global sales in 2030.

## Avoid-Shift-Improve (ASI)

ASi has been a long-standing framework for classifying mitigation interventions in the transport sector. A new dimension needs to be added to this framework: EASi: Electric-Avoid Shift-Improve. This is because ASi does not consider the electrification of vehicles and infrastructure. Electrification measures without electrification are not capable to reach carbon neutrality in the transport sector. Electric vehicles (EVs) have a significant role to play, but they need to be deployed at scale. The IEA recognized this fact and have started to develop a regulatory framework to go toward this direction by introducing Electric-Only Zones (EOZ), 100% EV targets, and favourable regulation systems e.g., for battery swapping (IEA 81 and report 2 Report 1). EASi also considers the impact of electrification on electricity demand and thus cost and challenges in the energy system. Additionally, other changes in the sector, such as congestion, safety, access, health can be addressed through the use of more efficient


---
**Page 11**
---

models of transport such as buses, and through non-motorised alternatives. Developing an electrified public transport as the backbone of urban transport is therefore key to a sustainable mobility system.

## Social and Environmental Context

Air pollution is a large problem in most Latin American cities with levels affecting seriously human health and quality of life. The WHO air quality database, 85% of cities in low- and middle-income countries with more than 100,000 inhabitants do not meet World Health Organization (WHO) air quality guidelines. The WHO estimates that annually 42 million deaths result due to excessive exposure to fine particulate matter (PM2.5), which is equivalent to 7.6% of all deaths. The transport sector is a major source of air pollution.

The COVID-19 pandemic has created an unprecedented challenge for Latin America, countries, cities and communities. It is also opening up opportunities for the region to achieve sustainable development goals (SDGs) through a transformation of its energy, transport, and urban landscapes while contributing to economic recovery from the pandemic. The International Labour Organization (ILO) shows that fostering of electric mobility can have a significant positive job impact (ILO, 2020).

## Country Conditions and Ownership

Latin America has strong enabling conditions in terms of clean air quality and mobility. The region has one of the highest levels of electrification in the world with the median of Program countries having a carbon grid factor of 3.252 kgCO2e/kWh. The adoption of an e-mobility strategy would allow the countries to move towards a decarbonisation pathway. The updated Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) for Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) have included electric mobility as a key mitigation measure to reduce transport emissions and air pollution. For example, the NDC of Chile includes an ambitious target of 10% of new vehicles to be electrified by 2030.

The main motivating factors to foster e-mobility in the countries included in the Program are: (i) reduce GHG emissions; (ii) improve air quality and reduce health problems associated with air pollution; (iii) reduce dependency on imported fossil fuels and exposure to external price shocks; (iv) achieve positive economic impacts through job creation, increased competitiveness of industries, and new business opportunities; (v) foster a new growth industry of EV and battery manufacturing; (vi) increase the attractiveness of public transportation via improved service quality.

This Program has been developed in close coordination with CAF whose FP91 has been approved by the GCF at SBM and which implements similar activities in Uruguay, Paraguay and Panama (private and public sectors). The Program will be closely coordinated by CAF, GEF and GCF and shall be implemented under the same label "E-Motion".

## Intervention Strategy

The Programs goal is to accelerate Electric Vehicles (EV) deployment and enable a large-scale regional transition towards electric mobility in LAC through the financial and technical assistance. The Program will support countries in their transition from fossil-fuelled vehicles by implementing a Business as Usual (BAU) scenario by reducing the risk profile of investments and by comprehensive technical assistance to overcome barriers to EV adoption. This will be achieved by developing an enabling a faster uptake of e-mobility than under a BAU scenario avoiding a lock-in of long-lived assets in fossil technology. The Program fits the gap between initial market readiness existing in the region and longer-term deployment of electric vehicles. It is important to note that the transition towards electric mobility thus require initial investment support - like the case in all countries which have a significant uptake of e- mobility.

The Program focus is on electrification of public transport buses. Electrification of buses is not a stand-alone measure but an important component to modernize and to upgrade the public transport system


---
**Page 12**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 6

countries, and electric buses position the public transport system as modern, environmentally friendly and attractive. The investment in electric buses is linked with investments to improve the public transport system and non-motorized transport (NMT) in urban areas as well as traffic demand management measures. Electric buses are a key element of the transition towards sustainable urban mobility systems that shift a mode shift towards zero or ultra-low mobility systems. The Program directly modernizes public transport systems by improving the efficiency, capacity, reliability, and safety of public transport services through increased efficiency of public transport systems e.g. through separation of bus ownership and bus operations and indirectly by reducing or eliminating fossil fuel use in buses. Electric buses are a key element of sustainable urban mobility internationally i.e., the complementary role of e-bus investments to such efforts is a selection criteria for investment projects of the Program. As example medium sized Colombian cities which are currently investing in electric buses, also have plans to invest in NMT and other transport infrastructure upgrading public transport whilst also fostering NMT and components of transit-oriented development.

Program activities improve the grid resilience and the resilience of urban infrastructure. All investments generate a second life application for the battery pack of electric buses. The batteries of electric buses have a second-life application where they can serve as storage units and thereby (i) increase the grid and resilience of the grid at critical spots such as hospitals or schools; (ii) increase the grid and resilience and reduce the risk of blackouts in urban areas; (iii) reduce the risk of battery pack fires and subsequent over-supply; (iv) act as peak shavers in urban settings.

The program will also benefit from the activities implemented by GEF and AFD in all program countries. The main objective of these activities is to support the development of policies for urban mobility and integrated multi-modal planning at the city level, in order to accelerate the transition of Latin American cities towards sustainable urban mobility systems. These activities are expected to contribute to the favorable conditions for the development of e-mobility in the framework of integrated and sustainable urban mobility plans. ---
**Page 13**
---

without the proof of concept have limited impact. Stakeholders consulted by the Program have repeatedly stressed the point that investment projects involving significant fleets (not pilot projects) need to be implemented prior to taking critical regulatory decisions which affect public transport services. The proof of concept is not a sufficient basis for policy advice, and the Program has therefore been unable to provide such advice. The proof of concept approach accompanied and followed by policy advice is therefore core to this Program. Not all concepts are suitable for implementation in all cities. However, the Program will support the development of innovative business models are already under trial. The experience of Columbus as well as Bogota or Santiago cannot be transferred to medium and smaller cities where for example private capital has much less interest to participate due to offering smaller markets, financially fragile transport operators and a lack of political commitment. In such cases, the Program will support the development of charging infrastructure, which is a core barrier for passenger car deployment, is not tailored as barrier due to the fact that it can be implemented in parallel with the public charging network but have prepayment chargers either at the bus depot or alongside to their routes.

The Program tackles these barriers and offers a comprehensive e-mobility package including financial and technical assistance thereby improving the policy framework and ecosystem and removing barriers that exclude electric vehicles from being deployed. The Program will support cities in implementing the CAPEX and OPEX of the charging infrastructure, which is a core barrier for passenger car deployment, by tackling through concessional loans and through technical assistance (TA) which promote new business models and innovative financing mechanisms. TA will also be provided to support the development of design which reduces costs and operational risks. The barriers of lack of financial profitability and high risk perception are tackled through concessional loans and TA that promotes new business models and systems, which has a knowledge exchange platform and allows to learn from experiences of other cities in Latin America, and through training and capacity building of staff. The policy and regulatory work done with TTA will be complemented by the development of a technical assistance package (TAP) which includes contract lengths in line with the (longer) lifespan of e-buses or through tender documents which favour the purchase of electric vehicles. The TAP will also include the development of a business model for policy and regulatory work build upon the proof of concept investment projects and uses these to promote successful and workable policies based on concrete results. Stakeholders from the government and private sector will be involved in the design of the TAP, which is designed specifically for electric buses vehicles but it needs to be demonstrated locally that this will not affect adversely the public transport system.

Program Components

For each country a market assessment has been performed analyzing (i) the current financial viability per vehicle category in terms of total cost of ownership, profitability, cash flow and debt structure; (ii) financial instruments available for financing electric vehicles and related infrastructure. Interventions are designed to accelerate market readiness and to foster widespread commercial deployment of EVs without further financial assistance.

Financing is provided through the selection of investment projects in different countries focusing on e-bus systems and PT upgrading. The selection is based on the criteria established in section B3. E-mobility system investments include vehicles, charging infrastructure, grid upgrades and bus depot upgrades and are selected by the Program based on a combination of financial viability and cost-effectiveness criteria. 35% of the total GCF funding. All countries receive technical assistance to enhance their e-mobility technology readiness level (TRL). The selection of projects for each country is made in consultation with the national authorities, and no country is included from proposing investment projects. During Program design city authorities, financial institutions and other relevant stakeholders can be involved in the identification of potential replacement or expansion of base fleets and where interest would exist to purchase electric units if

Technically and financially viable. The individual projects are listed in the country assessments included in Annex 3. For facility of review a concise sheet has been created listing all pre-invested project items (see Annex 3: List Investment Projects).

The Program is designed to achieve its outputs under Element 1.1: Establishment of a sustainable mobility ecosystem encouraging electrification and a shift to public transport. Work will be done to overcome the barriers associated with this output through the implementation of a set of activities that were specifically selected for their potential to have a high impact on the achievement of the Program’s main outputs.

Following TA activities are implemented in Argentina, Brazil, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Ecuador, El Salvador, Guatemala, Guyana, Honduras, Jamaica, Mexico, Nicaragua, Panama, Paraguay and regional level by providing TA to create a policy and business framework conducive for massive deployment of EVs on a local level (project design, business model design and development), city EV policies and strategies, national EV policies and strategies, public procurement policies, incentives policies for EV deployment, advice on battery re-use, recycling and disposal) and a regional level (capacity building for policy makers, technical assistance to cities, support to the development of business models and development); (ii) Program management. The program has in addition a comprehensive and ambitious gender action plan targeting to improve gender equity and reduce sexual harassment in public transport.

## Program Impacts

The Program supports a paradigm change to low-emission transportation systems. Impacts to be achieved are: (i) Increased adoption of EVs which have significantly lower lifecycle GHG emissions than fossil units in all countries included in the Program and mode shift triggered through program investments and technical assistance from modes with high levels of carbon emissions per passenger km to low carbon transport modes such as public transport, walking and cycling; (ii) Reduced oil dependency; (iii) Increased energy efficiency: EVs are up to 4x more energy efficient than fossil vehicles.

Program key figures are:

* Total Program volume 210.98 MEUR and 103.89 MUSD
* GCF investment 56 MEUR and 40.264 MUSD of concessional loan and 22.3 MEUR grant for TA
* Co-financing 70 MEUR and 29.636 MUSD
* Direct mitigation impact over lifetime of assets: 4,334.051 MtCO₂e.
* Direct GHG impact avoided per year: 8,668 tCO₂e saved.
* Indirect large GHG impact due to accelerated uptake of EVs.
* Economic internal rate of return of 16% of the total Program

## Program Sustainability

E-mobility is still in a learning stage in Latin America. It therefore requires at this stage TA and H. The only way to make the program that the Program is only required for a transitional period. By 2025 the cost of e-buses is expected to be at or below fossil units. However, the expectation is that even if total cost of ownership of e-buses is below those of fossil units, this does not automatically drive in operators purchasing decisions. The main reason being that the current fleet management experience with fossil buses requires also more equity investors and risks associated with e-buses (including risks of adequate maintenance infrastructure). In addition, the current fleet management experience available in the country which clearly show that a-buses have comparable reliability and availability rates as fossil units, that actual operational costs are as expected (perceived risk of much higher operational costs is not supported by evidence), and that the cost of e-buses can be managed this is also related in many countries to concerns over purchasing buses from countries with no local maintenance structure at place. The investments in projects and the TA allow to overcome the barriers of profitability and high upfront CAPEX through successfully implementing new business models


---
**Page 15**
---

and a regulatory and policy environment which favour E-mobility investments. Interchange of experience and knowledge management instruments allow to learn from experiences and to structure in an optimal manner new investment projects thereby reducing costs and risks. Thus market forces of decreasing prices for electric vehicles, batteries and charging infrastructure will lead to lower risk and higher profitability for follow-up investors in e-mobility, which will not longer require further technical or financial support.

### Program Replicability and Transformational Impact

The potential market for e-mobility is very large in Latin America. However, especially secondary cities are facing financial and technical barriers towards adopting e-mobility. Whilst pilot projects have been successfully implemented in several countries of the region, there has not yet been a scaling up of the Program to invest in large-scale application combined with TA to foster new business models and with knowledge management instruments which allow for optimal e-mobility system design, learning from experience and replication of best practices. The proposed Program will address these barriers and allow for a transformational impact towards mass deployment of commercial e-mobility in Latin America. A more detailed description on the scaling up approach of the Program was provided in section 3.2 which is included in details in report 3 per country attached under Annex 3 of this FP and also available in excel version under File per country.

### Program Pipeline

The investment Program pipeline includes 4 investment projects (among which 50% in secondary cities) to be realized in 2023/2024 covering the total available FA. The full list of initially identified potential investments is provided in Annex 1 of this FP and will be updated on a regular basis as part of the proposed Program. This is a clear indication of the demand for such investments. Many investments are for fleet renovation over various years where a first batch could be financed with future funding being brought forward to cover the remaining investment needs. However, several projects identified in the planned implementation 2023 could not be implemented due to lack of adequate finance. Other operators e.g., public transport companies and private fleets have expressed interest in implementing projects based on fulfillment of all Program investment criteria, on being projects with a high replication potential and demand driven i.e. first-come-first served. The Technical Assistance activities of the Program contribute significantly towards this objective by providing technical support to operators who wish to implement Technical Assistance for Projects feasibility studies and to structure projects from an operational, financial and legal perspective.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

**B.1. Climate component (max. 1000 words, approximately two pages)**

Green Climate Fund Proposal and the Role of Sustainable Transport (incl. Electric Mobility)

Transport contributes almost a quarter of the current global energy-related GHG emissions and is growing faster than any other energy end-use sector. Latin America already accounts for 10% of global GHG emissions as of today, and transportation-related GHG emissions from many countries in Latin America are projected to increase by more than 50% between now and 2030. Argentina (13%), Brazil (18%), Colombia (16%), Costa Rica (37%), Dominican Republic (19%), Mexico (24%) and Uruguay (18%) have GHG emissions from transport that are projected to be at least twice as high in 2050 compared with today, far higher than the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) countries but is an essential sector of their economies. The Paris Agreement calls on all parties to take action to limit global warming to well below 2°C above pre-industrial levels and to pursue efforts to limit it even further to 1.5°C. To achieve this, the transport sector must reduce its GHG emissions by at least 90% compared with today’s levels by 2050.

Under a Business as Usual scenario World Data Lab calculations come to 13GtCO2 of transport emissions by 2050 which is 67% more than 2022 and reflects a 1.9% annual growth rate of transport emissions*. This implies that even if all countries were to implement the Paris Agreement, the projected GHG emissions of the transport sector would continue growing albeit at a slower pace (see figure below).

Avoid Shift-Improve (ASI) has been a long-standing framework for classifying mitigation interventions in the transport sector. Electrification is part of the Improve dimension and a paradigm change and not just hybrid vehicles, eco-drive, increased vehicle occupation rates etc., i.e. typical efficiency measures.

Electrification coupled with zero-emission buses and other electric vehicles (EVs) and no improve measures will be capable to reach carbon neutrality in the transport sector. Electrification coupled with a fossil-free grid creates this opportunity. Not surprisingly do carbon neutrality plans involve a huge increase of public transport, especially electrified buses, which have a much lower emissions per passenger-kilometer than diesel buses, even including a life-cycle approach.

Electrification of public transport buses is an important component to modernize and to upgrade the public transport systems in developing countries. Electrification can also incentivise passengers to use public transportation. E-mobility is a game changer as new technology and higher quality enables cities to rethink and plan their entire public transport system.

The role of electrification in transformational public transport systems through establishing new business models which increase the financial stability and economic efficiency of public transport operators and public transport systems e.g., through separation of bus ownership and bus operations and the introduction of a leasing model for buses. The electrification of public transport is also an important component of public transportation due to the different financial structure of busses with a high upfront investment and low operating costs.

Public-private partnerships (PPPs) in the context of e-mobility are increasingly being used as a partnership kick-off that can be clearly observed in Chile and in Colombia, the 2 countries in Latin America which have already embarked on underlying public transport reforms. In both cases large private sector models to modernize their bus fleets and to upgrade public transport services. In both cases large capital heavy investors have entered the market (electric utilities, private capital funds and bus manufacturers) which results in public transport operations being financially more solid (in both countries

## Accelerating Transformation Towards Low-Carbon Mobility

By 2022, there were about 18 million full-size EVs on the world’s roads[6]. These have nearly 90% of their lifecycle GHG emissions in Europe and North America, but are still at an infant stage in Latin American countries and lag far behind Asia or Europe. None of the participating countries have reached 15,000 EVs yet. Reaching this milestone would require a significant increase in production capacity over the next 3-5 years depending on the department category and the country[7]. End 2022: 700,000 battery electric buses were operating worldwide of which 670,000 in China[8]. In Latin America in total (including trolleybuses)

The Programs core function is to accelerate EV deployment in involved countries through financial and technical assistance. The programs are designed as a bridge between the current pilot stage at scale transition towards e-mobility and closes the gap between the current pilot stage and commercial large-scale sustained growth of electric bus fleets. This is done by providing financial support for the purchase of buses, if successful business models are established, if the government is willing to cover the viability gap and/or if creative financial structuring is done as is shown e.g. by Chile or Bogotá. This linked with the experience gained from the pilot stage and the lessons learned from other countries and cities allows for a mass replication of the interventions.## Intervention Window of E-Motion Program

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| 2021 | 2022-2026 |
| 0% | 5% |
| 100% | Sustainable commercial market growth |

The Program has a strong focus on electrification, especially in its financial component. It implements the relevant measures to lock-in the deployment of electric buses significantly earlier than with a BAU scenario by reducing the risk profile of investments and by comprehensive technical assistance. The Program is focused in countries and vehicle segments where this gap can be closed i.e., where conditions for commercial large-scale sustained growth are met. This means that the transition towards sustainable e-mobility mobility feasible within 7 years by kick-starting the process and by eliminating barriers to market entry, such as lack of infrastructure, lack of charging points, lack of technical expertise and activities as well as support levels at all in countries. The Program is thus not about pilots and initial technical assistance, which already exist in the region, but about accelerating market-based commercial EV deployment.

## Strengthening Public Transport

Electrification is an enabler for improving not only vehicles but increasing services quality and working with partners on policies for incentives to shift to public transport and increasing ridership. Introduction of EVs is considered a key element in the electrification strategy for urban mobility systems. Projects with busses are located in cities which want to upgrade their urban mobility system and introduce electric buses.

Electric mobility represents a lever to shift the underlying paradigm that private vehicles are the preferred mode of transport. Introducing a large number of electric vehicles into the fleet requires transport operators and public authorities to consider the full range of impacts on the urban environment. Market-based commercial EV deployment across Latin America cannot be guaranteed by electric buses solely achieving cost-party with rival technologies. Perceptions of risk, operational practices, business models, and other factors will also need to change in order to incorporate sustainable urban mobility systems, including non-motorised transport (NMT), must also change.

Most existing EU bus electrification initiatives focus only on the exclusion of diesel vehicles and the provision of the necessary infrastructure for charging. In contrast, the aim with the new system is often only a few vehicles running on one or two lines are replaced. Initiating a larger-scale replacement of vehicles offers the best opportunity to also re-think the urban mobility system in order to create an attractive alternative to private vehicles, utilising the advantages of vehicles to trigger a systemic change towards a more diverse mobility environment centred around public transport and non-motorised and low emission modes.

### Figure 2 illustrates the push and pull elements that enable a shift towards more use of public transport, and if well-connected, to walking and cycling. The goal is to enhance efficiency of public transport systems and make them more attractive for users in order to reduce private car usage and contribute to the transition away from fossil fuels and in declining public transport ridership. Shifting to electric buses can contribute to this by providing more attractive vehicles.

The programme will provide financial assistance to de-risk financial products and accelerate the deployment of e-buses, including through the development of a framework for the identification of conducive policy conditions, as well as the identification of innovative business models and contractual arrangements that are suitable for public transport systems in Latin America. The programme will also support the implementation of the E-Motion project.

The upgrade of the vehicle fleet is an opportunity for reforming the transport systems in cities. Electric mobility offers an unique opportunity to restructure the PT sector, introduce new PPP and business models, and improve service quality. It can help to reduce operating costs, increase productivity, strengthen powers, and all this leading to the structuring of a modern, attractive and integrated offer to potential users of PT. This is why the technical assistance component goes beyond electrification and concerns the development of an overall strategy for the deployment of electric buses in cities with low-carbon emissions. Customers might also appreciate an EV more due to less noise or due to a positive image associated with it.

The programme will support the implementation of the E-Motion project, which aims at deploying 100 eVAs and therefore additional EVs. A report found for example that if buses could attract 19% additional ridership in cities, this would be equivalent to 25 million passengers per year (Curie, 2018). More importantly however the impact goes beyond the reorganization and modernization of public transport systems through sustainable bus-based models which increase the financial stability and economic efficiency of public transport operators and public transport systems. It also contributes to a reduction in CO2 emissions by replacing fossil fuel carbon vehicles but also in modernization of the public transportation system increasing its attractiveness.

The E-Motion programme will also have a significant impact on the development of sustainable and low carbon cities, as it is aligned with the objectives of the EU’s Horizon 2020 Programme, the EUROCLIMA program, implemented by AFD and GIZ, and that establishes all the enabling conditions to do this shift towards sustainable transport in the region through the implementation of measures for the period from 2014-2020. The E-Motion project is aligned with the EUROCLIMA+ Programme: Initiatives 5 (Urban American national governments National Urban Mobility Plan (NUP), a Sustainable Urban Mobility Plans (SUMPs) and other urban mobility plans, as well as the implementation of pilot projects in Costa Rica and 3 pilot projects. The Budgetary credit lines financed by AFD and supporting a high level political dialogue on transport decarbonization in Costa Rica and Dominican Republic also ensure that the projects

## Local Context

Latin America has strong enabling conditions in terms of clean transport and e-mobility. The region has one of the cheapest electricity generation portfolios in the world (high hydroelectric production and growing deployment of wind and solar power plants). Therefore, the adoption of an e-mobility strategy would allow the region to reduce its GHG emissions by 50% compared to fossil fuels.

### Table: Carbon Grid Factor of Program Countries

| Country | Carbon Grid Factor in kgCO2e/kWh |
| --- | --- |
| Argentina | 0.3986 |
| Brazil | 0.4178 |
| Colombia | 0.2058 |
| Costa Rica | 0.2058 |
| Dominican Republic | 0.4266 |
| Mexico | 0.2522 |
| Peru | 0.2522 |
| Uruguay | 0.397 |

Source: IFI Version 3.2, 2022

Latin America is also well known for having developed innovative bus-based transportation systems (especially in Colombia and Chile) and cities like Bogotá, Medellín and Curitiba have been internationally recognized as leaders in sustainable urban mobility.

The region has a strong automotive industry with a large number of auto manufacturers and auto manufacturing companies. Three large vehicle producers operate in the region (Argentina, Brazil and Mexico) and four countries have large auto manufacturing plants (Brazil, Colombia, Peru and Uruguay). The main economic drivers for e-mobility are: (i) Reduce GHG emissions; (ii) Improve air quality and reduce health problems associated with air pollution; (iii) Reduce dependence on imported oil; (iv) Promote the development of a new industry in electric vehicle manufacturing; (v) Modernize the public transport system to increase its attractiveness and mode share; (vi) Achieve positive economic impacts by creating jobs, increasing exports, reducing import bill and job creation; (vii) Participate in the new growth industry of EV and battery manufacturing.

Air pollution is a large problem in most Latin American cities with levels affecting seriously human health. The WHO estimates that 42 million people living in urban areas with more than 100,000 inhabitants do not meet WHO air quality guidelines*. The WHO estimates that approximately 4.2 million deaths result due to excessive exposure to fine particulate matter equivalent to 7.6% of all premature deaths worldwide. Children and the elderly are particularly vulnerable. Recent studies show that women are more affected by poor air quality than men**.

Argentina is one of the largest economies in Latin America. It is also, along with Brazil, Mexico and Chile, a major producer of electric vehicles (EVs). The country’s large fleet of public transport vehicles represents a unique opportunity for the transition towards electric mobility. The National Action Plan for Transport and Climate Change represents the set of initiatives that Argentina has planned to do to reduce GHG emissions in the transport sector by 2030, as well as to implement adaptation measures. Argentina launched the development of its National Electric Mobility Strategy in May 2019. This strategy includes a series of actions aimed at promoting the adoption and use of electric vehicles, establishing the conditions for the installation or operation of charging centres, and at the same time, exploring possible options for the local development of the electric mobility industry. Various pilots are being run with electric buses in Argentina.

The Brazilian automotive industry is among the ten most important in the world. The government promotes this sector as a strategic priority, with the aim of increasing its competitiveness on an international level. The transportation sector shall promote efficiency measures, improve urban public transportation infrastructure and boilers to achieve mitigation targets. Brazil has already established various tax incentives for the introduction of electric vehicles (EVs) into the country’s fleet. These incentives are part of the NDC strategy of the country and 21 bills are being discussed in parliament (tax reductions for fuel efficiency; stringent rules for manufacturers, import fee reduction).

## Peru

**Peru has very low levels of Crude and Natural Gas (CNG) prices due to having abundant natural gas resources. However, the country is currently not connected to any pipeline network for natural gas.**

### Barriers addressed by the Program
- **Limited commercial attractiveness of e-mobility investments:**
  - A core barrier to EV deployment in Peru is the 20x higher CAPEX of EVs including associated infrastructure and maintenance costs.
  - Higher CAPEX results in higher capital requirements, higher finance and insurance costs, and higher risk exposure due to higher debt levels. Higher CAPEX can be partially or fully offset by lower operating costs (e.g., fuel savings), but this is not sufficient to overcome the maintenance costs and a higher lifespan of EVs.
  - However, the profitability of EVs is only in the long-term and insecure.

## Key Challenges and Opportunities in Electric Vehicle (EV) Ecosystem Development

### 1. Limited Knowledge and Experience with Mobility Systems
This refers to a lack of know-how on how optimal EV ecosystem design (identification of types of EVs to use, fast/low/opportunity charging etc.), maintenance & operations, as well as an use battery management policies including options of 2nd-life usage, recycling and disposal. This barrier is addressed through the TA’s support in developing a comprehensive e-mobility package that includes financial and technical assistance thereby improving the policy framework and ecosystem and removing barriers which allows to kick-start mass deployment of EVs.

### 2. Business Models and Policies Non-Conducive to Sustainable Urban Mobility and EV Deployment
This barrier is prevalent in various countries as governance structures and business models are not conducive to sustainable urban mobility and EV deployment. Local regulations, contracts and incentives for public transport operators and private sector actors (e.g., concession periods which are too short to recover the high investment in a-buses) are risk-averse and do not incentivize innovation or long-term investments.

### 3. Limited Knowledge and Experience with Mobility Systems
This refers to a lack of know-how on how optimal EV ecosystem design (identification of types of EVs to use, fast/low/opportunity charging etc.), maintenance & operations, as well as an use battery management policies including options of 2nd-life usage, recycling and disposal. This refers to a lack of know-how on how optimal EV ecosystem design (identification of types of EVs to use, fast/low/opportunity charging etc.), maintenance & operations, as well as an use battery management policies including options of 2nd-life usage, recycling and disposal. ## GIZ – Changing Transport! Supporting partners in defining low carbon transport strategies.

Implementing climate actions to reduce emissions and harnessing sustainable transport benefits through intergovernmental cooperation is a key focus of the Global Environment Facility (GEF) Electric Mobility Program in Costa Rica and other initiatives at all the cities concerned by the Program. GIZ is also the organization in charge of delivering the TA activities of the e-mobility program.

### Related projects/interventions

Multiple actors are engaged in promotion of e-mobility, including: E-Motion Program implemented by AFD in 7 countries of the region (Argentina, Brazil, Colombia, Costa Rica, Peru, Mexico and Dominican Republic). The AFD FP and the GIZ project (implemented in the same countries) are part of the same E-Motion program and approach aiming to promote e-mobility in the region.

UN Environment is implementing the GCF Readiness Program: "Advancing a regional approach to e- mobility in Latin America and the Caribbean" which includes the development of strategies and policies to scale-up electric mobility. The Global Environment Facility (GEF) is also implementing through UNEP a Global E-Mobility Program at the world level including Costa Rica. The individual TA projects covered have been designed so that a complementarity exists on-going and planned initiatives of other actors.

The Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) is one an active e-mobility player in various countries, e.g. through its pilot fleet of 6 buses in Panama City. The IDB also supports the GCF e-mobility program (FP189) for the Latin American region, with complementarities with the E-Motion Program.

The GIZ project includes a pilot program in Costa Rica and other countries covered by the E-Motion Program including the FP189 program. The IDB program is also supporting the development of the pilot hydrogen projects as well as electric vessels with potential electric vessel projects having been identified for implementation in Panama, Colombia and Peru. These initiatives collectively have the potential to define a transformational change in the transportation system of the region.

Projects/programs in the region implemented by intergovernmental organizations and multilateral development banks are:

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 22

## B

### The E-Motion Program

The E-Motion program is a program implemented by AFG and GUZ with activities in the Program countries Argentina, Brazil, Colombia, Costa Rica, Mexico and Peru. Its objective is to develop national policies for urban mobility and integrated multi-modal planning at the city level to accelerate the transition towards sustainable transport systems that reduce greenhouse gas emissions. As a result, EUROLIMA+ contributes to create the favourable conditions of the development of electric vehicles (EVs) in these countries through the E-Motion program. This is therefore an important link to achieve the structural change of the urban transportation system toward more efficient and environmentally friendly solutions for sustainable urban mobility development. More specifically, with a total allocation of 10 million euros, Euromida has the following goals: (i) Support the National low carbon Urban Mobility Policy in Ecuador and promotion of the National Urban Mobility Plan (NUMP) in Mexico; (ii) Support the implementation of the following 7 Sustainable Urban Mobility Plan (SUMP), Regional Plan for Mobility and Logistics of Bogotá (Colombia), SUMP for the Municipality of Arequipa (Peru), Sustainable Urban Mobility Plan for Córdoba (Argentina), improvement of public transport in Tenerife (Spain) and the National Urban Mobility Plan in Mexico; (iii) Support the implementation of 3 pilot projects: Mobility Observatory to improve the performance of public transport in Tenerife (Brazo), Pilot Project on soft mobility in Córdoba (Argentina) and the E-Motion Platform. The E-Motion program is based on a platform/community of practice is strengthening exchanges and capacities of cities and national Latin American governments for NUMPs and SUMPs.

The E-Motion Program will leverage on ongoing programs of GIZ and Euromida - specifically through the following projects:

- **TRANSFR Colombia:** being a "project preparation facility", designed a national program, including the development of a business plan, identification of potential partners, technical assistance guarantees and subsidies. Furthermore, an MRV system was developed, training curricula for business models, technical operation and planning were developed and a government mechanism was established.
- **MiTransporte:** the project implemented an electric bus pilot, including data collection, which included the development of a business plan, identification of potential partners, technical assistance guarantees and subsidies. Furthermore, an MRV system was developed, training curricula for business models, technical operation and planning were developed and a government mechanism was established.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 23

#### B

**TURB Bus Mission:** This project is supporting 10 Deep Dive Cities in Brazil, Colombia and Mexico to set ambitious targets for bus deployment, and to elaborate and implement a work plan for tendering electric buses.

- **UNEP Country Project Support to Countries with the Shift to Electric Mobility**: The aim of the Global Programme (covering Antigua & Barbuda, Chile, Costa Rica, Ecuador, Grenada, Jamaica, Peru and St. Lucia in CA) is to support low and middle-income countries in overcoming these barriers by providing technical assistance and capacity building for policy development and implementation.

- **Country Project Level:** The project will provide technical assistance at the country level on the Country Child Project level includes awareness raising campaigns, capacity building and the development of national action plans. The project will also provide support to the public sector reformer including laying the ground for large-scale market introduction of electric mobility through 1) The development of a regulatory framework, including legal and regulatory issues; 2) The identification of suitable business models, financing mechanisms and incentives; and 3) The development of plans and studies to ensure environmental sustainability. The investment component of the Country Child Project will be up to $50 million.

- **UNEQ GCF Readiness Programme on E-Mobility in Latin America:** The UNEP Regional Office in Panama currently implements a GCF Readiness Programme on E-Mobility with the title "Advancing a regional approach for the deployment of electric mobility in Latin America and the Caribbean". Costa Rica. The project aims at building capacity, development of e-mobility policies and business models, identification of strategies to finance the shift to electric mobility in the region and will be implemented through a combination of technical assistance and financial support. A proposal is sought for with the GEF Global E-Mobility Programme.

- **UNEQ - Move Electric Mobility in Latin America:** In 2016, UNEP and Marc Morin Centre Chile launched the initiative to promote the deployment of electric mobility in Latin America. Since then, Move has provided (1) capacity building to public technical officers from almost 20 countries in the region; (2) financial support for the development of national action plans on electric vehicle and e-mobility in Latin America and the Caribbean; (3) technical assistance, through the elaboration of National Electric Mobility Strategies in The Program countries Colombia and Argentina and (4) resource mobilization to support the implementation of these strategies.

The ongoing and planned project / programmes are addressing the current invisibility of socially and economically viable alternatives to fossil fuel road transport. However, despite those ongoing initiatives being implemented at a regional level, there is still a need for further action in order to reach the extent to which the projects focus only on technical assistance lacking financial instruments and not always allowing for the development of enabling policies and regulatory frameworks. The key towards mobility, overcoming the investment barriers will be key in the Program countries. Gap finance and financial incentives are still missing to a larger extent. The E-Motion Programme will contribute significantly by providing financial support for the implementation of national action plans and complementary TA elements for commercial fleets and general enabling activities, which will help accelerate the transformation towards low carbon mobility in the respective countries.

### 8.2.6 Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

IF low carbon urban mobility systems are implemented and an enabling environment for a mobility can be established THEN the population will have access to affordable, attractive and convenient urban transport


---
**Page 30**
---

investments be interested in pouring resources into EV projects which helps the involved countries to meet their GHG reduction targets because transport systems will have a more gender balanced and comprehensive mobility offer using low carbon EVs.

The potential for electrification at scale is a shift from the current reliance on fossil-fuel powered transportation towards electrified low-emission transport means. The Programs technical and financial assistance in the design and implementation of business models in line with the specific needs of each country will be key to this transition. This includes the identification, development and implementation of business models which tackle the barrier of high upfront investments pull in other renewable energy projects. The Program is also designed to support the scaling up of electric vehicles through the Programs investments in electrified urban transport combined with the market trend of strongly decreasing prices of EVs allow for solutions to be replicated across other cities in Program countries. This is enabled through the identification and implementation of business models which are attractive to private investors, implementation projects which reduce decisively the risk levels for follow-up investors. The paradigm shift is a combination of the availability of low-cost financing for electrification of urban transport infrastructure combined with a market where EVs will achieve price parity with fossil units within this decade. The interventions make cities more livable resulting also in a behavioural change towards public transport and walking, which are key to reducing emissions from road transport. This is coupled with commercial mass-uptake of e-mobility and the interaction with the private sector in the implementation of projects that will be attractive for follow-up investors. The Program is designed to support cities moving towards low-emission electric mobility happens faster and at a much larger scale than under a BAU approach. The Program will also help countries to identify and implement business models which meet conditions (e.g., guarantees, concession contracts) and the performance risks of e-buses are manageable. This has been the case in Santiago de Chile as well as Bogota. A coalition of investors announced end 2017 a $5 billion commitment from investors to deliver zero emission buses in Latin America - C40 Cities which is favourable to actually materialize such investments and the investment component of the Program will help reduce risks to investors and operators. The Program is thus critical to unlock the private investment potential.

The expected outcomes of the Program are (i) improved public and private sector capacity and a more favorable business environment for electrification of urban transport infrastructure; (ii) increased transport sector output due to a shift from private to public modes of transport and due to a shift from fossil to electric means of transportation resulting in 4.33 MCO2, decreased GHG emissions and reduced energy use; (iii) improved air quality and health outcomes for citizens as well as increased road safety; (iv) mobility efficient than fossil units and FTB being more energy efficient per passenger-km than other modes of transport.

Expected co-benefit outcomes are: (i) Reduced economic & social cost of air pollution due to an improved air quality resulting in environmental co-benefits of 41 tons less PM2.5 and 8,482 tons lower NOx emissions; (ii) Increased access to public transport for women as well as mobility for economic benefit by women; and gender benefits due to implementing measures which favour a shift from private to public transport by women; (iii) increased climate resilience of urban transport infrastructure.

Outputs of the Program are: (i. 1) Establishment of a sustainable mobility ecosystem encouraging electrification of urban transport infrastructure, including electric buses with FTB purposes to foster mode shift to low carbon transport. This is made stronger in attractiveness and convenience of PT including multi-modal integration; (ii. 2) Increased access to public transport for women as well as transit-oriented development, fostering of electric micro-mobility and gender sensitive interventions realized in 2 countries; (ii. 2.E) Electric bus fleets are deployed with innovative business models to enable the transition towards low-emission urban transport.

### Co-benefit: This program's main focus is on mitigation of climate change impacts and reduction of GHG emissions through fleet replacement. As such and due to a necessary investment proposal, the TA will be considered as a co-benefit of the program and considered as a co-benefit only. Measures related to adaptation will be addressed through the large technical assistance included in the program. This TA will look at aspects such as road infrastructure, drainage capacity, and other measures that can support the deployment of non-motorised transport (NMT) along and around mass transit corridors, conditions of drainage capacity through the selection of porous materials for NMT infrastructure, inclusion of bioswales, etc.

#### B.3. Project/programme intervention description (max 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

The program aims to stimulate the operation of more electric buses and increase their ridership. It plans to achieve this by creating an enabling environment for electric mobility, embedded in a policy and system environment that enhances the efficiency and attractiveness of public transport and thus make electric vehicles (EVs) more competitive.

The Program has a comprehensive approach and includes implementation-oriented activities such as targeted policy advice for an integrated sustainable urban mobility and transport demand management, coordination with other stakeholders to ensure alignment between policies and investments, combined with financial instruments to ensure deployment of large-scale fleets. The establishment and operation of large-scale EV fleets works as proof of concept of the commercial viability of EVs and fosters a conducive environment for further investment in electric vehicles.

The program will also include targeted interventions aimed at overcoming current market barriers, such as high upfront costs, limited charging infrastructure, and measures that increase ridership, thus enhancing the financial viability of public transport systems.

For each country a market assessment (see for details section below) has been performed analysing (i) the existing fleet structure; (ii) the level of readiness; (iii) financing, primarily payback period, cash-flow and debt structure; (iv) required financial instruments to overcome current market viability barriers; (v) market readies of EVs in the specific country based on energy and vehicle prices and other factors such as urban planning and land use. The assessment will also identify opportunities for widespread commercial deployment of EVs without further financial assistance. Therefore interventions in each country are targeted to service segments where: (i) existing conditions are favourable for EVs in terms of policies, energy prices, interest of investors; (ii) limited financial support can make a decisive

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 28

## B

### Change and market conditions allow in the medium term (end of the Program) for a sustained uptake of EVs based on commercial conditions.

A power sector assessment has been made in detail per country. This includes power availability, electricity generation, and expected grid capacity based on renewable energy (RE) trends. All countries have high RE potential and are committed to increasing their share of RE. These factors will result in a decrease of the carbon grid factor. Commercial EVs will only result in marginal incremental electricity usage compared with conventional vehicles. The main barrier for commercial electric vehicles is mainly for commercial vehicles the issue is much more on localized grids and e.g. supply of sufficient electricity and uptime of charging infrastructure. This is also an important issue to assess, optimal when, and where buses can be charged are assessed to find localised solutions per investment.

The Program is for commercial EVs and charging infrastructure related to the commercial EVs only. Private passenger cars and motorcycles are not financed. Commercial vehicles result in a much larger share of CO2 emissions than private vehicles. The Program will finance electric buses with the lowest abatement costs compared with private vehicles. With a small number of vehicles and a limited investment a large impact can be made on reducing CO2 emissions, which is more cost-effective for the environment and socially equitable and could result in a mode-shift from public to private transportation. Commercial EVs targeted are electric buses which have attracted the most attention from stakeholders in the involved countries.

The Program only includes 100% BEVs, hybrid electric vehicles (HEVs) and plug-in hybrid electric vehicles (PHEVs) are not included. HEVs and PHEVs were an intermediate technology as long as BEVs seem to be a more cost-effective solution for the environment. The environmental impact is limited by incremental investment but only have a limited environmental impact of around 20% of emission reduction compared with BEVs. This is due to the fact that the battery in HEVs and PHEVs are not recharged overnight resulting in an overhanging share of plug-in hybrid buses never be re-charged and thus only result in 20% energy and GHG savings (ADB, 2018). BEVs have a higher environmental impact due to the fact that they have no emissions during daily operations and frequent prolonged black-out periods on very demanding routes. PHEVs are not a commercially attractive option. The battery size of PHEVs is small resulting in an average range of only around 50 km. This makes it difficult for PHEVs to be used as public transport charging of more than 1 hour to re-charge their already small batteries. A recent study of CICT revealed that the cost per kWh of BEVs are lower compared with HEVs and PHEVs. BEVs have a higher powertrain, they are more complex and expensive to maintain due to having both electric and internal combustion powertrain. The limited operational savings are insufficient to recover incremental investments of PHEVs compared with BEVs.

The Program is focused on the use of hydrogen fuel cell buses (HFCBs) as an alternative for diesel buses in a life-cycle base 3-4 times more electricity than BEVs due to highly energy intensive hydrogen production. This means that the environmental impact of HFCBs is similar to BEVs and PHEVs, but if hydrogen supply is exclusively based on electrolysis using an overwhelming extent renewable energy. In such cases, hydrogen can be of interest especially for long-haul vehicles or large vessels. However, this will not be considered in the Program.

The Program focus is on electrification of commercial vehicles, especially public transport buses. As outlined in Section 8.1, electrification of buses is however not a stand-alone measure but an important component to increase the attractiveness of electric mobility and to make it more affordable. This will position the public transport system as modern, environmentally friendly and attractive. The investment in electrification of public transport buses will also have a positive impact on other modes of transport by improving the public transport system as well as non-motorized transport (NMT) in urban areas which result in increasing the attractiveness of all carbon forms of mobility. Together with transport demand management measures, this will contribute to reducing CO2 emissions and air pollution.

## Program Description

The program modernizes public transport systems through establishing new business models, which increases the financial stability and economic efficiency of public transport systems e.g. through separation of bus ownership and bus operations. Indirectly various urban mobility projects fostering NMT and public transport are supported by this program. The main objective is to promote the implementation of electric buses (e-buses) in cities with a high potential for take place (the complementarity of e-bus investments to such efforts is a selection criteria for investment proposals). In addition, the program supports the development of urban mobility plans (SUMPs) with the aim of modernizing and upgrading public transport while fostering NMT and components of transit-oriented development.

The program is designed to bridge this gap between the national commitment on large-scale electrification in the transport sector and the reality of cities implementing medium-sized electric buses. The program combines TA and FID efforts to promote the implementation of large-scale EV projects and contribute to the efficiency and attractiveness of public transport for both users and operators. It will also support the development of urban mobility plans (SUMPs) with the aim of modernizing and upgrading public transport while fostering NMT and components of transit-oriented development.

compared to a weighted average capital cost (WACC) of the transport sector, the dynamic payback, cash-flow and debt levels and required equity investment levels (see Annex 2). Whilst results differ between countries and vehicle segments core take-aways are:

* **No significant financial benefits:** EVs are not financially competitive in most country lower or at par with fossil units. However, this depends on various critical assumptions including life-time of EVs versus fossil vehicles, maintenance costs, energy usage and energy costs and battery replacement costs.
* **A critical barrier to adoption is the lack of infrastructure:** The availability of charging points, the quality of maintenance and availability of qualified maintenance staff and repairs respectively availability of spare parts are all key factors. In many countries, the availability of charging points is low, with charging rates being as low as 60% whilst diesel buses have, well maintained, 95%. This results in huge cost increases as well as difficulties to comply with the time-table and service offer. The reason for low bus fleet electrification rate is not only related to the lack of infrastructure but also to the fact that adequate maintenance and trained staff and lack of spare parts. Issues related with poor after-sales services are also a major barrier, especially in countries where there is no established network changing bus providers and bus technology. These barriers or problems can be overcome with adequate planning and investment in charging points, maintenance, training and contracts requiring on-site manufacturer staff and training.
* **The differential FIRR (profitability of the incremental investment in an EV versus a fossil vehicle due to operational expenditure savings) is some cases below the benchmark WACC which means the revenue stream from the sale of electricity will not be enough to cover the extra costs involved in changing the propulsion technology.**
* **The dynamic payback period can be long and in some cases exceeds the asset lifetime.**
* **EVs require 3-5x more investment capital, 3-4x higher equity investment** and higher loan volumes resulting in higher debt levels and increased financial risk. Most investors prefer to purchase more fossil vehicles thus renewing their fleet more rapidly or increasing revenues instead of investing in EVs.
* **EV investments result in prolonged periods of cumulative negative cash-flow which can result in liquidity problems of investors.**

This is not to say that there is scepticism concerning the reliability and performance of EVs and a lack of know-how on EV maintenance. Also, spare parts are scarce in the country without deployment of sufficient charging infrastructure. However, take and ride, although many acknowledge that this might be an interesting investment.
* **Regulations are often not conducive for EV investment e.g., the length of concession contracts is too short to cover the payback period or prepayment system penalties, are not conducive for other ownership structures.**

Future market development has been modelled based on expected future price development of EVs per sector and country as well as international trend forecasts of EV cost parity and battery prices (see Annexes 2 and 23 for a description of the modelling approach).

## Financial Support Instruments

The Program will provide loans that without initial support commercial EVs are not yet viable in Program countries. Concessional finance is required to promote EVs. No investment grants are given. Concessional loan conditions of GFDR for public sector projects and private sector projects are a 95% interest rate with a maturity period of up to 10 years.

The Program will give loans in Euro or USD. The maximum USD requested loans from the GCF under this Program will be $4,264 MUSD, and the maximum EUR requested loans from the GCFP will be $8 MEUR.

In absence of concessional loans investments in fleets will not take place. Initial financial support for large-scale e-bus fleets is required to overcome market barriers. The Program will provide grants for initial fleet deployment in this area is clear: No country worldwide has been able to deploy on a large-scale e-bus fleets without initial financial support (see Benchmark Report in Annex 22).

| Country | Incentive |
| --- | --- |
| China | Free charging, free cost subsidised; chargers and grid connection at 10% to 15% subsidized; electricity price incl. 3.5 years subsidized, decreasing rates since Program start in 2009 |

| Germany | 25 percentage points higher subsidy levels than FAME II; 75% of incremental costs for charging infrastructure, grid connection, bus depot upgrades subsidized plus coverage of 100% of the costs for the first 300 e-buses |
| Switzerland | 80-100% of incremental costs of buses and 100% of charging infrastructure and grid connection |

| Poland | 100% of incremental cost of entire e-bus system subsidized |

| USA | 75% of incremental costs for e-buses subsidised under Phase II; 100% of incremental costs for e-buses subsidised under Phase III during 10 years, phase II longer concession periods for e-buses compared to conventional buses |
| Chile | 100% of the incremental cost of e-bus plus chargers is paid in monthly instalments during 7 years; phase I and II longer concession periods for e-buses compared to the government savings on subsidies used on diesel fuel |
| Uruguay | 100% of the incremental cost of e-buses subsidised by the government savings on subsidies used on diesel fuel |

### Source: Granting Mechanism (2020); Benchmark Report in Annex 22

Countries consistently subsidized 80-100% of the entire e-bus system incremental cost for the initial phase. Countries decreased their subsidy rates significantly within few years (e.g., China, India, UK, Chile). The importance of upfront investment subsidies – independent of TCOs – to kick-start deployment of e-buses is clear: No country worldwide has been able to deploy on a large-scale e-bus fleets without initial financial support. Subsidies are gradually even be eliminated due to more competitive prices of e-buses combined with the experience of mass operations of e-buses. Concrete examples are[2] and[3].

## Business Models

Two components stand out and that improve in a decisive manner the attractiveness and commercial viability of e-bus projects: (i) bulk purchase—that will be promoted through the TA; and (ii) separation of asset ownership and operations/management. Bulk purchase is important not only due to purchasing economies but also because it allows for the development of a more efficient fleet infrastructure and bus types allowing e.g., to use fast-charged buses with common usage of chargers (up to 12 buses per charger). Separation of asset ownership & management is critical due to high investment costs, long-term nature of projects, and need for large-scale financing. In the case of public sector operators typically have a weak balance sheet and are financially preferred clients of Fis. Business models that allow for separation of asset ownership and operations/management can be applied not only to electric utilities but also bus manufacturers as well as private investment companies. To thrive such business models, it is important to have clear rules and regulations in place, the process in case of bankruptcies of parties, clear long-term concession contracts etc. Separation of asset ownership & management can be done with public led delivery systems (public asset owners) or with private led delivery systems (private asset owners). The business model will need to be adjusted and structured per country based on national and even local requirements, priorities and conditions. This shift in business models is a major opportunity for the sector and it is important to strengthen the ability to replicate this business model in Latin American cities. One of the main features of the Program will thus be to design, establish and implement such business models which can work as delivery systems for public transport.

This shift of business models incited by e-mobility can be clearly observed in Chile and in Colombia, where in both cases large cash-heavy investors have entered the market (electric utilities, private capital funds etc.) and are now leading the development of the sector. In Chile, the entry of private investors into countries bankruptcy of traditional bus operators has been a major problem), more efficient and with better service quality. The new business model allows for separation of asset ownership & management and allow for large investment firms with solid balance sheets and access to cheaper finance to enter the game. This has been the case for example in Chile or Colombia. The same is true also in Brazil, where private investors have entered the market and are now involved in modernization of the public transportation system increasing its attractiveness and thereby resulting in an important mode-shift towards public transport compared to a business as usual situation. ### Examples of Chile and Colombia in e-Bus Promotion

Chile and Colombia have been particularly successful in promoting e-buses. Why that? What was the game-changer?

The case of Chile is especially interesting due to the introduction of new business models separating bus ownership and bus operations. The first batch of 200 electric buses was based on investments of electric utilities (public sector) and private companies, with a focus on infrastructure development.

Critical for the first batch of e-buses was not only the investment of electric utilities but also a government decision to allow private operators to operate the buses under concession contracts. This led to concession contracts reducing risks for investors in vehicle assets and ensuring payments from vehicle operators. Additionally, the Chilean government provided incentives such as tax breaks and subsidies.

The case of Colombia is another example where the separation of ownership and operations was crucial. The Colombian government allowed private companies to operate e-buses under concession contracts with public utilities as suppliers and others being firms or bus operators with investment firms. Electric utilities are no longer engaged or involved in operating the buses, which has led to a more efficient use of resources.

In both cases, AMP Capital and NEIF Capital have entered as investors. The government no longer pays for incremental investment costs but still offers other advantages for e-buses such as longer concession periods and updated infrastructure.

Chilean case: (i) separation of ownership and operations can be an effective instrument to promote e-buses and reduce the cost of public transport; (ii) subsidies are critical; (iii) e-buses still require incentives to be competitive with fossil buses; (iv) required subsidies can be reduced significantly after an initial fleet of 40 buses and after having established a working business model.

# Colombia

Large Colombian cities already run successfully since many years BRT systems with private operators and a public system manager paying companies based on distance driven cum service delivery targets. e-buses have been introduced in Bogotá (2018) and Medellín (2019). The first tender (2017) was for 50 buses, awarded based on a tender which only allowed for the provision of e-buses. A subsequent second tender which allowed for any type of bus resulted in a single supplier winning with a conventional diesel bus. The third tender (2020) included significant additional points for e-buses as well as other environmentally friendly vehicles. The final award was made to a consortium of two companies, one of whom is the incumbent operator. The total cost per km of operation was 15% higher per km costs of buses which is being paid by the municipality. The experience of Colombia reveals three important points: (i) e-buses can be integrated into public transport operations and will be offered by private operators as a competitive alternative to conventional diesel buses; (ii) the cost per km for e-buses is 15% higher per km costs of buses which is being paid by the municipality. The experience of Colombia reveals three important points: (i) e-buses can be integrated into public transport operations and will be offered by private operators as a competitive alternative to conventional diesel buses; (ii) without financial incentives e-buses are commercially not yet competitive. However, some large cities such as Bogotá have already made the decision to invest in e-buses.

The importance of these business models for e-buses relies in their ability to reduce upfront CAPEX barriers which are larger than for fossil vehicle counterparts. The high CAPEX required for e-buses and long-plant life (20 years) makes it difficult for new entrants to make a business case, which can result in difficulties as concession contracts might be for a shorter duration than the payback time for e-buses, and operators might not have the financial strength to invest. The high CAPEX required for e-buses (there is only a very thin market currently used for e-buses). Without proper estimate of the re-sale value of used e-buses, it will be difficult to make an investment case for new entrants. High CAPEX also result in high finance costs and the importance of low interest rates. Asset separation can allow for new, financially stronger companies, to enter the market and act as SPV leasing vehicles as they have a lower risk profile than operators. The introduction of e-buses will be accompanied by an additionality of GCF resources by de-risking the sector and providing incentives to new actors to invest in this technology. This can be achieved through the use of public-private partnerships, concession contracts and companies willing to invest in urban transport schemes in concession contracts, regulations and the structuring of business models conducive for e-bus deployment. The delivery scheme for component 3 of the project (e-buses) can thereby be public or private led (see figures below).

## Public-Sector Led Delivery

In the case of Sovereign Investment Loans or Grants to directly finance Projects under this Funding Proposal, the AFD eligible borrower, acting as EE (EE at level 1) or a designated EE at level 2 (if different from the eligible borrower) will directly execute all project activities following AFID rules including definition of the project scope, preparation and management procurement processes under AFID rules, perform financial management and reporting of results.

Accordingly, the most simple execution arrangement corresponds to the structure presented in the Figure below. In this context, Subsidiary Agreements (Sovereign Investment Loans and Grants) are entered into between the AFD eligible borrower and the project entity. The AFD eligible borrower will either be EE or TE of transfer proceeds to a separate EE if this case a project execution agreement is normally required. The EE will be in charge of the execution of the Project and will carry out any procurement, AFD as EE will have an agreement with each EE, as required pursuant to the AMA.

The indicative structure presented in the above figure includes a private-public special vehicle (SPV)—instead of a PTP—as an EE if funds are channeled through an AFD eligible borrower. The SPV would typically be structured as a separate legal entity, with its own board and management team. The SPV or its corresponding national or subnational government agency would be established under the terms of a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) with the AFD. The SPV would have the responsibility for acquiring infrastructure assets. To this effect, the SPV may: (i) structure and raise financing from selected financiers and investors; (ii) procure the buses and charging equipment assets from an OEM; (iii) ensure the availability to the public of a fleet of electric buses that are interoperable with the charging infrastructure in its inventory. OEMs can also be involved directly in the SPV or team up with SPVs for tenders. The financing mechanism could include leasing, purchase, and/or other arrangements. The SPV can include the vehicles plus charging infrastructure only or it can also include service and maintenance.

### PTO Public Transport Operator

In case of a private sector-led delivery the GCF loan is a public sector loan (e.g. to the MoF) with sovereign guarantee or either a loan without sovereign guarantee directly to the private SPV or a public sector loan to the municipality without sovereign guarantee which on-lends it to the private SPV or directly to the PTO.

OEMs can also be involved directly in the SPV or team up with SPVs for tenders as has been the case e.g. in Colombia or Chile. Also in Brazil, the PROMOB project has established a Working Group on Electric Buses and is currently exploring the possibility of establishing an electric bus fund to which it will co-finance many projects of the program e.g. as PPFs and public development banks can also be involved directly in SPVs for tenders or as co-financiers of projects (e.g. as PPFs) or as lenders of loans for projects). In some countries, e.g. Costa Rica or Colombia, national banks are already involved or have established lending facilities for EVs which are however not being used by clients due to lack of infrastructure and/or financing.

To ensure an active participation of national development banks in the Program and allow them to gain experience and de-risk the sector, the Program will propose technical assistance and financial assistance including a loan facility for the pilot line proposed by these banks is disturbed.


### Objectives, Impacts and Outcomes

The Program supports a paradigm change to low-emission transportation systems. The Program shall overcome the barriers identified to kick start the mass deployment of commercial EVs and increase ridership in public transport.

The following impacts shall be achieved:

- GHG emissions from urban buses will decrease by 50% compared with fossil units in all countries included in the Program.
- Public transport mode shift results in additional GHG reductions.
