# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 38

## IMPROVEMENTS FROM ELECTRIC VEHICLES (EVs)

- Improved air quality: EVs have no combustion emissions and are a crucial instrument to achieve clean air in cities.
- Reduced commercial vehicles as a major source of PM10, NO2, and NOx emissions in cities. Replacing diesel powered commercial vehicles with EVs improves air quality significantly.
- Public transport electrification is an important component of the program.

## REDUCED ENERGY DEPENDENCY

- EVs use domestic resources and reduce reliance on imported fossil fuels, which are generally subject to price volatility or external oil supply shocks.
- Increased energy efficiency: EVs are able to go more energy per unit of electricity. In addition, public transport is more energy efficient than private motorized modes. Electricity consumption even in the most energy intensive vehicles (e.g., buses) is lower compared with gasoline and diesel supported by the International Energy Agency (IEA) is marginal compared to national production levels.
- However, localized grid problems need to be addressed. Public transport mode shift results in additional benefits.

## EXPECTED GHG EMISSIONS REDUCTIONS

The expected GHI fund-level impacts are reduced GHG emissions through increased access to low-emission transportation resulting in direct emission reductions of 4,334,051 tCO2e over the lifetime of investment. The program is designed to support the transition from fossil fuel-based transport systems which are induced by the Program and the introduction of innovative business models in public transportation but are considered an indirect and non-quantiﬁed Program impact[9]. The potential impacts on GHG emissions are calculated based on the GHI fund-level impacts, which have been reported in 4 countries only whilst TA is delivered in all countries. The GHG reduction calculations are only based on the TA activities that are implemented by the program in each country and not on the TA activities of participating country and are selected based on high quality proposals put forward on a running basis to the AE. The available funds give an indication of investments in minimum 4 countries with 1 project each for technical assistance (TA) and investment grants (IG). These projects will be implemented in practice however, this might have variations and no country is included from proposing investment projects.

## TECHNICAL ASSISTANCE

To enable the envisaged system change, technical assistance is an essential element of the program, supporting and enabling the ﬁnancial assistance. The exact activities and themes addressed in each city are determined by the TA team based on a needs assessment conducted at the beginning of the project analysis already conducted during the preparation of the program, updated at the start of the program to include any new information.

All activities developing strategies, policies or concrete system design will be conducted using a multi-stakeholder approach supported by technical inputs generated within the program. Relevant stakeholders will be involved in the process through workshops, dialogues and other forms of interaction.

## PROGRAM COMPONENTS/OUTPUTS

The Program is structured in 3 components:

- **Component 1:** Establishment of a sustainable mobility ecosystem encouraging electrification and shift to public transport (TA)
- **Component 2:** Policy measures for PT mode shift (FA)
- **Component 3:** Deployment of EVs (FEA)

[9] Multiple other factors difficult to separate from the Program activity influence the impact on mode shares

Additionally Program Management is put as separate activity.

**Component 1:** Establishment of a sustainable mobility ecosystem encouraging electrification and a shift to public transport

The objective of this component is (1) Improved public & private sector capacity & regulatory environment for e-mobility.
- Component 1 is linked with Output (1.1): Establishment of a sustainable mobility ecosystem encouraging electrification and a shift to public transport.

The activities of component 1 are:
- Implement a local e-mobility conducive framework; (1.1.2)
- Implement a national e-mobility conducive framework; (1.1.3)
- Implement a regional e-mobility conducive framework; (1.1.4)

This component is TA only and realizes the TA activities for components 2 and 3 which are FA only.
- This component provides technical assistance to create a policy and business framework conducive for sustainable mobility and massive deployment of EVs. Includes a set of activities aimed at overcoming the barriers to the development of sustainable mobility in general and multiplication of electrification projects in particular.

Experience has shown that the introduction of new technologies can only follow a sequential path of applying T1 then FA, when the barriers that the market faces are only partially explained by financing availability and lack of awareness. The main barrier is the lack of enabling conditions (e.g., policies to create the required enabling conditions: development of normative and regulatory framework, development of technical standards, etc.). This work paves the way to the entry of the necessary resources that will allow the implementation of projects.

In the case of the market of EV for public transport, the main barriers that were identified are associated with financial aspects (high investment costs, problems of access to financing), risks associated with a high degree of innovation and technological uncertainty, lack of awareness among consumers, competition from other modes of transport, often high levels of subsidies and lack of an integrated attractive offer-of-attraction for clients, resulting in declining use of public transport. The introduction of new technologies is also hampered by the fact that the electrification has decreased the attractiveness of public transport. Therefore the required strategy must be comprehensive, contemplating simultaneously both the generation of the enabling conditions that this market requires and the development of a business model that will allow the implementation of projects in the short term.

Through the development of details feasibility studies in each of the proposed country, extensive information has been obtained to know and understand the electrification mobility market in the region. The starting situation is described for each country, the main barriers are identified and the challenges are described and made it possible to understand the main barriers that the electrification faces in the region (Section B 2 Theory of change). Risks and risks mitigation strategies have been developed.

**Activity 1.1.1:** Develop a local e-mobility conducive framework.

This includes required local policies and frameworks that are needed to support the development of public transport systems in general and sensitive material and catering to a diverse set of users. It further supports cities to optimize public transport systems, including route planning, depot design and location, and city planning, including the

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 41

Roadmaps are an important strategic tool to support the implementation of long-term goals and underpin these with intermediate targets and action areas. They can be developed at different levels (regional, national & city) and can address the overall sector or individual transport segments. For individuals, countries and cities, roadmaps can be part of a larger strategy such as the National Transport Policy or Programme (NTPP), that outlines the overall vision for the sector and empowers cities to implement sustainable urban mobility plans (SUMPs). Roadmaps are also an important element in the Joint National Mobility Partnership (JNMP), which outlines the specific vision for the sector and empowers cities to implement Sustainable Urban Mobility Plans (SUMPs). Some countries and cities also prefer to develop their own roadmaps, as they provide a clear direction of action. The development of roadmaps should provide clarity and predictability for investors. If they are underpinned with the actual implementation of policy and it can be demonstrated that envisaged goals are practically feasible. The program design aims to build capacity in countries and cities to develop roadmaps and to ensure that these address real, actual challenges faced on the ground while demonstrating technical and commercial feasibility in the context of the country or city’s specific circumstances.

The following links provide access to a range of tools and resources for developing national and local urban mobility plans (available at https://changing-transport.org/tools/ ) which will support the program activities. A range of countries and cities are currently in the process of developing NMPs, SUMPs and other related documents. For example, the English summary of the JNMP of Haiti can be found here: <https://www.greenclimate.fund/documents/2016/05/JNMP-Haiti-English_summary_final.pdf> The English summer 2013/Santo Domingo’s (2014) MJP is available at <https://www.greenclimate.fund/documents/2016/05/MJP-Santo-Domingo-summer_2013_English_summary_final.pdf>

### Activity 1.1.3 Establish a regional e-mobility conducive framework: Capacity building, knowledge sharing and experience exchange are conducted at the regional level. Activities include exchange and transfer of best practices between countries and cities. The program will also identify key institutions where available to disseminate information and experiences generated within the program.

Sub-Activities include:
* Dialogue with EV suppliers on the strengthening of regional equipment and support industries.
If at the moment, the manufacturers mainly focus on assembly of vehicles, this sub-activity will also act as a launching platform for increased transfer of know-how and footprint of local manufacturers in the region.
* Dissemination of knowledge materials developed in the city and national components;
* Sharing of training courses and curricula developed in other components;
* Gathering feedback activities.

Through its capacity-building activities, the program will be in a unique position to also identify potential candidates for further financial and technical support, which may be provided by consortium partners or external funders.

### Activity 1.1.4 Implement a Gender Action Plan: The technical assistance component will work to ensure that:
* The design and revision of roadmaps and policies includes a gender perspective and adequately considers the gender equality gaps and opportunities for enhancing the participation of women in mobility.
* The design of transport projects and investments considers gender equality;
* Gender disaggregated data on urban transport is collected to enhance the knowledge base;
* Prejudices against sexual harassment are promoted through communication campaigns.

## Component 2: Investive measures for PT mode shift

The outcome of this component is (2) Reduced GHG emissions from the transport sector.

Component 2 is linked with Output (2.1.1) mobility is combined with PT measures to foster mode shift to low-carbon public transport in 5 countries.

The activities of Component 2 are:
- (2.1.1) Implement urban mobility measures to foster PT in 2 countries.
Urban mobility measures are implemented by the city. They are measures to increase the attractiveness of public transport and reduce the attractiveness of private motorized transport, including NMT. These measures are applied in many countries. One of the reasons is the lack of attractiveness and convenience of PT. The program will work in the involved cities to increase PT attractiveness through measures such as bus only lanes thereby reducing congestion and improving travel time reliability; road pricing; improved public transport services; inter-modal integration; transport policies which favour in cities PT and de-incentive private means of transport by increasing parking costs, etc.; and other measures. The program will also work on the implementation of NMT measures such as bus route restructuring, exclusive bus lanes, integrated fare systems, or inter-modal regions to encourage modal shift and usage rates. Projects are also integrated with measures to increase the use of NMT including dedicated cycling lanes, pedestrian zones, etc.

## Component 3: Deployment of EV fleets

The outcome of this component is (2) Reduced GHG emissions from the transport sector.

Component 3 is linked to the Output (2.2.1) FEV fleets are deployed with innovative business models to enable their mass application.

The activities of Component 3 are:
- (2.2.1) Deployment of e-buses.
- (2.2.2) Deployment of charging infrastructure for e-buses.
- (2.2.3) Implement bus depot upgrades for e-buses.
- (2.2.4) Implement grid upgrades for connecting e-bus chargers.

This Component is FA only.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND VEHICLE PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 43

This component addresses the barrier of limited commercial attractiveness to invest in e-buses (batteries initial CAPEX barrier; performance barrier and risk barrier).

It is expected that this component will be applied in 4 countries with investment projects (Brazil, Colombia, Peru and Mexico) which have an indicative number of 850 e-buses deployed of different sizes. The goal is to identify, design and implement localized innovative business models in public transport which modernize and electrify the fleet. The project will also include a demonstration phase where the selected cities will showcase their technical and commercial viability to operators. Technology-wise this includes different e-bus configurations (e.g., single-deck buses, double-deck buses, articulated buses, trolleybuses), and different operational structures (mixed traffic buses as well as units operating on bus-only routes). The project will finance pilot projects that make a positive impact with innovative business models. Buses are operated by public transport operators or private companies in collaboration with identified business models.

E-bus systems always include 4 components: (i) the vehicle itself which accounts for around 70% of total CAPEX; (ii) charging infrastructure; (iii) grid upgrades to accommodate the charging infrastructure especially at bus depots but also if on-route charging or opportunity charging is used. In the areas of charging infrastructure, there are different options that can be considered including e.g., roof chargers over buses and increased space or layout changes to the bus depot as buses cannot be stored in a normal way with battery packs. The project will consider all these options during the feasibility study phase and the final feasibility study. This can be e.g., ultra fast charging at stations whilst passengers board, end of route opportunity charging, intermediate fast charging or overnight charging. This changes the number of charging points required as well as the size of the charging infrastructure needed (e.g., different layout changes as well as different grid impact and grid upgrades). The feasibility studies realized per city will consider all these aspects in detail including e.g., the charging infrastructure design, the vehicle design, battery types and battery sizes need to be optimized with the charging infrastructure and with financial and technical criteria concerning the grid to not create too much grid stress. There is no one-size-fits-all solution for this project as it depends on many factors such as bus size and characteristic, conditions, bus size and characteristic, grid conditions, climatic conditions etc. Batteries will need to be charged at different times of day and in different locations depending on the charging infrastructure used (in some cases and some battery types even longer). However, this investment will not be that critical in 8 years from now due to falling battery prices and is in the range of a complete engine overhaul and bus replacement.

## Program Management

### Activity 1.1.5: Project sourcing, structuring and monitoring.
This includes technical, financial and institutional due diligence and the full feasibility of the project including economic, environmental, social, gender and legal. This includes the definition of a project contractual scheme, ensuring a proper risk allocation scenario and the identification of any potential co-financing opportunities for a project that is eligible to receive GCF concessional resources. This support is key as innovative business models are often needed to bridge the cost gap between EVs and internal combustion engines (ICEs). Project preparation can also support the development of an investment pipeline, including the identification of other projects in early stages. Developments projects sourced and designed under this activity will be financed within components 2 and 3 if they meet the eligibility criteria for these components as part of this activity.

This activity will contribute to a proper structuring of the Program, an adequate risk allocation and bankability and to the deployment of innovative business models.

This activity will be implemented by AFD only.


## Project Selection Criteria

All investments must be compliant with the GCF investment framework and follow the principles, terms and conditions agreed by AFD group and the co-financers, including project eligibility criteria, governance, implementation arrangements, environmental and social management systems, gender consideration, monitoring and evaluation. The selection of projects will be based on their ability to deliver high-quality projects maximizing their GHG mitigation benefits and contributes to the bankability of projects by introducing climate change considerations into all projects. This applies to all projects a standard GHI accounting methodology to determine GHG benefits going beyond the baseline.

## Project Eligibility Criteria

Eligible projects must fulfill the following minimum criteria:

1. All investment projects proposed under Components 2 or 3 must reduce GHG emissions relative to the business-as-usual scenario and provide positive development impact. The approach to be used is determined in Annex 23 and is based primarily on UNFCCC methodologies registered under the Clean Development Mechanism (CDM);
2. All investments projects proposed under Components 2 must be linked to transport demand policies ensuring shift to public transport;
3. All investment projects financed under Components 2 or 3 must demonstrate a transformational potential and increase ridership;

### Not more than 35% of QCF funds shall be invested in one specific country. Investment projects will be determined by the availability of PT and/or NMT services, depending on their level of maturity at the time of availability of QCF proceeds.

### EVs are purchased instead of new conventional vehicles. The vehicle is not the asset of its lessee and not required to be returned upon termination of Project (P) lease (i.e., the Project (P) has no option to purchase a fossil or an electric bus). In both cases (baseline fossil as well as project case electric) a new unit enters and the old unit is also the same in both cases. There is thus no increased number of old units, but there are fewer new units entering the fleet. The baseline scenario assumes that there will be no scrapping, in additional emission due to the purchase of NEW vehicles but only accounts for emissions associated with the production of fossil fuel and battery electric vehicles. The difference between the Program is that instead of a new fossil vehicle a new electric vehicle is purchased. What happens with the old vehicle is the same as the project case or in the baseline case. The difference is not the old vehicle but the NEW vehicle which is electric.

### Calculations are also a comparison of a new fossil against a new electric unit. The Program does not promote earlier replacement or scrapping of vehicles. Scrapping Programs in any case have limited merits

## Finance Instruments

The Program uses loans and grants. Grants are only used for technical assistance in component I channeled mainly through GIZ, the implementing agency for TA (34 ME grants of which 21 ME from the GCF) and for project sourcing and development managed by AFD (8.5 ME grant of which 1.1 ME from the GCF). The investment components 2 and 3 are purely loan financed.

### B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

GIZ has a strong technical expertise in the development of large TA programs and the supervision and implementation of projects in the public sector. GIZ is also active in the field of electric mobility in Latin America (NATUR project) and Uruguay (Eurolima - program). Moreover, GIZ is particularly active in the promotion of e-mobility in the public transport sector in Brazil (FELICITY program with EIB), Colombia (EcoBus program), Peru (REFROTA), integration of bus systems and electrification in Florianópolis (FELICITY program with EIB) and technical assistance for the development of a national electric mobility strategy in Mexico (EcoBus program). GIZ also develops two bankable projects for new electric buses on 2 routes in Quito and providing assistance for new electric bus corridor and container lines in Mexico City. The FELICITY program is at an advanced stage of implementation, with the first vehicles currently being implemented across Latin America, with a common ambitious financing and technical assistance proposal specifically targeted towards the commercial EV segments, which are now able to reach the scale and become commercially viable in the short to medium term.

### Implementation Arrangements

E-Motion will be implemented jointly by the following institutions:

- AFD: as accredited entity, executing entity and co-financer for the Financial Assistance targeting public sector project owners (Components 2 and 3), for the technical assistance dedicated to Projects preparation, and for the gender action plan activities related to the investments projects.
- PROGEO: as implementing entity and co-financer for the Financial Assistance targeting private sector project owners;
- GIZ: as executing entity for technical assistance activities.

AFD project teams will be responsible for the identification, approval, implementation and evaluation of eligible projects. The project approval process will follow AFD's procedures. In particular, task team leaders will carry out due diligence and appraisals on each project in accordance with AFD Group standards and procedures.

## CONTRACTUAL ARRANGEMENTS

The GCF and AFD will sign a Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) and all GCF funds, whether reimbursable or non-reimbursable funds, will be transferred from the GCF to AFD according to the provisions set in the FAA. The GCF loan proceeds will be provided to AFD in the form of a sub-participation, and the GCF grant proceeds will be provided as a grant.

AFD will sign a grant sub-licensing agreement with GCF and will channel GCF Technical Assistance funds to GCF for the purpose the implementation of the majority of the activities 1.1, 1.2 and 1.3 and 1.4 (i) to (iv) of component 1.

AFD will sign a subsidiary agreement with PROPARCO which will define the fiduciary and implementation arrangements between AFD and PROPARCO and PROPARCO will have a mandate to act on behalf of AFD, with AFD being the lender. A grant agreement may be signed to delegate part of TA project preparation funds to PROPARCO.

## FINANCING AGREEMENT BETWEEN AFD OR PROPARCO AND THE BENEFICIARIES

Eligible projects within AFDScope (sovereign and non-sovereign public sector) and PRSP/PRCScope (socioeconomic private sector) will benefit from financial support extended by AFD and PROPARCO through the Financial Assistance on a project-by-project basis. For this purpose, PROPARCO and AFD will sign financing agreements with the beneficiaries (public sector entities, private sector companies, local authorities, ministries, etc.). Financing agreements will describe the provisions and modalities for disbursing and using the funds allocated to implementing the projects, and will be developed and negotiated according to AF Group templates and procedures.

A detailed financial assessment will be realized by AFD and PROPARCO in the framework of the appraisal process of the project to ensure that the borrowers answer to the required criteria to sign a Credit Facility Agreement with AFD and PROPARCO (price risk, revenues profile).

AFD will hire consulting companies to implement TA dedicated to the preparation of the projects and is subject to the terms and conditions set out in the Project Preparation Facility of E-Motion. As a result for tenders will be launched to hire a pool of consulting firms that shall be mobilized to meet the specific needs

## Rationale for and Additionality of GCF Funding

The Program introduces a concessional financing mechanism that addresses barriers to the uptake of investments in electric vehicles (EVs) and supports a transformative shift to low carbon transportation. Currently fossil fuels dominate the market and fuel reinvestment. In all involved countries no large fleets of commercial EVs operate. The paradigm shift towards electric mobility not only entails a change in the vehicle fleet but also requires changes in the business model. Actors in the commercial vehicle sector are keen on promoting e-mobility as this can reduce emissions and dependence on (often imported) fossil fuels whilst also creating long term sustainable jobs. They therefore often announce political targets or pledges to promote electric mobility, but rarely have a clear strategy for how they will achieve these goals. The Program aims to start EV massification with a first batch of scale implementation projects. Such initiatives are dependent on concessional financing and technical assistance in order to be successful. This is because they lay the foundation for meaningful, practical and action-oriented roadmaps and for developing policies which can be implemented at national level.

Stand alone pilots or technical assistance have proven to be insufficient to kick-start mass deployment of EVs. GCF concessional financing is critical to ensure this comprehensive e-mobility approach – in absence of such funding the projects will not be viable.

The major financial barriers for the potential beneficiaries which prevent alternative financing options are (i) the low financial profitability of EV investments reflected in a low FIRR and long payback times; (ii) the high risk associated with EV investments, including the need to invest in infrastructure development and replacement investments (batteries); and (iii) high capital and investor's capital demand resulting in high debt and risk exposure levels of the investing entity. Commercial funding is thus not willing to enter


---
**Page 56**
---

This market segment involving vehicles with a higher risk appetite are potentially interested but are critical of the lack of profitability and/or the high risk profile. A good example for this case is Chile where traditional banks were reluctant to finance commercial EVs and private electric utilities entered the market with a limited number of buses. However, these entities were only willing to kick-start the process when the government recognized and supported the need for such vehicles. The same applies in other countries like India, Brazil, etc., i.e., without concessional finance and risk mitigation instruments the deal would not have taken place.

The main barriers why investors, cities or PTOs do not want to purchase e-buses is the lack of profitability and/or high risk profile. This is due to: (i) higher upfront costs for electric vehicles; (ii) lower revenue and will increase the total cost. Potential investors as well as PIs are only interested in guarantees if these incur no cost. They also mention that the guarantee will change the decision to purchase or not EVs. For concession contracts, it is important to note that the higher the risk profile of the project, the higher the guarantee levels (100%) would be required in slight lower interest rates. For some concession contracts in which the government has a high level of control over the project and/or the technology, a credit guarantee instrument would be relevant. High concessional finance is critical for deployment of initial commercial EV fleets. Concessional finance is not required in replication projects which happen at a later stage when the market is more mature.

Risks which are reduced by large fleet applications are: (i) vehicle reliability and vehicle performance risks; (ii) improved information and therefore lower risk on assessing the re-sale value of used EVs important for investment decisions. Large fleets also reduce the risk associated with the availability of spare parts including secondary space parts; markets; (iii) reduced risk on the workability of business models and by enabling with concessional finance the uptake of large fleets which are critical to reducing costs, improving reliability and performance, and increasing customer satisfaction. The cost of fleet is an objective set by the country. This allows EVs to be more competitive. Lower costs, reduced risks, and improved financing conditions all create favourable conditions for investors to pour capital into this market segment. Concessional finance is not only needed to compensate the higher upfront costs of electric vehicles but also to cover other associated costs such as: (i) higher interest rates; (ii) higher risk premium beyond just higher upfront costs; associated with this, there are higher capital requirement, higher equity requirement and higher interest burden and guarantees (also due to uncertain resale value) usually literally speaking, the cost of fleet is a sunk cost. The higher the risk profile of the project, the higher the cost needs to be considered as a sunk cost. Finally, there is a higher risk in comparison: TCO calculations are more difficult than for conventional vehicles and the impact of fuel price volatility on the development of diesel prices, maintenance costs and operational readiness of e-vehicles. Lifetime of battery is also an important factor which can affect the TCO.

Conventional financial institutions are also not willing to enter the market due to the novelty of the technology. A pilot project will not dispel their concerns as these are associated basically to the lifespan of the technology, i.e., the life cycle of the vehicle and the willingness of the original equipment manufacturers (basically Chinese bus manufacturers which are not all well known in the market), resale value of vehicles, and potential savings in terms of maintenance and repair costs. Financial institutions perceive


---
**Page 57**
---

These risks are also thus reluctant to finance EVs. The establishment of new technologies and new business models also entails significant additional transaction costs. Concessional GCF financing including grants can provide for technical assistance to design and structure e-mobility investments with a reduced profit margin, thereby making them more attractive from the perspective of investors and increasing profitability of the investment. Increasing the role of public transport strengtheners operators and transport policies will reduce the risks associated with investments.

The most country approach is based on a transformational and accelerated shift towards e-mobility which are also required to achieve the target of limiting the global temperature increase to below 2 degrees Celsius. Many countries have already started this process by implementing national programs in collaboration with other countries, such as the realization of different knowledge materials including publications, webinars, benchmark and best practice studies, preparation of capacity building guidelines and outreach events. The GCF is a key player in supporting these efforts through its Global Program for Electric Vehicles (GPEV) which is realized within the program. Through participation of various countries with similar context the learning experience can be shared and lessons learned can be applied to other countries. The implementation of other critical is if the program is realized only in one country, the replication will be basically on a national level. National programs are always important but experiences from other countries can be brought into the national program through capacity building activities and knowledge sharing events.

It is also necessary given the scale of climate change challenges facing countries and the critical need to decarbonize transport sectors that GCF support should be provided in a way that ensures maximum impact, resulting in long lock-in times of investments. The GCF involvement is critical to support low carbon transport solutions. However, the GCF cannot finance all projects. A project selection approach is needed, resources can be used efficiently to highest priority investment projects – this cannot be achieved with a piecemeal, project-by-project approach. Individual projects are in most cases too small to warrant GCF involvement and should be financed by other sources. The GCF will focus on financing electric mobility in most cases and not integrate it into its transport investments. The Program will also aim at supporting the development of public transport systems and vehicle sectors and can effectively ensure transfer of knowledge and capacity building between stakeholders.

Concessionality of GCF

GCF concessional financing including grants are used for PTs, and for PAs. GCF assistance enables linking financial resources with technical assistance. Investments alone is not sufficient to achieve the transformation in climate action needed by the transport sector. Strategic sectoral planning and policy reform are critical aspects to enable the transformation towards electric mobility. The GCF, with the goal of supporting countries in their transition to low carbon transport solutions, has established a concessional assistance component of the Program. The Program provides private transport operators, national PTs, and public authorities with technical assistance for project preparation and implementation by involving in projects national PTs and by having PPF as the major delivery agent for projects.

PTOs are in most involved countries private companies. Fits are as included in the national fund part. Also GCF support to PAs is delivered through a combination of grants and loans, where private companies are involved. In some countries however public sector led PTs may be more appropriate depending on circumstances.

FA concessional financing are critical to reduce upfront CAPEX to make investments commercially viable and compensate for risks from initiating climate investments in a new technology and in new business models. GCF’s ability to offer concessional terms in investments enables prospective clients to invest in e-mobility projects with reduced profit margins, thereby increasing the profitability of the investment. (Figure 2) clearly shows that all countries with large fleets of commercial EVs (>100 units) have used at least a portion of GCF funding for their electric vehicle fleet. The level of support varies widely depending on the investment costs of e-buses was grant financed by countries by 80-100%. The magnitude of subsidies can be reduced with adequate policies and regulations (e.g. concession periods) and business models per


---
**Page 58**
---

vehicle scale. This experience of countries is also that once a large fleet is established subsidies can be reduced gradually and even be eliminated within 5-10 years due to more competitive prices of EVs combined with the experience of mass operations. This reduces risks for investors in EVs primarily performance risk, operational risk, and financial risk. The GCF has already provided support to countries to address some risks related basically to the availability of a fast charging infrastructure. GCF concessional financing is currently being used by several countries to finance the development of this critical infrastructure. However, the missing part of initial large scale fleets combined with business models, policy advice and technical designs which reduce risks needs to go into EV investments and allow to close the gap between political aspirations and market realities.

Long tenures and low interest rates are critical for the affordability of such loans as the fiscal situation of countries and of operators has lightened in LAC drastically with COVID-19. Longer tenure and low interest rate concessional financing is also critical because it allows for a more gradual transition, avoiding significant additional fiscal imbalance. Also, the public transport sector has been hit by COVID 19 with reduced demand and increased costs. The result of this will be that in many countries the public transport system will decline in importance due to bankruptcy of multiple private operators. Concessional loans with long tenure and low interest rate increase the fiscal space of governments to provide subsidies to the public sector, which is critical for maintaining sustainability of the services and thus of the program. They are thus absolutely critical for Program success.

Grants are used for technical assistance such as new policies or business models, battery regulations, etc., which are essential in order to make sure that the transition towards electric vehicles will work. TA is considered as absolutely essential for the paradigm shift. Only with changed policies, business models and with trained and informed people will this happen. Without this effort one-time projects and interventions will not be sufficient.

The Program will benefit from technical assistance that will be made available to accompany each project and will ultimately serve to enhance the viability of the projects. The TA package has been developed in close collaboration with the countries, taking into account their specific needs and assessment of other actors in this area in the same country and other regional readiness programs to avoid duplication. The TA package is designed to provide a comprehensive approach for the transition and shift on a local, national and regional level with activities structured from the local to the regional level.

The Program will provide direct technical assistance to projects that will potentially receive financial assistance under the Program. This includes capacity building and training of project staff in technical advisory as well as advisory on supportive transport demand policies as well as possible business models and technical designs. The TA package also includes charging networks and the interface between vehicle, charging, and the grid. The TA includes also:

- Capacity building, training and monitoring: TA is used to ensure delivery of adequate training from equipment suppliers or third parties for FTEs and charger operators and maintenance, safety training, etc.
- Technical advisory: TA will provide technical advice on the design of projects, performance monitoring of EVs in technical (energy usage, emissions) and financial terms (operations and maintenance costs, charging infrastructure costs), as well as operational and commercial performance risk of EVs and to create a solid base of actual costs and benefits of EVs in the country.
- Support of enabling public policies for shift to public transport and EV deployment: Technical support will be provided to the relevant national and local authorities in the countries and cities where the Program is active. This includes technical advice on the design of projects, development of public policies and legal, regulatory, fiscal and/or normative frameworks for e-mobility. The Program will also provide technical assistance to the relevant authorities in the design and implementation of public transport and roadmaps for taxis and urban freight. Sectoral and specific roadmaps are a tool to steer climate investments and initiatives beyond the Program's scope and will help to inform


---
**Page 59**
---

and/or detail the countries long term mobility strategies and their NDCs. Policy advice is also performed for battery handling, re-use, recycling and disposal. TA in policy also includes advice on concession contract issues, electricity pricing (e.g. demand charge for public charging networks).

* Development and advisory services on optimal business models and financial structuring which help to unlock private sector investment in the transport sector. This can be done through e.g. for atomized urban transport providers, models which allow for increased influx of private capital and which lower financing costs and risks for operators, leasing models, inclusion of electric utilities as investors.

* Inclusion of gender aspects in all projects to be financed as well as on a national and regional level.
* Knowledge sharing and capacity building. This can be done through the exchange of best practices and cases through channels such as webinars, electronic reports, case-studies, and workshops to disseminate experiences and build a base for replication thereby accelerating EV uptake on a regional scale. The knowledge sharing should not only focus on technical aspects of EV information but use existing channels such as the one created by UN-ECE MOVE. An interchange of experience shall also be encouraged where best practices can be shared from countries more advanced in specific issues, e.g. Colombia on bus systems.

B.6 Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Exit Strategy

Repayments under concessional loans provided with GCF funds under the Program will be financed through the GCF Special Account in accordance with repayment schedule set forth in T/A or Proponent’s loan agreement. It is envisaged that tenors will not exceed 15 years. All loans will be monitored by AFD group. GCF resources will be reflowed back to the GCF in accordance with the terms of the FAA.

Program Sustainability

At the time of this Project (based on an implementation period* of 7 years which would be 2030), there are large operational fleets of e-buses in all Program countries. This forms the base for a large scale commercial replication without grants. The projections of dropping EV-bus prices put cost to baseline battery (TOC) below that of fossil fuel buses by 2025. A recent survey conducted by EY per country shows that a significant share of investors would then be willing to purchase electric buses without subsidies. This is in line with the findings from the GCF’s own internal analysis. The main arguments core assumptions concerning energy usage, maintenance cost, vehicle reliability, and system technology choice (types of charging, charging method and site, battery lifetime) and battery size on buses.

While the TOC for an electric bus is significantly lower than that of a fossil fuel bus, it is currently lower OPEX (basically energy plus maintenance cost) make the TOCs significantly lower than for fossil bus systems (in many countries the TOCs are already today lower but not sufficient: i.e. the payback period is still long and sometimes even longer than the depreciation period of PT operators thus not making the investment financially attractive under a risk viewpoint.


* Implementation period refers to the period of development of the TA and contractualization and disbursement of the credit lines.

It is expected that by 2030 e-buses will be close to sticker price parity with diesel units, whilst total system costs per e-bus are still expected to be around 40-50% higher than a fossil unit. However, the savings in fuel and maintenance costs can be used to offset these higher upfront investment costs for purchases of an e-bus at the standard, without necessity of further concessional finance. TCO costs will be much lower than diesel buses and payback periods of incremental bus investments under this investment price trend would be significantly shorter compared with diesel buses. The cost savings from reduced fuel and maintenance costs are realized as soon as the first e-buses are reduced. This is possible through the establishment of new business models and through operational efficiencies that can be achieved by using electric vehicles. These savings can be used to purchase more e-buses, thereby reducing the overall fleet size. Countries which originally subsidized 80-100% of the entire e-bus system incremental cost can reduce their subsidies significantly within few years. The importance of significant upfront investment subsidies is reduced as soon as a large fleet is established and the costs are shared among many operators. As more e-buses are introduced, a large fleet is established subsidies can be reduced gradually and even be eliminated with 5-10 years due to more competitive prices of e-buses combined with the experience mass operations of e-buses.

Example countries:

* In PR China the average subsidy level per 12m urban e-bus decreased within 5 years from 100% in 2013 to less than 40% in 2018 (including charging infrastructure subsidies).
* In India under FAME II(2017-2019) is buses where subsidized with 60% of incremental costs. This was reduced to 50% within two years and further reduced to 30% by the end of 2018 (charging infrastructure subsidies were reduced by more than 50% within 2 years).
* In the Philippines, in 2017, the government subsidized 90% of the incremental cost of e-buses under the first scheme and 2nd scheme since 2018 75% of the incremental cost of e-buses (including charging infrastructure) was subsidized.
* Chile subsidized for the first lot of 20 electric buses 100% of incremental costs. The next lots received no more investment gap subsidies but some additional benefits for buses relative to fossil fuel buses, such as lower tolls and priority lanes.

Source: Greuter Consulting contract with ADB for monitoring of e-bus fleets in 18 cities with 60,000 buses financed by leasing funds with support by ADB

## Establishment of a conducive EV framework, local business models which match the EV situation (e.g., separation of ownership and usage of assets) as well as reduced risks of operating EVs due to the economic benefits of large-scale deployment of buses in cities with existing roadmaps.

### The establishment of a conducive EV framework, local business models which match the EV situation (e.g., separation of ownership and usage of assets) as well as reduced risks of operating EVs due to the economic benefits of large-scale deployment of buses in cities with existing roadmaps.

Based on the market studies performed the Program expects that a commercial uptake of EVs without financial support will be achieved. The Program has identified several factors which are conducive to this, including: (i) the intervention of the Program accelerating in a decisive manner the commercial uptake of EVs and fast track the deployment of buses; (ii) the creation of an enabling environment for the development of business models where the Program intervenes with different policy actions including roadmaps; (iii) attractive business models which are implemented with the projects and which provide investors with confidence of the attractiveness of the business model. The Program has also identified several factors which are conducive to the deployment of buses in cities with existing roadmaps, such as: (i) the combination of factors which create better working conditions, clarity on investment models combined with a strong policy environment; (ii) the creation of an enabling environment for the development of business models where the Program intervenes with different policy actions including roadmaps.

It is expected that commercial investors will crowd in to the e-bus area once the framework conditions (e.g., guarantees, concession contracts) and the performance risks of buses are managed. This has been the case in other countries such as China, India, and South Korea where it was possible for investors to invest 1 billion in e-buses in Latin America). The Programs TA creates conditions which are favourable to actually materialize such investments and the investment component of the Program reduces the risks to investors by providing guarantees and concession contracts. In addition, the Program also provides technical assistance to help investors to understand the business models that large-scale fleet deployment with such business models take place as proof of concept and to reduce performance risks for operators and financiers. Both TA as well as concessional finance for FA are however not sufficient in themselves to make these profitable investments happen. The key is to ensure that costs are reduced as low costs of EVs make these profitable already in the short to medium term under the most challenging conditions.

The technical assistance supports long-term sustainability through a number of crucial factors:

* Roadmaps at the national or city level are developed and are underpinned with actual policies that strengthen the EV ecosystem and make public transport more attractive. These frameworks will support sustainable deployment of buses in cities, which is key to making the business case for e-buses.
* The demonstration effect of large-scale deployment of e-buses in cities with selected roadmaps will enhance the attractiveness of buses, reduce uncertainty for investors. Coupled with the demonstration effect of large-scale deployment of e-buses in cities with selected roadmaps, the business models in the selected four cities, this will enhance the commercial viability of future investments.
* Countries within the program have very different starting points. Those with already existing roadmaps will serve as role models implementing all components at both national and local level. Others will focus on developing roadmaps and other enabling conditions for the deployment of e-buses in cities where components 2 and 3 are implemented. For countries without actual implementation components, the policy

Experiences from on-the-ground implementation in other countries can help shape better policies and enable a more ambitious strategy.

Enhanced understanding of business models in the specific context of Latin America, including for second-life use of batteries combined with a proof-of-concept on the ground will reduce the risk of investments and motivate additional action.

Capacity-building activities ensure that there is a broader understanding of technology options and their suitability to the region. This includes the development of a technical assistance program. ### Capacity Building
Capacity Building is delivered under Component 1. This includes specifically:
- Project specific training during design and planning stage specifically the transfer of skills for optimal vehicle dispatch, planning and design as well as technology choice of EVs.
- Project specific training during design and planning stage specifically the transfer of skills for tender documents, design of relevant business models, financing plans, risk sharing mechanisms.
- Project specific training and capacity building by implemented project on technology related issues such as optimal vehicle dispatch, planning and design as well as technology choice of EVs.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 60

## safety and risk avoidance with EVs, battery management and disposal policies and regulations etc.

- Capacity building and policy advice specifically for the establishment of sub-sectoral road-based electric mobility in the field of public transportation, taxis and LDVs.
- Capacity building with knowledge products and their dissemination based on the best global practices and lessons learned from successful projects implemented through different products and channels to reach a wider audience in Latin America.
- Capacity building with policy advice.

## Training and CB of service providers including maintenance and repair staff for buses as well as charging infrastructure to minimize the risk of low vehicle availability rates.

The purchase contracts with equipment providers (buses, chargers) will include specific training and CB courses which are provided by the supplier. The training is targeted at bus drivers, maintenance staff, service staff and transport operators. The procurement contracts will also include a minimum commitment from the supplier to provide a dedicated service provider for buses and chargers during minimum 2 years in collaboration with the staff of the equipment owner/ user operator.

## Technology Transfer

Technology transfer facilitated through identification and design of appropriate e-mobility technologies, including and their subsequent funding. Technology transfer agreements between bus and charging equipment providers are tied with purchasers into 2-3 year service provision contracts where the supplier must provide for on-ground expert staff to train local maintenance and repair staff. All technology transfer agreements include a minimum commitment on spare parts availability which ensure smooth operations and high vehicle availability rates. The supplier is also responsible for training of local maintenance and repair staff in charge and ensures this through training programs which are also specified in the bidding documents for suppliers.

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.

**D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)**

* Area of emission reductions are (i) impact of replacing fossil with electric vehicles and (ii) increased ridership in public transport.
* **Impact of E-Buses**
    * GHG emission calculations have been made for potential investment projects for e-buses per country. While baseline emissions per vehicle category are comparable for different countries (variations are due to vehicle mileage, fuel type used and to a lesser extent due to different specific fuel consumption values) the actual impact on emissions will vary significantly.
    * The GHG impact is determined based on a well-to-wheel approach. This includes for EVs the carbon emission from the grid including production, as well as transmission and distribution losses. With the assumption that the electricity mix in Brazil, Colombia, Peru and Uruguay is 50% renewable energy, this would reduce GHG emissions by 70-90%. The methodology report on GHG reductions of the Program also includes a life-cycle or cradle-to-grave approach to determine emission reductions to assess the potential impact of e-buses. This analysis shows that the use of electric buses (compared with diesel buses only) changes the picture marginally.
    * For the grid factor the IFI database last version 3.1 of July 2021 was used, which assumes a lifetime of measures is 15 years in accordance with e-bus Hespas with 1x battery replacement.
* **Impact of Public Transport Measures**
    * This includes GHG reductions from ongoing investments in public transport measures to increase its performance. The methodology for calculation GHG reduction is based on the UNFCCC methodology ACM026 and the UNFCCC methodological tool “Baseline emissions for modal shift measures in urban passenger transport”. GHG emission calculations have been made for potential investment projects of e-buses which would have used other modes of transport with on average higher emission factors. Estimation of the incremental patronage of PT systems is thereby based on monitored results of already implemented projects. The estimated impact of the Program is a modal shift from private cars to public transport, modern electric bus fleets and increased ridership in urban passenger transport. This would result in an increase in passenger-kilometre factor 2x higher than for PT. The assumed lifetime of measures is 25 years in accordance with infrastructure lifespan.
    * The total projected GHG direct impact of the Program is estimated at a 434,051 tCO2e over the lifetime of the project (i.e., until the end of the useful life of projects). This includes only the impact of the 4 countries. Actual Project Programme emission reductions will depend on which projects are actually implemented and will be more significant.
* The important aspect of the Program is however that it creates the conditions for an accelerated uptake of EVs. This has been modelled based on an investor-risk model which per country and vehicle sector considers factors such as cost, availability, range, charging infrastructure (which increases over time due to decreasing relative EV prices), the investment risk level and the target WACC.

The same applies to Germany where demand created by EVs, The problems which grids must face are of limited localized power supply, limited availability of charging infrastructure and the need for a new business model. ### D.2 Paradigm shift (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The paradigm shift of this proposed Program is achieved by having as long-term outcome a EV conducive ecosystem which results in investors purchasing EVs on a commercial basis and a modal share of sustainable transport increased. Barriers towards mass EV deployment have been successfully eliminated or reduced to an acceptable level through the implementation of the Program. The main reasons for take place due to experiences made, business models established, reduced entry barriers, lower risks and cost reduction. The success of this program is also achieved by having a well-functioning market system is achieved by increasing services quality and working with partners on policies for incentives to EVs. The combination of the above factors together with the willingness of governments to support and the mobility transition, together lead to a paradigm change towards sustainable mobility. The transformative shift is possible through the uptake of commercial EVs and the improved ecosystem for EVs


---
**Page 69**
---

result of concrete interventions as well as of policy assistance and capacity building. The transportation sector is transformed to a sustainable low-carbon system with a dominance of public transport and electric vehicles (EVs). This transformation is accelerated by the opportunity presented by the COVID-19 crisis as a vehicle to transform the sector by investing in profitable infrastructure which creates economic, environmental and social benefits, short-term jobs and a long-term greening of the economy. Electric mobility is seen as an important part of this transformation. Many countries are now interested in shifting to e-mobility. This is expressed in updated NDCs as well as in activities on going in all continents. The GCF has been supporting several projects in this area, and is currently looking for appropriate business models especially for e-buses and experience with initial large fleets, especially also in Latin America. In the context of the GCF, it is important to note that the GCF can help to remove these barriers and can showcase successful and commercially attractive implementation of large fleets of EVs. The GCF has a specific focus on electric mobility as part of its strategy to support mass scale and move from a fossil-based transportation system to an electric one. The willingness of the public sector to invest in this technology is key, but so are other factors such as the ability to manage risks and an acceptable profit rate – what can bring these components to spur e-mobility deployment is the design and implementation of adequate policies, framework conditions, business models and financing schemes.

In absence of a program E-mobility will also be the norm in Latin America as in the rest of the world - just at a later stage. The key issue is about accelerating mass transformation at a faster pace and on an earlier stage than what would naturally happen without any support. The question is not so much if (it will happen but) if we can accelerate with GCF investments WHEN and to do so in a way that makes the transition as smooth as possible. A good example for Colombia: the Program allowing the projected deployment of EVs. The same model applies for all other countries for e-buses, LCVs and taxis. The results including the model assumptions and modeling approach are available on the GCF website (see Annex 3: Country Intervention Strategies) and the respective Excel files per country included under Annex 3.

The initial pipeline developed in 2021/2022 and updated thereafter showed that the majority of investment plans for electric buses were concentrated in Colombia, with a focus on Bogotá. The main reasons are concessional finance and the prevailing risks as core reason. The program country which has progressed strongly in e-buses in Colombia but focusing to an overwhelming extent on Bogotá plus to a much smaller extent Medellín. This is not sustainable from a national perspective, and it is not aligned with the GCF strategy.

The need for the Program is thus obvious and the delay in approving this Program is one of the reasons why the GCF has not been able to support Colombia in implementing its plans in reaching clearly needed initial investments in this technology to reduce with speed GHG emissions

The potential for scaling up and replication is ensured at the national and the international level through capacity created in the e-mobility ecosystem. Proven cases of successful business models, improved project preparation and implementation, and a strong policy environment are key to this.

In several countries that show once fleets of EVs are operational and not just pilot projects, the technology is taken up by other operators. This has been demonstrated in Chile where the government has introduced a trigger programs demonstrating the commercial viability of EVs. Performance risks are reduced greatly and business models have been introduced which make the uptake of EVs on a purely commercial base viable. The same can be expected to happen in Colombia, where the first fleet is operational now and in the medium term. Scaling up and replication is also enabled through TA at policy level including the development of enabling policies, regulatory frameworks, and appropriate policy incentives and knowledge management instruments as well as capacity building.

It is expected that commercial investors will crowd in to the e-bus area once the framework conditions (e.g. public procurement rules, concession contracts) are established. This has been demonstrated successfully in Chile where the case in Santiago de Chile as well as Bogota. A coalition of investors announced end 2021 the intention to invest $1 billion in e-buses in Latin America®. The Programs TA creates conditions which are favourable to this and provides a strong basis for scaling up and replication.

The Program is critical to unlock the private investment potential. For commercial operators, the program offers attractive business models through the introduction of concession contracts adn bus models as well as clear government policies. Just as important is that large-scale fleet deployment with such business models take place as proof of concept and to reduce performance risks for investors.

The Program also provides a strong basis for scaling up and replication in Colombia, however not required anymore in the future once the framework conditions are established and once the risk profile has been reduced. The program will be important in the short to medium term under the mentioned conditions of an adequate e-mobility environment.

suppliers and parties for EV and charger operations and maintenance, safety trainings and capacity building in optimal management of FEV fleets. ## Contribution to the creation of an enabling framework

The TA Component includes the support of national authorities in setting up enabling frameworks. This support will be provided to the relevant national and local authorities in order to set, up, improve and/or enforce enabling public policies and legal, regulatory, fiscal and financial measures. The TA Component will also provide technical assistance for the development of roadmaps for electrification of public transport, roadmaps for electrification of taxis, and roadmaps for electrification of LCVs. Whilst various countries have general EV roadmaps or targets these remain at a relatively high level of ambition and are not yet translated into concrete actions. The TA Component will support the development of more detailed roadmaps that include specific time-bound targets, intervention instruments and actions allowing to take steps towards actual implementation.

The TA Component will also provide technical assistance for the development of national EV Roadmap Program's scope and will help to inform and/or further detail the countries long term mobility strategies and the role of electrification in these strategies.

## Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

The TA Component includes activities such as roadmaps (see above) which outline an agreed upon set of pragmatic and realistic measures to achieve sustainable mobility targets. Regulatory activities realized by the Program will tackle transport demand policies, vehicle fleet policies, urban planning policies, public transport policies, pricing policies for infrastructure standards, pricing policies for EV charging, public transport regulatory aspects including accessibility, safety and security, and other relevant policies such as those related to parking, vehicle ownership and vehicle operators, tariff structure etc. Technical assistance in this area is linked up with the development of roadmaps and will be provided through a multi-stakeholder approach involving all stakeholders and have clear short and medium term targets, means to achieve these targets to be realistic and implementable and to go beyond a vision statement. An example of a recent and clear roadmap for electrification in public transport is the recently published Electrification Roadmap for the state of Colorado, USA which outlines the pathway for full electrification of buses. An interesting roadmap for the Netherlands has been developed by the Dutch government.

## Overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways consistent with relevant national climate change adaptation strategies and plans

Transport contributes almost one-quarter of the current global greenhouse gas emissions from all sectors and is the only energy end-use sector. Limiting the global temperature increase to below 2 degrees Celsius requires a significant reduction in transport emissions trajectory. The Paris Declaration on Electric-Mobility and Climate Change & Call to Action, calls for an accelerated transition towards electric mobility. Many countries have already set ambitious targets but have otherwise expressed their keen interest in embarking on this transformational shift towards e-mobility. Without such a shift, many of the transport sectors in most countries will not be able to achieve their decarbonization plans. Fostering e-mobility and kick-starting EV deployment is thus consistent with relevant national climate-resilient development pathways.

D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

# Sustainable Development Goal alignment

The Program aims to make large-scale cuts in GHG emissions. It contributes significantly to sustainable development goals (SDGs) 3 ("good health and well-being"), SDG goal 7 ("affordable clean energy"), SDG goal 9 ("industry, innovation and infrastructure"), SDG goal 11 ("sustainable cities and communities"), and SDG 12 ("responsible consumption").

## Environmental co-benefits

Major environmental co-benefits are reduced emissions of pollutants and reduced noise emissions. The major pollutants targeted in the cities in PM10 and NOx emissions. The impact of the Program on these pollutants has been quantified based on combustion emissions of new fossil-based vehicles (comparing a new vehicle with an old one). This is a conservative approach as emission reductions are not based on comparing the old replaced with the new EU but on comparing a new (EU) fossil with a new PM1. For the methodology used, the database as well all assumptions see Annex 2. The following table shows the projected reduction of pollutants of the Program.

| Projected PM10 Reductions (in tons/year) | Projected NOx Reductions (in tons/year) |
|---|---|
| 43 | 6,842 |

## Social co-benefits including health impacts

The major social benefit is improved air quality (plus reduced noise). Air pollution is a major problem in most Latin American cities with levels affecting seriously human health. According to the latest urban air quality data from WHO, 10 out of 15 Latin American cities have PM2.5 concentrations that do not meet WHO air quality guidelines[6]. The WHO estimates that annually 4.2 million deaths result due to excessive exposure to fine particulate matter equivalent to 7.6% of all deaths[8]. The poor are disproportionately affected by air pollution, and the impact is even more severe for children and the elderly who are particularly vulnerable. Recent studies show that women are more affected by poor air quality than men because they spend a greater proportion of their time outdoors in Latin America. Fossil vehicles are a major source of air pollution in cities. Although they do not represent a majority of vehicles they are extensively used (large mileage), have high emission factors and are basically diesel powered. Diesel-powered vehicles are also the most polluting type of vehicle, with PM10 emissions from fossil vehicles contributing in general more to air pollution than private passenger cars or motorcycles[see Annex 2]. The use of electric vehicles is a key element for reducing air pollution.

The COVID-19 pandemic has created an unprecedented challenge for many Latin American countries, from the health and economic perspectives, but it is also opening new opportunities for an accelerated sustainable transformation towards more resilient cities and regions in support of a green and inclusive recovery. A recent report prepared by the ILO suggests that fostering of electric mobility can have a significant impact on reducing air pollution and associated costs[9]. It also has the potential to save or increase input savings of consumers on petrol and maintenance resulting in increased spending on goods with a high income elasticity which tend to be


---
**Page 73**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 67

## Economic co-benefits

The monetary value of reduced air emissions (GHG and air pollutants) has been calculated (see Annex 23 for details). The total economic benefit is estimated at $14 billion. This includes the monetized monetary value of emissions of PM<sub>10</sub>, and NO<sub>x</sub> for each country based on local levels of pollution at the ground level. These estimates are based on the assumption that the health effects of air pollution are linearly related to exposure.

The TA will include plans for a just transition to capture the potential positive benefits in terms of new jobs being created with mobility whilst enabling people which loose jobs e.g., in the maintenance area to find new jobs.

## Gender-sensitive development impact

Gender issues and development impact will be described in the gender-disaggregated targets in project targets. Each Program will be required to meet AFD's policy on gender equality. Individual projects will also be guided by the gender action plan (GAP) as included in Annex 8. The GAP includes actions and targets for each project, including: (i) Improved access to transport services; (ii) Increased collection on urban transport; (iii) Improved women’s access to jobs; (iv) Interventions including awareness raising campaigns and training of staff and service providers; (v) Provision of gender-sensitive trainings and workshops to disseminate best practices for reducing sexual harassment in public transport.

On a cross-sectoral level the GAP has as target to integrate gender considerations in transport sector activities. This includes: (i) The usage of a gender lens when designing the roadmap for different EV services; (ii) Increased awareness raising campaigns and training on how to include women’s opportunities in transport through capacity building and training; and (iii) Increased knowledge in how to include gender equality in Urban Transport projects through specific training materials.

## D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

* Mobility investments in the next normal | McKinsey


---
**Page 74**
---

The market study (Annex 2) identified the following barriers hindering a take-up of EVs in the countries involved:

- High upfront investments
- Lack of profitability of EV investments
- High costs of ownership (financial and technical risks)
- Lack of know-how on appropriate system design, business models, financial models and technical know-how
- First-mover problem as no commercial EV fleets yet operate in the country
- Lack of an enabling (policy) framework for sustainable mobility and EV deployment

APD has also realized market costing studies on the different vehicle segments for different price ranges and countries. The cost of ownership of electric vehicles compared to conventional vehicles has been assessed based on parameters such as the Total Cost of Ownership (TCO), FIRR, capital and equity required to acquire a vehicle, etc.

The costs of EVs are currently higher than those of conventional vehicles in all segments. However, it is expected that the cost parity will be reached by 2035. The market study has also assessed the barriers to commercial viability of EVs in the selected vehicle segments. The conclusion drawn from this analysis is that the current market conditions do not allow for a viable business case.

The creation of a favourable mobility ecosystem is supported by the benchmark study (Annex 2) which shows that countries which have followed this path are succeeding in deploying on a mass scale EVs whilst offering attractive returns to investors.

Contrary to renewable energy generation that is increasingly cost competitive, EVs are not yet commercially viable and remain largely underfunded. The market and benchmark studies show that, if unaddressed, the barriers will continue to exist for at least another 10 years or more in some countries. This will delay the transformation towards low carbon transport systems in the Program countries.

The EV sectors target have (i) limited access to commercial funding, (ii) early-mover costs and delays in technology development, (iii) high upfront investments, (iv) lack of profitability and acceptable risk levels. Therefore financiers are not in a position to fund such activities with commercial loans at the current stage.

Governments and transport operators also lack capacity and know-how on how the appropriate technology choices can be made, facilitate and conduct policies and business models to foster in a cost-effective manner EV deployment. This is clearly evident from public statements in favour of electric mobility which is however not followed up with concrete actions and implementations.

Argentina

Argentina’s total GHG emissions are estimated at 381 MTCO2e in 2020 (excluding LUCF) of which the transport sector 39 MTCO2e. In its NDC (Nationally Determined Contribution, 2015) Argentina commits to an absolute net emission target of 359 MTCO2e by 2030. The updated NDC of Oct 10/2021 includes that electric vehicles will be included in the National Electric Mobility Strategy. Argentina has also adopted a version of its National Electric Mobility Strategy in May 2018 with the support of UN Environment. Argentina is currently formulating specific legislation on electric vehicles, establishing the conditions for the installation or connection to public and private networks of charging stations, as well as the technical standards for the development of the electric mobility decree. Decree 51/2018 establishes a tariff reduction for the import of electric vehicles and their batteries. The National Electric Vehicle Charging Infrastructure Act, provision 293/2018 regulates the service of charging at fuel stations and defines safety specifications on the installation and registration of charging stations.

Brazil

Brazil’s total GHG emissions in 2020 were 1.06 GtCO2e (excluding LUCF) and transport emissions 110 MTCO2e. In its updated NDC of 11/2023 Brazil commits to reduce GHG emissions by 53% by 2030 compared to the year 2005 with the goal of achieving climate neutrality by the year 2050. The NDC indicates that Brazil will continue to invest in renewable energy and energy efficiency, as well as in public transport infrastructure to achieve mitigation targets. Brazil has already established various tax incentives for the import and purchase of EVs. There are 21 bills pending in the Chamber of Deputies on electric mobility that seek to regulate the use of electric vehicles. The Federal Law No. 9487/1996 which sets a tax reduction from 35% to zero % for electric vehicles and fuel cells. Senate Bill No. 64/2017 which seeks to prohibit the sale of non-zero emission combustion vehicles by 2065, is currently under discussion in the Federal Senate.

Colombia

Colombia’s GHG emissions for 2020 are estimated at 187 MTCO2e (excluding LUCF). Transportation emissions for the same year are 39 MTCO2e. Colombia NDC Update estimates that according to the reduction scenario, Colombia will reduce its total GHG emissions by 54% in 2030 compared to 2010 and commits to emit a maximum of 169 MTCO2e in 2030 (equivalent to a 51% reduction of emissions). The NDC compensates for the transport sector amongst others to achieve 600,000 registered electric taxis, buses and motorcycles by 2030. Colombia has also established a law that requires all public transportation companies in the country to have at least one zero-emission vehicle (ZEV) per 100 vehicles managed to provide for measures in public transportation services such as compliance with a minimum quality standard of service and access to information on the use of ZEVs. According to the same law, the goals for the incorporation of EVs is the acquisition of the fleet of zero-emission mass transport systems must follow the scheme of minimum proportions of 10% in 2025; 30% in 2030 and 60% by 2040. The National Energy Plan (PEN) 2020-2050 presents projections for the incorporation of EVs.

### Climate data
In 2020 COVID-19 resulted in large GHG reductions of the transport sector of more than 20% compared to pre-COVID whilst total GHG emissions dropped by only c.7%. The transport sector of Colombia reduced its emissions by 53% compared to pre-COVID whilst total GHG emissions dropped by only 1%. Climate data shows that in 2020, the transport sector of Colombia reduced its emissions by 14% compared to pre-COVID whilst total GHG emissions remained stable.

**Page 77**
