# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 71

## Costa Rica

Total GHG emissions of Costa Rica are estimated at 19.9 million tCO2e in 2019 with land transport being responsible for more than 50% of emissions. Emissions under a BAU scenario are expected to increase by 40% by 2050. The updated NDC of Costa Rica includes as target net emission of 9.1 MtCO2e by 2030 and holds on to the net zero target by 2050. Greening the transportation sector is key to achieving these targets. In this context, Costa Rica has developed a National Strategy for Electric Vehicles (NEVE) that quantified 2020 electric mobility targets for public transport, passenger cars and fleet (8% of the vehicle stock), as well as private vehicles (15%). The NEVE also includes a target of 30% of all road transport towards EVs. Costa Rica has also developed a national plan for electric transport which includes concrete strategies to achieve these targets such as the adoption of new legislation on incentives and promotion of electric transportation which includes targets for EV penetration, the establishment of a public charging infrastructure as well as important tax incentives for private EVs.

## Dominican Republic

The estimated GHG emissions of the Dominican Republic in 2020 were 37.0 million tCO2e (excluding LUCF) and 41% of these came from the transport sector[5]. The updated version of the Nationally Determined Contribution NDC-RD 2020 (Gobierno de la República Dominicana, 2020) has as target a 27% reduction in GHG emissions in relation to a Business as Usual (BAU) scenario by 2030. In terms of electric mobility the plan includes: (i) the promotion and adoption of electric vehicles for public transport, passenger vehicles, such as cabs and "conchos" with electric and hybrid vehicles; (ii) introduction of electric buses to replace diesel buses in urban areas; (iii) promotion of electric two-wheelers and mopeds; (iv) electric and hybrid mobility for private usage. In 2020 the Dominican Republic has adopted a National Strategy for Electric Vehicles (NEVE) which includes targets for EV penetration, the establishment of public charging infrastructure as well as important tax incentives for private EVs. It provides for a short, medium and long-term transformation of the transportation sector. The plan is aligned with all the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs 2030 and the Sustainable Development Plan (PDS) 2016-2030). The NEVE sets as target that by 2050 at least 40% of all vehicles in the country will be electric. The plan also includes targets for public transport, which are to be operational by 2030. By 2050 the respective goals are 100% EVs for official vehicles and public buses and 70% of all private vehicles shall be electric. For the freight sector the target for 2050 is 50% of all units to be electric.

## Mexico

The GHG emissions of Mexico for 2020 are estimated at 592 MTCO2e(excluding LUCF) with the transport sector contributing 109 MTCO2e. The baseline scenario for 2030 projects GHG emissions of 973 MTCO2e, of which 28% comes from road transport (Secretaría de Medio Ambiente y Recursos Naturales, 2013). The Government's NDC commits Mexico to reduce its emissions by 22% by 2030. For the transport sector the target is set at 18%, reaching 218 MTCO2e in 2030. The Climate Change Strategy for 2050 published in 2016 established that Mexico will reduce its GHG emissions to 45% below 2000 levels by 2050, with a focus on all types of transport (SEMARNAT-INECCS, 2016). In addition to the national instruments, the federal states

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 72

## Incorporate other Programs strategies to incorporate electric mobility. As example the Management Program to Improve Air Quality in the State of Mexico 2018-2030 proposes to prioritize the implementation of electric transportation and public systems over those that use fossil fuels, to provide the necessary infrastructure for charging stations, and to promote the adoption of electric vehicles (Secretaría del Medio Ambiente, 2018). Mexico City published the Strategy for Electrification of Mexico City 2018 - 2030, in which it sets a target that by 2030, 5% of all new cars sold will be electric vehicles and 40% of taxis and private operators, with the goal that 20% of the fleet will be electric in 2020 and 80% of the cabs will be hybrid or electric for the same year.

### Peru

The GHG emissions of Peru are estimated at 90 MCO2e (for 2030 excluding LUCF) of which the transport sector is 20.5MCO2e. The Peruvian State committed in December 2020 that its net greenhouse gas emissions would not exceed 208.8 MCO2e in 2030 (unconditional target). In addition, the Peruvian government has set a conditional target of reducing GHG emissions by 45% below business as usual levels by 2030 on the availability of international external financing and the existence of favourable conditions (conditional target). Adding both targets, unconditional and conditional, a total reduction commitment of 40% would be required. The Peruvian government has also set an unconditional target to reduce GHG emissions by 15% below business as usual levels with or without international support, the limit is fixed at 179 million tons (NDC, 2020). The technical background document for Peru’s NDC states that the country will have a fleet of approximately 348,000 vehicles and 75,000 electric light-duty vehicles by 2030 (measure E3M39) (Pérez Ministerio de Ambiente, 2018). Initiatives will be limited for investment projects in areas without access to gas pipelines (as long as EVs are not considered a viable alternative), and the government has set a target of 5% of all new cars sold at this level. The Competitiveness and Productivity Plan 2019-2029 that includes (i) a regulatory package for the promotion of electric vehicles, and expand their supply infrastructure by the end of 2019 if technical standards for charging stations by mid-2025.

### Regional Initiatives

The GEF E-Mobility Program implemented by the UN Environment Program (UNEP) in partnership with the International Energy Agency (IEA) is active in various targeted countries. Its focus is on technical assistance and capacity building to support national governments in developing policies, strategies, and plans for electric mobility. The program ensures that close coordination with existing programs and initiatives. The proposed Program is focused on supporting the transition of public transport towards electric vehicles and internet transformation while existing initiatives are more for creating readiness for electric mobility in general.

### Engagement with civil society

In each country AFD has performed country diagnostics and market studies with multiple interviews and meetings realized with all major stakeholders including national and local government bodies, energy sector, transport operators and civil society. Consultations have also been realized with stakeholders during the conception of the Concept Note and the Funding Proposal (see Annex 7 for details).

## Average
297

### Source: Gutter Consulting; see Annex 23
### Note: Includes bus plus charger

Based on the project pipeline AFD has realized a financial and economic modelling (see for details in Annex 3). For EVs FIRR and EIRR are based on differential analysis i.e. the FIRR and EIRR expresses the profitability of the incremental investments of purchasing an electric versus a fossil vehicle. Only costs which differ between these two technologies are included, e.g., fuel costs, maintenance costs etc. Fixed costs such as staff or overhead are the same between the 2 technologies and are not included. Revenues are also excluded from this analysis since they are common to both technologies. The EIRR therefore does not express the profitability of operations overall for a transport operator but reflects if the operator makes more profits or less losses if purchasing an EV instead of a fossil unit. Without GCF financing, the FIRR is estimated at 10% and the EIRR at 9%, both above the Weighted Average Cost (WACC) of 8% whilst with GCF finance the FIRR is on average at 9% above the WACC. The EIRR of e-buses is estimated to be around 27%.

The total Program EIRR is estimated at 18% including the measures of e-buses and public transport (in 2 countries) against the total Program cost including TA (see Annex 3).

### Efficiency considerations
The mitigation cost is 27 Euro/CO2, which is in the medium to higher range of the GCF portfolio reflecting the high level of investment costs of transport interventions.

### Effectiveness considerations
The economic internal rate of returns are superior to the social discount rate for all countries. Based on the financial analysis, the FIRR will be below the WACC without the GCF for all countries.

### Long-term financial viability
The long-run viability of the project is based on proven business models and the decreasing technology cost curve. The sensitivity analysis of the financial model shows that if the CAPEX for e-buses drops by 10%, the FIRR is in most cases higher than the WACC without GCF. In summary, as the CAPEX of e-buses drops, the projects will be financially viable with attractive returns in all the countries.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Project level
During the disbursement period, AFD Group will receive regular reports from the Beneficiaries, in order to track progress and identify potential issues as well as improvement opportunities. In this document, the Beneficiary will report on performance indicators, as well as qualitative information, based on the Financial Agreement signed with the Government.

AFD Group local agencies (represented by headquarters and/or teams) in the case of Proparco), will ensure the day-to-day monitoring by overseeing the implementation of the projects, meeting with the Beneficiaries on a regular basis, ensuring coordination with TA, exchanging with local government, etc. Every semester, they will review projects' performance and assess any potential risks. The local agency staff will be able to check the progress of the project at the field level and provide feedback to the project team in case of unusual situations.

Project Task Team Leaders based in Paris (headquarters), or in Proparco offices in Latin America will monitor and meet the Project Officer on a regular basis. Following this mission, a project implementation report will be shared with all the project stakeholders. At Proparco, a third party technical advisor hired at project level usually handles these tasks. Project TTAs at AFD and portfolio officers at Proparco will be responsible for overseeing project implementation and for approving funding disbursements. On a yearly basis, all projects financed by AFD


---
**Page 94**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 88

will be assessed through an internal review process based on several indicators (technical implementation, disbursement, procurement outcomes, etc.). Red flag projects that meet any kind of difficulties are subject to a specific monitoring process before returning to a regular implementation pathway.

At the end of the disbursement period, AFD will prepare, for each project financed by AFD, a project completion report summarizing the main events of the implementation phase, the results achieved, and the lessons learned. This report will also provide recommendations for future implementations.

During the repayment period, a biannual monitoring of the use of funds will be carried out by the local agencies and at corporate level by the middle office.

In addition, about a year after the drafting of the project completion report, the Evaluation department of AFD may conduct an ex-post evaluation of projects with a report a local consultant to do so. In most cases, AFD prefers to select a local consulting firm based in the country where the project is located. This allows the Agency to take advantage of deep contextual knowledge and to help develop local evaluation skills.

The consultant will be tasked with evaluating the impact of the project (economic, environmental, etc.) and writing a report that will be shared with all relevant stakeholders (central and local governments, companies, NGOs, etc.) and could also be made public.

## Program Impact Report

An Annual Performance Report (APR) will be submitted to the GCF following reporting requirements under the AMA and FAQs, in parallel to performance and financial information at country and Borrower levels. In particular, the APR will include a narrative report on the implementation progress of the project, including an assessment of the impact of the project on poverty reduction, gender equality, environmental sustainability, and ES&S as well as gender. Progarcó and GIZ, as Executing entities, will be required, through the subsidy agreement, to provide periodic reporting, in line with GCF requirements, to indicate progress on fund utilization as well as development outcomes.

The APR will be submitted to the Secretariat on an annual basis for the period ending on 31 December within 60 days after the end of the relevant annual period. The last APR will be submitted by the Parties once both the Parties have entered into the relevant FAA and the last APR will be submitted within six months of the end of the relevant reporting period.

The implementation reporting period of the Programme will start from the date of effectiveness of the FAA until the Programme implementation and data collection are completed, including the development of the teams.

The mid-term and final independent evaluation of the Programme will be organized and managed by the Evaluation department of AFD, on the same basis as any project funded under the Programme.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Category | Category |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Credit | Credit | Credit |

#### Description

Inability of lender to service debt

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

AFD's comprehensive due diligence and technical support are designed to assess the borrower's ability to service the loan. All loans from the program will be subject to a credit analysis. As such the credit or loan default risk on repayment of program loans is low. Further to ensure that underlying projects of the Program are financially sustainable, AFD has developed a comprehensive financial analysis framework for each project. The design and implementation of each project shall be conducted in accordance with AFDs principles for the economic analysis of projects. The Program explicitly focuses on developing financially sustainable models at the project level itself.

### Selected Risk Factor 2

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

#### Description

EVs do not perform as expected and the operator cannot realize standard operations.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

AFD's comprehensive technical design and structuring of projects minimize this risk. All technologies have been proven at least in pilot cases in other countries.

### Selected Risk Factor 3

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | Medium |

#### Description

Beneficiaries' failure to comply with national regulations and AFD and GCF environmental, social policy, gender requirements and compliance with standards, policies and procedures.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

Each project will be subject to AFD's Environmental Management System as well as gender assessment. Each Project will be appropriately appraised and structured to meet AFDs and GCF’s requirements. Environmental, Social and Gender Action Plans will be developed during project appraisal to address any potential adverse impacts thereof will be covanteed through financing agreements. Its implementation will be monitored by AFD

## Selected Risk Factor 4

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| No or limited commercial uptake of EVs after initial investment uptake resulting in limited indirect Program impact. The declining EV price is an external factor, while the magnitude and speed of EV price decline is disputed. The trend of reduced price differential between fossil and EVs is undisputed. Modelling has been done for the 2015-2030 period to reflect the impact on the EV program and under BAU (see Reports 3 per country and the corresponding excel file included in Annex 3). | Other | Low |
| Description |  | Medium |

## Mitigation Measure(s)

Successful EV investment projects and well implemented TA measures to reduce the investment risk levels of EVs. Investment risk levels are affected by the EV price and the cost of EVs which result from a commercial uptake of EVs. Good design and implementation of investment projects and of TA can thus minimize this risk.

## Selected Risk Factor 5

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Risk of projects being involved in illicit practices (money laundering, terrorist financing or other prohibited practices) | Prohibited Practices | Low |
| Description | AFD has its internal system of control. All investment projects realize a first level due diligence to ensure that this risk is mitigated. |

## Mitigation Measure(s)

AFD will have its internal system of control. All investment projects realize a first level due diligence to ensure that this risk is mitigated.

GCF financing will be provided as loans or grants to sovereign and sovereign-backed entities, to cities or the private sector (see Annex 1). A GCF loan will be provided alongside co-financing from AFD. AFG integrity principles and associated due diligence will apply at project level, which will also mitigate this risk. All projects shall comply with AFD’s anticorruption policy and acknowledge that AFD reserves the right to investigate any allegations of corruption or other prohibited practices in relation to the project, including relating to the projects; and recipients of the grant/loan shall cooperate with any such investigation and provide all relevant information and documentation as requested. In addition, a due diligence and risk assessment procedures, prior to the approval of each specific project, AFD’s fiduciary team will perform and/or update an institutional capacity assessment on the corresponding EED. This assessment includes overall technical capacity, including procurement systems, internal and external controls, and recommendations on any fiduciary duties identified.

None of the project activities will be undertaken in a jurisdiction which is subject to or affected by United Nations Security Council. The AFD project procurement policies that will regulate the procurement process for all projects funded under this Facility will ensure that no goods, services, or works will be excluded if by an act of compliance with a decision of the United Nations Security Council taken under Chapter VII of the UN Charter. This includes any goods, services, or works provided directly to, from, or originates in, a particular country, person, or entity. Where the Borrower’s country prohibits payments to a particular firm or for particular goods by such an act of compliance, that firm may be included.

## Selected Risk Factor 6

### Description
Limited capacity or experience to design and implement e-mobility projects by beneficiaries.

Mitigation Measure(s)
- Technical assistance will focus on supporting entities to identify, design and structure e-mobility projects.
- Detailed legal and technical due diligence will be carried out by the AFD to develop robust projects.
- Support will be provided under the Program to assist in developing favourable policies for deployment of e-mobilities.

## Selected Risk Factor 7
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | Medium |

### Description
battery supply chain risks

Mitigation Measure(s)
This is managed by the vehicle manufacturers and might be a concern for individual manufacturers but not the Program. The Program will focus on the total number of buses to be procured, which is higher than the annual demand for e-buses of about 20k units. Moreover, one major e-bus supplier has sold about 35,000 buses in 2023. Hence the risk of the e-bus supply shortage is low. Separately, battery technology and input materials are evolving - in terms of technologies and input material. It is expected that market forces will adjust.

## Selected Risk Factor 8
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | Low |

### Description
Sustainability risk of vehicle maintenance and repair if local capacity is low.

Mitigation Measure(s)
It is envisaged that the bus supply contracts shall come with a 3-year maintenance and service contract with guaranteed bus availability. The Program will also provide training for the local mechanics. Tender documents shall include also lists of spare parts, equipment for repair and minimum training and capacity building courses. A trained chief mechanic of the supplier must be on-site during the 3-year contract this is to ensure that the supplier can provide the necessary support. The tender documents are currently being reviewed and subject to further adjustment, pending detailed technical due diligence to be conducted for each sub-project.

Selected Risk Factor 8

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | Medium |

Risk related to EV batteries during operations and end of life

### Main Considerations

The Technical Assistance component would conduct studies on this aspect on battery disposal/recycling. It also has training on safety aspects of batteries during operations. Depending on the outcome of the proposed study, the Technical Assistance component may recommend further actions. These could include, for example: (i) drafting battery regulations in line with those approved. For example, inclusion of EV batteries in the Basel Convention and other international agreements; (ii) developing a tracking system for all batteries or importing batteries to ensure environmentally sound management of batteries. This would prohibit the manufacturer and/or importer from disposing waste batteries in landfills and incinerators and obligate them to either collect and recycle used batteries or re-export them. The tracking system could include a tracking system for all batteries.

The batteries have a guaranteed lifespan of 8 years at 90% Scale of Health (SOH) and thus significant quantities of EV batteries will be generated in the industry on a normal scale only be possible in around 10 years from now. In the meantime, the used batteries are likely to be re-purposed or re-exported to the manufacturing country for which the Basel Convention has a provision.

See for details section B3 TA and ESG Annex.

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

AFD has a comprehensive Environmental and Social Policy (ESP). All projects need to comply with these requirements. The environmental and social risks and impacts of the project should be addressed in its planning, implementation and operation. The appraisal process also identifies opportunities for improving the environmental and social performance of the project. AFRICAN countries have implemented, and monitored in compliance with its policies, applicable regulatory requirements and good international practices. These include conducting an Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) to identify potential adverse impacts as well as opportunities to improve the environmental and social outcomes of the project.

AFD is committed to the principles of transparency, accountability and stakeholder engagement, and provides guidance on how these should be applied in projects. To ensure that AFRICAN countries have scale and scale and environmental and social risks and impacts of the project, AFRID requires its clients to disclose sufficient information about the risks and impacts arising from projects, engage with stakeholders in a meaningful, effective, inclusive and mutually appropriate manner and take into consideration the feedback provided through such engagement.

Each project under the Program will require a Stakeholder Engagement Plan, including a grievance mechanism. AFD will disclose on its website appropriate EAS information for each project under the Program.

AFD works very closely with governments, authorities and civil society to uphold human rights in country ownership of projects. In many regions, AFRICAN countries have been at the forefront of this Funding Proposal. AFRID has realized in all countries diagnostic and market assessment missions which have extensively discussed intervention options with the country and with stakeholders.

### G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

A gender assessment has been realized for all countries as well as a gender action plan. This serves as guideline for each individual project to be funded under the Program (see Annex B).

The last two decades brought significant advances for women in Latin American Countries. All of the countries in this Country ratified the Convention Belém do Parque, whose main achievement was to incorporate into law the right to equality between men and women. In the last 20 years, in Latin America, 19 countries have been advancing to prevent, sanction, and eradicate violence against women. In general terms, the situation of women has improved significantly since the ratification of this Convention and underreported, and before 2006 no country in Latin America established femicide as a crime, while by 2015 countries already did. Likewise, in many parts of the World, civil society has raised strongly against violence against women and has been successful in obtaining laws that criminalize such violence (less) than 30 years ago.

The seven countries have included quotas of around 30 percent for political representation, and many countries have reached it at the national level yet at the local level, most lag behind. These countries have achieved this through a variety of mechanisms, including gender quotas, reserved seats, or by ethnic group or race. Party in education is mostly rated in all countries at the primary and secondary levels, and in many cases, girls are outperforming boys; whereas in tertiary education attainment varies by country, and careers in STEM (Science, Technology, Engineering and Maths) remain predominantly

The outlook is both keeping the progress and continue reducing the gaps in gender equality is challenging after the COVID-19 pandemic. UN Women and other international organizations fear that recent gains in gender equality might be lost. The COVID-19 pandemic has worsened women’s situation and has aggravated violence against women, especially domestic violence. In 2020, the rate of intimate partner violence increased by 38% for women aged between 15 to 49 years old compared with pre-pandemic levels. Gender based violence has increased and women in lockdowns are living with their aggressor under the same roof and can’t access support networks or services. In Pakistan, the rate of gender-based violence increased by 89%, and in Sri Lanka it increased by 72%.

According to UN Habitat, two-thirds of the population will be living in cities by 2050 (UN Habitat, 2018). Transportation systems are becoming overcrowded, lack of modal integration, are some of the problems that the increase of urban population is exacerbating. 46% of surveyed people in Peru responded that they have experienced sexual harassment or violence while using public transport.

The lack of safety features in the design of the transport system can contribute to gender-based violence, such as sexual exploitation and abuse which has increased in other health crises (18). Many women have reported that their fear of being attacked led to them household’s movement during the pandemic. Women in lockdowns in most countries have experienced a lack of access to public transportation services while work is deferred for all.

# G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## Financial Management

The detailed provisions regarding financial management of the GCF resources by AFD will be described in the FAA. Reporting will be provided by AFD to the GCF specifying among others:

- The amounts already committed and disbursed by AFD to local partners, by country, by project and by sector.
- The amounts already committed and disbursed by GIZ by country.
- The remaining amount on the different GCF accounts.
- A breakdown of the disbursements according to the repayment horizon.
- For the loan component, if any, a list of all the incidents recorded during the repayment period of the loans provided to the borrower.

Subsidiary agreements will be signed between AFD and PROPARCO, and AFD and GIZ.

For non-sovereign borrowers, a credit risk monitoring will be carried out by AFD through the assessment of the financial statements of borrowers, monitoring of financial covenants, etc. Audits will be performed as per AFD's procedures and as per the AMA.

## Disbursement

At AFD level, disbursement will be related to:

- Loan disbursements on projects. Disbursement conditions will be defined on a project-by-project basis and will be described in the financing agreement signed between AFD and the borrower.
- Fund transfers to GIZ and PROPARCO whose disbursements will be reported to AFD.
- Payments of the consultants in case of providing consultancy services for projects.

## Procurement

AFD's procurement rules are the same as those applied by the World Bank Group. The World Bank Group’s guidelines will apply to the public owned final beneficiaries in charge of implementing the projects financed by the Program. For a detailed presentation of AFD Procurement rules, see the Procurement Guidelines for AFD Financed Contracts in Foreign Countries available on AFD's website.

For privately owned projects sponsors, PROPARCO will apply the procedures dedicated to the specifics of private sector financing and project implementation.

GIZ procurement and awarding rules apply for procurement within the TA component for which GIZ is the procuring agency. AFD, PROPARCO and GIZ are accredited to the GCF; as such their procurement rules and procedures were assessed and evaluations are communicated on a yearly basis during the AES self-assessment exercise.

## G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

- ☐ No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.
- ☒ With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence.

# Secretariat's assessment of FP237

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The programme consistently scores medium-high or high against GCF's investment criteria, and is expected to contribute significantly towards a low carbon transformational shift and innovation in low-carbon transport. | The risk of limited commercial uptake of electric vehicles after the programme ends. |
| GCF's concessional finance will accelerate an e-mobility market transition in Latin America. | The project design needs to be carefully monitored by the project team. |
| GCF and other public sector actors will be providing essential financial assistance to (i) build transactional, and (ii) business models in the public and private sectors. | In order to avoid strong reliance on financing and parallel financing to materialize and support the programme's objectives, it is important to continue the strong country engagement demonstrated through the funding proposal process. |
| The programme provides technical assistance to develop a national e-mobility strategy that will identify innovative business models to create synergies with other ongoing public-private initiatives in the region which are crucial for scalability success in a region with a potential for rapid growth of electric vehicles. | The programme needs to carefully balance between the need for immediate and rapid implementation to react to developing market opportunities, and the need to rapidly demonstrate the publication e-mobility market. |

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the terms shown in the agreement between the Secretariat and the accredited entity, and if considered appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in Annex II of document GCF/JB.39/02.

## Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

### Programme background


---
**Page 122**
---

1. The ultimate objective is to accelerate electric vehicle (EV) deployment and enable a large-scale regional transition towards electric mobility in Latin America through financial and technical assistance. The programme will implement interventions to kick-start EV mass deployments by addressing key barriers to market entry and adoption, thereby reducing the risk profile of investments and utilizing comprehensive technical assistance.
2. The key strategic value of the programme is that it functions as a market accelerator, enabling countries to make significant progress towards their long-term low-carbon development goals through long-lived path dependencies and allocations of assets into fossil fuel technology pathways. The programme will support countries in developing and implementing national strategies for transformational targets. These interventions are made now at a time when oil is not yet commercially viable and thus initial investment support is required, as is the case in all countries that have prioritized EVs.
3. Latin America has strong enabling conditions for clean transport and e-mobility. The region has a high potential for electric vehicle deployment due to its large population of people having a median carbon grid factor of 0.252 kilograms of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO₂e)/kilowatt-hour, thus enabling a mobility with a significant greenhouse gas (GHG) mitigation potential. The region is also in the process of transitioning from fossil fuel dependency towards a decarbonization pathway. The updated nationally determined contributions (NDCs) of programmes and countries in Latin America are aligned with this transition.
4. The majority of countries have prioritized e-mobility as a core mitigation measure to reduce transport emissions, which is supported by the region’s strong enabling conditions for clean transportation policies or EV road maps, which are also supported through the present funding proposal.
5. The main motivating factors to foster e-mobility in the countries included in the programme are: (i) reduced air pollution and associated health problems; (ii) reduced greenhouse gas emissions; (iii) reduced fuel cost dependency on imported fuels and exposure to external shocks; (iv) increased energy security; (v) reduced oil imports, thus reducing import costs; (vi) reduced fuel import bills; and job creation. (vii) participate in the new growth industry of EV and battery manufacturing; and (ix) increase the attractiveness of public transportation vis-à-vis privately motorized transport.
6. The programme financial envelope is EUR 397.31 million, of which EUR 210.84 million and USD 150.00 million are committed to Component 1, EUR 10.00 million and USD 10.00 million to Component 2, and EUR 66.47 million and USD 39.56 million to Component 3.
7. The direct mitigation impact over the programme lifetime of 25 years is 8,334,051 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO₂e).
8. GCF categorizes the programme as II (medium) for environmental and social safeguards.

### 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

The programme is structured in three components:

- **Component 1:** Establishment of a sustainable mobility ecosystem encouraging electrification and shift to public transport (technical assistance);
- **Component 2:** Investments into electric public transport mode shift (financial assistance); and
- **Component 3:** Deployment of EV fleets (financial assistance).

**Component 1: Establishment of a sustainable mobility ecosystem encouraging electrification and a shift to public transport (EUR 154 million, GCF and EUR 12.81 million in grants).


---
**Page 123**
---

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

The programme consistently scores medium-high or high against GCF’s investment criteria. It is also consistent with GCF’s aim of supporting a low-carbon transformational shift and innovation, in this case e-mobility and low-carbon transport. The programme is well


---
**Page 124**
---

3.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium to high

The impact potentials are expected to be 4.334 GTCO2 eq of GHG emissions avoided.

These low estimates are based on a conservative estimate related to an indicative project in sub-Saharan Africa and four countries receiving investment support.

GHG emission calculations for a base have been made for potential or business-investment projects in the transport sector, which include electric vehicles (EVs) and public transport. The specific country variations are due to vehicle mileage, fuel type used, and, to a lesser extent, different speeds of infrastructure development. The choice of the factor per country influences the emissions of the EVs and therefore the emission reductions.

In addition, GHG emissions include calculations for public transport measures. This includes infrastructure investments in bus lanes and bus stations, smart ticketing, improved connectivity between urban areas, optimized bus fleets, and related measures to promote public transport systems in urban settings.

The emission factor per passenger-kilometer of private means of transport is two times higher than the emission factor per passenger-kilometer of bicycles for the programme in 25 years according with infrastructure lifespan.


3.2 Paradigm shift Scale: High

The programme will lead to transformation of urban EV transport systems and penetration via enabling policies, infrastructure investment, and technology and knowledge transfer at national and local levels.

The programme has potential for upscaling and replication at the national and international level through capacity created in the mobility ecosystem, successful business models, and a strong network of stakeholders. The programme will also have potential to enable policy for EV deployment. Technical support will aim to set up, improve, and enforce enabling policies for EV deployment.

The programme also aims to have an overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways consistent with national climate change adaptation strategies and plans. It will lead to significant GHG reductions in the transport sector. Without this contribution, the associated emissions from urban transport would be significantly higher. Kick-starting EV deployment is consistent with relevant national climate-resilient development pathways.


---
**Page 125**
---

3.3 Sustainable development potential

The programme contributes to the following key Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): SDG 3 (Good health and well-being), SDG 7 (Affordable and clean energy), SDG 11 (Sustainable cities and communities), and SDG 13 (Climate action).

The major social co-benefit is improved air quality, plus reduced noise. Air pollution is a major public health concern in many developing countries, including India.

The majority of countries have included electric mobility in their NDCs as a core mitigation measure to reduce transport emissions and have formulated collective electrification targets for the period 2030–2050. The GCF concessionary loan will support the implementation of these measures in each country. Each country is fully contextualized in the country plans, as further described in the proposed national action plan.

The accredited entity (AE) Agence Française de Développement (AFD) has also followed a robust stakeholder consultation process, which includes the national designated authorities (NDAs), ministries of finance and energy, transport, environment, and other relevant national and regional states, power utilities, transport operators, etc.

The GCF grants are therefore essential for the successful implementation of the required technical assistance activities.


3.4 Needs of the recipient

GCF concessionality is expected to address financial and technical capacity barriers, de-risk and leverage public-private capital for the EV sector, support public finance flows and incentives for electric mobility.

The programme countries depend on technical assistance that serves to enhance the stability of the national power grid and ensure the sustainability of the EV charging infrastructure. The GCF grants are therefore essential for the successful implementation of the required technical assistance activities.


3.5 Country ownership

No objection letters have been issued by all programme countries.


---
**Page 126**
---

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The total programme cost is EUR 210.98 million and USD 163.90 million, with a GCF contribution of EUR 78.33 million and USD 40.26 million, resulting in very efficient mitigation costs of EUR 5.28 per ton CO2 equivalent (tCO2e).

The economic internal rate of return (EIRR) is estimated at 18 percent, including the measures for e-buses and public transport (in two countries), against the total programme cost, including GCF contribution. The EIRR is above the weighted average cost of capital (WACC) of 9 per cent.

The mitigation cost is EUR 27/CO2e, which is in the medium to higher range of the GCF portfolio, reflecting the higher average GHG abatement costs of transport interventions.

The total programme cost per ton CO2 equivalent ranged between USD 97 (Côte d'Ivoire) to USD 476 (Poland), with a total average of USD 277/CO2e across the eight countries. The programme therefore has an efficient and effective cost structure.

The long-run viability of the programme is based on proven business models and the decreasing costs for e-buses, which are expected to be below EUR 100 per kilometer in the future. Analysis shows that if the capital expenditure for e-buses drops by ten percent, the FIRR is in most cases higher than the cost of capital (WACC). If the capital expenditure for e-buses drops further and costs drop, the subprojects will be financially viable with attractive returns in all the countries.

The AEF's financial analysis shows that GCF concessional financing improves the FIRR of subprojects by up to 10 percentage points. This is achieved through the GCF's ability to enable the FIRR to improve above or close to the WACC of 9 per cent. In Colombia and Peru, however, the FIRR for the subproject with GCF concessional financing is still below the target of 16 per cent. As AEF has presented an indicative pipeline of subprojects, the Secretariat does not have certainty of the exact amount of GCF resources to be allocated to each programme country. To mitigate the risk of country concentration, AEF has set a maximum of


---
**Page 127**
---

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

**4.1 Programme Background:** The programme is part of the regional efforts that aim to accelerate EV deployment and enable a transition towards electric mobility in 7 countries in Latin America. The programme intends to invest the deployment of e-buses and upgrading of bus infrastructure, including the construction of charging stations, in the programme pipeline, with focus on financing (a) urban mobility measures such as pedestrianization and bus lanes and route restructuring, which include infrastructure connections, road safety improvements, and public transport services; (b) the deployment of EVs e-buses, the related charging infrastructure, bus depot upgrades, and grid updates. The programme is expected to have a positive impact on the environment that includes the reduction of air pollution, noise levels and GHG emissions, and subsequently improving public health. In addition, the programme is expected to have a positive impact on micro-mobility options tend to lower accident rates, lesser road congestion, and improved the quality of life.

**Environmental and Social Risk Category and safeguards instrument:** The AE assigned the programme low to moderate environmental and social (ES) risk category that is equivalent to GCF’s A1 category. The programme will be supported by a combination of financing activities with potential limited adverse environmental and/or social risks and impacts in Category B, which would be mitigated through the application of high mitigation measures. Activities with potential significant adverse environmental and social risks and impacts that would fall under Category A will be supported under the programme. The Secretary General has approved the Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) applicable to the programme at the country level.

**Compliance with GCF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Standards:** The paragraphs below describe the programme's compliance with GCF’s ESS standards:

- **ESS 1.2: Identification of Environmental, Social, and Economic Risks and Impacts:** The ESMF provides an analysis of the potential E&S risks based on the characteristics of the programme, suggesting that the programme has a low risk for EV deployment in terms of environmental and social impacts. It outlines the construction of charging infrastructure, grid connection and maintenance, and outlines the eligibility criteria, exclusion list, and the ESS screening checklist for subproject due diligence. The ESS screening checklist is used to identify high, medium, or moderate risks that are identified as a result of the AE’s subproject E&S due diligence, an E&S consultant review, and/or the Secretary General’s review.

- **ESS 1.3: Mitigation Measures:** All subprojects under the programme are required to comply with the applicable national and local ES laws and regulations, including any relevant international standards or guidelines that are aligned with the International Finance Corporation (IFC) Performance Standards on Environmental and Social Sustainability.


---
**Page 128**
---

### ES 3: Labor and Working Conditions

The programme identifies potential risks and adverse impacts on occupational health and safety for workers involved during the construction, implementation and operation of EVs, charging stations, grid connection and bus depot upgrading projects. As part of the project design, comprehensive risk assessments will be conducted to determine the terms and conditions of employment, non-discrimination and equal opportunity, worker’s organizations, labour protection and the prevention of child labour and forced labour, etc., as part of the programme. The programme will ensure that all workers are protected from hazardous work or forced labour or child labour. The programme will promote the employability of local labour in the construction and operation phases through capacity building.

### ES 3: Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention

The programme is expected of minor short-term impact of natural resources due to construction activities. EIS is guided with the aim of minimizing environmental impacts, including those on water bodies and ecosystems through the prevention of pollution. Activities under the programme will potentially generate waste such as construction debris, hazardous materials, spent batteries, battery recycling, and disposal of EV batteries (primarily lithium batteries and the peripheral components). The programme will ensure that all waste is disposed in accordance with the grid-connected charging stations and bus depot upgrading, which may generate noise, dust particles, solid waste, and other pollutants. Waste management plans for construction debris, hazardous vehicles, such as oils and lubricants, lead-acid batteries, tires and scrap metals. The programme will exclude the production, trade, storage or transport of large volumes of products hazardous chemicals and materials that are not required to be used in the construction and operation phases.

### ES 4: Community Health, Safety and Security

The investment under the programme could potentially adversely affect community safety. These could include infrastructure development and safety risks during the construction and operation of the EV charging stations and upgrading related systems; increased accidentality as the low or non-existent use of electric vehicles may lead to a reduction in road traffic accidents; exposure to hazardous materials and chemical substances related to EV battery disposal and scrap recycling, which can be released into the environment and potentially affect the local community during the installation and cabling activities which may fluctuate the electric power service and cause damage to property. The programme will ensure that all waste is disposed of in accordance with the grid-connected charging stations and bus depot upgrading, which may generate noise, dust particles, solid waste, and other pollutants.

### ES 5: Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement

The programme will exclude projects that involve land acquisition and involuntary resettlement through the exclusion list. As part of the project design and implementation of the programme, it is essential to ensure that through appropriate project design, compensation packages for affected persons, community engagement, physical and/or economic displacement, and collaboration with other responsible agencies or sub-national jurisdictions. The programme will construct small-scale


---
**Page 129**
---

### ESS & Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Use of Living Natural Resources

#### 5.6 ESS: Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Use of Living Natural Resources

The Program will consider any eligible projects that pose risks or degradation to protected areas, critical habitats and/or sensitive areas or affect any modified, natural and semi-natural ecosystems in the countries that are part of the Programme. Projects that do not trigger AEF’s safeguard in this regard. The Program will exclude projects that involve any modification of land use or land cover within protected areas and recognized areas. As part of the subproject due diligence, the programme will exclude any operation leading to or requiring the destruction of critical habitat, or any forestry project which does not meet the requirements of the Green Climate Fund Indigenous Peoples Policy.

#### 5.6 GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS:7 Indigenous Peoples

All of the countries that are part of the Programme have committed to a transition away from fossil fuel-based Electric Vehicle deployment and a large-scale regional transition towards electro-mobility in Latin America through financial and technical assistance, the management of batteries for electric vehicles, and the development of charging infrastructure. Furthermore, there are no specific regulations or policies for the management of batteries for electric vehicles in the countries that are part of the Programme. Accordingly, the AEF has included an Indigenous Peoples Policy (IPP) as a safeguard requirement to ensure that all projects under this ESS will meet the requirements of the Green Climate Fund Indigenous Peoples Policy. In line with its role and responsibilities, the AEF will provide technical assistance and/or financial support to the AE who is also encouraged to share emerging good practices and success stories with the Group.

#### 5.6 Cultural Heritage

The ESM establishes specialized requirements for subproject specific ESM, when warranted, must contain measures to ensure the protection of the cultural heritage in the countries that are part of the Programme. As a part of an assessment, eligibility projects under the program do not affect or expect properties and sites of archaeological, historical, cultural, artistic, and religious significance. The Program will exclude projects that involve any modification of land use or land cover within protected areas and recognized areas. The project will include Any activity involving the destruction of culturally significant heritage or any activity leading to risks and impacts on cultural heritage.

#### 5.6 Sexual Exploitation, Sexual Abuse, and Sexual Harassment (SEAH) safeguarding

The AEF will ensure that all projects under this ESS are compliant with the transport sector and an in low gender-based violence (GBV) and SEAH during the construction of small-scale infrastructure projects. The AEF will also ensure that any potential GBV or SEAH questions will be identified. Potential mitigation measures include training transport operating companies on GBV and SEAH, ensuring that all project staff are trained on GBV and gender-based violence (GBV) and SEAH, and supporting the establishment of an adequate regulatory framework for the prevention and response to GBV and SEAH in the transport sector preventing SEAH and GBV. All subprojects will comply with the requirements established in the GCF regarding Sexual Exploitation, Sexual Abuse, and Sexual Harassment.

#### 5.6 Implementation

The PMO will be responsible for supervising the social performance and compliance with contractual requirements of the specific subproject. A dedicated team of E&S experts, including a mix of professionals from the AE and the Executing Entity and supported by an external E&S and Gender expert, will be established and will support early E&S screening and to ensure the day-to-day implementation and monitoring of the programme's impacts. The PMO’s E&S specialists


---
**Page 130**
---

will also ensure E&S due diligence, ensure E&S supervision of all subprojects. The AE will also ensure E&S monitoring. These steps are determined for the appraisal at the project level being: identification, instruction and commitment. Early E&S screening requirements, verification and categorization of risks and impacts, as well as the preparation of an Environmental and Social Plan (ESP) to be conducted under the responsibility of the project owner with review of the PME's E&S specialist, elaboration of the ESFP having the project owner responsible for including E&S specifications in the tender documents. The AE will ensure that the ESP is reviewed by the PME before being signed. During the supervision component, the implementation of the ESP and ESFP measures conducted by the project owner will be supervised by the AE. The monitoring and reporting requirements are defined by an environmental and social ex post evaluation conducted by AF or external auditors.

### Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure
The development of the programme has evolved based on consultations conducted with various stakeholders including national governments, international financial institutions, multilateral development banks, private sector companies in charge of electric energy, municipalities of the main cities in targeted countries, and major transport operators. The AE will ensure that the project owner engages with all relevant infrastructure and in the battery value chain. The programme also provides for engagement of stakeholders during the planning phase of the project to identify potential risks and impacts associated with activities through an ongoing process involving stakeholder analysis and planning, disclosure and dissemination of information, consultation and participation, grievance mechanism, and environmental and social risk management measures. The AE will ensure that the project owner prepares an indicative outline of the stakeholder engagement plan required by all the subprojects. The AE will also ensure that the project owner complies with the requirements for the identification phase, and time frame as the safeguards documents, a summary of the activities, along with the environmental and social risk management measures.

### Grievance redress mechanism
The programme provides access to grievance redress mechanisms at the AE implementing partners and subproject subjects. The AE will use its own E&S compliance framework for managing risks related to GRIW and SEAR. The project owner will design a GRM to receive and facilitate concerns or conflict resolution in relation with E&S risks and impacts, including those related to GRIW and SEAR. The AE will ensure that an understandable and transparent consultation process consistent with project and/or local content is established for the implementation of the GRM. The AE will also ensure that the AFSD's Environmental and Social Complaints Framework. So far, the AE does not have a specific procedure to manage the risks related to GRIW and SEAR. This has however covered by the operating procedures of the project owner. The AE will ensure that the project owner manages social risks and impacts and human rights violations that could result from AFSD-funded activities. In particular, the AE will ensure that the project owner complies with the AFSD Environmental and Social Framework, which includes risk management measures related to SEAR. The AE is responsible for ensuring that the project owner complies with its corporate GRM. Its corporate GRM is established and keeps the ESFP and guidance to subproject GRMs updated.

### Gender policy
The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan along with the funding proposal and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.
#### The gender assessment (using secondary data) provided an analysis of gender equality and women's empowerment in relation to transport infrastructure projects. It was conducted in the Eastern countries of Argentina, Brazil, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Mexico and Peru. All these countries have unique infrastructures and while the document does not put a spotlight on some of their gender dynamics, country level and transport sector-specific gender assessments are


---
**Page 131**
---

required to comprehensively unpack these gender issues and tailor the country’s gender action plan (GAPs) in alignment with the gender-based issues and the funding proposal. The AE has committed (in the GAP) to conducting additional sector-specific and country-level gender assessments.

### Risks

#### Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

The gender assessment report also presents initiatives and good practices emerging from the targeted countries and region on gender equality and urban transportation. In general, for the LAC region, the importance of women’s access to economic opportunities is often underreported. Before 2006, no country in the LAC region had established feminism as a crime, while by 2015, 13 countries had done so. Since then, the number of countries where feminism has become strongly activated against GBV and Argentina, Chile, Mexico, Peru and Uruguay have marched under the motto “No una menos” (Not one less woman).

While there are many initiatives that foster women’s access to economic opportunities, studies have shown that there are still constraints on women's emancipation in the region. These include: lack of access to land and property; lack of access to credit, loans, savings, etc.; constraints on women’s mobility (usage, fare schemes, time/commute factors); and safety (exposure to harassment and/or sexual violence risks involved). The gender assessment report also presents initiatives and good practices emerging from the targeted countries and region on gender equality and urban transportation. In general, for the LAC region, the importance of women’s access to economic opportunities is often underreported.

The programme will also integrate gender components in the projects financed by the programme. These include: improved access to public transport; improved safety for women investors; improved sex disaggregation of data collection on urban transport; improved women’s access to credit and loans; promotion of women’s entrepreneurship; capacity-building; promotion of gender equality at the workplace; and capacity-building for the staff of public transport projects on gender equality and work-life balance issues. The AE is required to ensure that the grievance mechanisms of the programme are accessible to both women and men.

The programme allows for the provision of financing for both public and private sector entities. The GCF will only be used to provide technical assistance (component 1) that cannot be financed by the private sector. All the subprojects must be aligned with the eligibility criteria in the term sheet.

### 4.3.2. Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (risk level)

- AFD is the AE and co-executing entity (co-EE) (components 2 and 3) for this programme.
- In addition, GIZ will be acting as co-EE for the programmes.
- AFD will be responsible for the implementation of eligible investments in the public sector.

### 4.3.3. Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

1. **Delays in implementation and impact risk:** The programme is planning to implement different modalities, including direct investment, co-financing, delivery/public-private sector-led indirect delivery in multiple countries.
2. **The final financial structure and business model will be subject to an ongoing review during the programme's implementation:**
   - This great flexibility and complex implementation arrangement may pose a risk of delays in the programme.

### 70. Credit risk (GCF) will be exposed to the credit risk of borrowers:
- GCF loans may be provided on a stand-alone basis or as co-financing with participation without sovereign guarantee, and to the private sector.
- The sovereign credit rating of the partner countries ranges from non-investment grade to investment grade (Moody’s).
- Most of these countries are mainly targeted for intermediate cities, which may not always have sovereign guarantors.

### 71. Credit risk (GCF) will be exposed to the credit risk of borrowers:
- The credit rating of the partner countries ranges from non-investment grade to investment grade.
- GCF loans will be provided on a stand-alone basis or as co-financing with participation without sovereign guarantee, and to the private sector.

### 72. Co-financing risk: The total financing amount is indicative.
- The equity contribution from the GCF is subject to an annual adjustment based on the project eligibility criteria in the short lists.
- A minimum of 40 per cent in each subproject and a minimum or financing of 60 per cent at the overall programme level.

### 73. Risk note: The GCF reimbursable funds will be provided in hard currencies (in EUR and USD) and will be subject to exchange rate fluctuations.
- Exchange rate fluctuations may significantly affect returns of subprojects, and consequently the borrowers’ ability to repay.
- If the GCF proceeds approved for a subproject are converted into local currency from the exchange rate fluctuations, GCF shall not bear any responsibility of providing additional financing.

### 4.5.4. Project viability and concessionality
- The programme enables commercial uptake and mass deployment of EVs without further support from the GCF at the end of the programme in the targeted countries.


---
**Page 133**
---

expected commercial investors will be crowded in to the beaten path over the framework condition (e.g., concession contracts) and the performance risks of a bus are manageable. However, the current term sheet does not have a split allocation between public and private sector providers, nor any budget or indicators to track the likelihood of commercial uptake of EVs in the funding proposal.

75. The interest rates of the GCF senior loans are differentiated by the public and private borrowing costs. The final pricing for each loan will be determined by the AE/EE on a case-by-case basis with different spread. The AE/EE will ensure that if there is any concession, it is fully transparent and principally ensure that the GCF concessionally passes down to the final borrowers. The pricing to the private sector will not be lower than the pricing for the public sector.

### Compliance risk (medium risk)

75. Following a thorough assessment of various risk factors outlined in the proposal, the Secretariat has determined a medium risk rating of medium for the project. Upon evaluation, we have identified several key points warranting attention and consideration.

First, regarding risk assessment, it is noted that due diligence of counterparties is contingent on the completion of the feasibility study (post-feasibility stage), the organizational structure remains fluid, posing challenges to conducting due diligence. The Secretariat has established a robust framework between eligibility criteria and due diligence questions, ensuring that the latter focuses on implementation risks.

Second, concerns surrounding sanctions and prohibited practices have been addressed with assurances that project activities will not take place in jurisdictions affected by UN Sanctions or other international prohibitions against certain individuals or entities. Additionally, mechanisms are established to mitigate risks related to illicit practices and ensure compliance with anti-corruption policies.

Third, the importance of robust counterpart due diligence is emphasized, with adherence to accredited procedures and regular assessments of technical capacity and fiduciary risk. Similarly, the Secretariat has established a robust internal control system policy ensuring compliance with integrity principles and due diligence at the project level.

Additionally, stringent measures already in place to address E&S risks can also serve to address other risks such as illicit practices, corruption, and prohibited practices. GBMs, including stakeholder engagement plans and an established mechanism for handling complaints, underscore the commitment to transparency, accountability, and stakeholder engagement.

In summary, the Secretariat has conducted a review of the proposal in accordance with relevant guidelines and determined that it does not present undue risk development with respect to compliance issues. Based on available information for this funding proposal, the Secretariat has determined a risk rating of 'medium' and has no objections to this proposal proceeding at this time.

#### 4.4 Results monitoring and reporting

1. In the case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of results area and single proposal is not material.

The roles of these institutions in the implementation process are illustrated as follows: AFD, as AE; KfW, as EE and on-finance for the financial assistance targeting public companies; Proparco, as EE and on-finance for the financial assistance targeting private companies; IFC, as EE and on-finance for the financial assistance targeting project preparation, and for the G4 activities related to the investment projects; Proserv, as agent for the disbursement of funds from AFD; KfW, as EE and on-finance for the project owners; GIZ, as EE for technical assistance activities; and KfW Development Bank and GAF, as co-financers for financial assistance.

The steering committee shall notably be responsible for ensuring that the projects are in line with the programme’s implementation issues and identify solutions, and ensure coordination and communication between partners.

In this document, the beneficiary will report on performance indicators, as well as qualitative and quantitative information about the project’s impact and results. ### Legal assessment

The AEA was signed with the AE on 13 November 2017 and became effective on 16 January 2018. The AE's five-year accreditation term lapsed on 15 January 2023 but was extended by decision B/37(18), paragraph (g), until the earlier of three years from the date of lapse or the end of the programme period, subject to the approval of a new accreditation framework as adopted by the Board. The Board approved the re-accreditation of the AE pursuant to decision B/40(20) on 16 February 2023.

The AE has provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the programme.

The proposed programme will be implemented in seven countries: Argentina, Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Guyana, Suriname, and Venezuela. The GCF Secretariat provided draft agreements on privileges and immunities in these seven countries. This means that, among other things, GCF is not protected from legal action by the host country or its nationals. These agreements will be further assessed. The Secretariat has engaged with the relevant countries as follows:

- **Argentina:** The GCF Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities to the Government of Argentina most recently on 7 February 2023. The latest communication was sent by the GCF Secretariat to the Government of Argentina on 15 March 2023.
- **Brazil:** The GCF Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities and a background note to the Government of Brazil most recently on 1 February 2023. The latest communication was sent by the GCF Secretariat to the Government of Brazil on 22 February 2024.
- **Colombia:** The GCF Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities and a background note to the Government of Colombia most recently on


---
**Page 136**
---

May 2024. The latest communication was sent by the GCF Secretariat to the Government of Colombia on 8 May 2024;

(i) Costa Rica: The GCF Secretariat and the Government of Costa Rica have an agreed version of the draft agreement on privileges and immunities. The latest communication was sent by the GCF Secretariat to the Government of Costa Rica on 13 March 2023;

(ii) Dominican Republic: The GCF Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities and a background note to the Government of the Dominican Republic most recently on 24 January 2023. The latest communication was sent by the GCF Secretariat to the Government of the Dominican Republic on 31 March 2023;

(iii) Mexico: The GCF Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities and a background note to the Government of Mexico most recently on 24 January 2023. The latest communication was sent by the GCF Secretariat to the Government of Mexico on 14 March 2023;

(iv) Peru: The GCF Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities and a background note to the Government of Peru most recently on 28 July 2023. The latest communication was sent by the GCF Secretariat to the Government of Peru on 14 August 2023.

The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism and the Independent Integrity Unit both have expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, any such activities or investigations will only be made after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expatriation of assets, including through the provision of relevant privileges and immunities.

To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:

(a) Submission of a statement of legal opinion, in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, within 120 days after Board approval, confirming that the GCF has obtained all final internal intervals, whichever is later; and

(b) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.
