# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP237

## Proposal name: E-Motion: E-Mobility and Low Carbon Transportation

### Accredited entity:
Agrone Français de Développement (AFD) (French Development Agency)

### Countries:
Argentina, Brazil, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Mexico, Peru

### Programme size: Large

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Overview
The programme intends to promote an integrated transformational shift to climate resilient zero-emission mobility systems through investments in climate resilient urban transport infrastructure and low carbon vehicles (electric vehicles) combined with mode-shift measures to increase the performance and convenience of urban public and non-motorized transport, while reducing the demand for private motorized transport. The programme will support countries undergoing shifts in sustainability-oriented policies and practices towards increasing the share of public and expanding non-motorized transport. The paradigm shift of the programme will be achieved by addressing barriers to EV deployment and uptake to demonstrate proof-of-concept in the targeted Latin American markets.

- The proposed programme's financial envelope is EUR 307.7 million, utilizing loans and grant instruments from AFD (EUR 158.9 million) and IDB (EUR 148.8 million). This represents a loan share of 52 per cent, EUR 115.6 million, consisting of EUR 93.9 million in concessional loans and a grant of EUR 22.7 million.
- The programme is expected to result in the deployment of approximately 400,000 vehicles over a period of 10 years, which is expected to be 4.38 billion tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (Mt CO2 eq).

### 1.2 Impact potential
The programme objective is to accelerate electric vehicle (EV) deployment and enable a large-scale regional transition towards electric mobility (e-mobility) in Latin America through financial and technical assistance.


---
**Page 138**
---

## Paradigm shift potential

### Scale: Medium

Most importantly, these investments, if successful and sustainable, would lock-in of long-lived high-emission infrastructure in public services, providing additional benefits to society in terms of reduced reliance from fossil-fuel imports, clean air and health benefits, and the increased use of public transport.


---
**Page 139**
---

13. The programme aims to enable paradigm shift by shifting to an accelerated master the transition from a fossil mobility sector to an electrically led zero carbon mobility sector. The paradigm shift is achieved through the development of an EV conducive ecosystem in each of the market segments (urban, intercity and rural).

14. However, based on the funding proposal, the programme design is very broad at this stage and the implementation aspect specifically remains largely undefined as no specific fully prepared project proposals have been submitted for consideration. This leaves a wide range of interesting conceptual implementation arrangements such as three the options for Public–Private Partnerships (PPPs) that could be considered. The PPPs are envisaged to be implemented mostly private sector led with the public entities overseeing. The difference in the three PPPs is the optimal alignment of social, environmental and impact and paradigm shift benefits, for example: (i) the ability to create a private sector financed and operated vehicle fleet; (ii) the creation of an enabling environment; and (iii) the creation of scale and replicability. The stated goal of the programme is to “enable the transition from fossil fuel vehicles to electric vehicles for investors” – however it is unclear in both the theory of change and its implementation how this will be achieved, as there are no clear mechanisms or agreements between the parties; how they will be aligned with the relevant countries, and how will risks be allocated between the public and the private sector. The programme design is very broad at this stage.

15. Secondly, AFD’s role in the AE fails to present a cohesive strategy for leveraging co-investment from private sector actors. While the programme has a strong focus on scaling up and this programme will certainly contribute to scaling up new models for investing in EV buses, the programme does not provide any clear guidance or framework for how these investments can be scaled up in this sector long term. By some International Monetary Fund 2023 estimates, local savings in Latin America could reach US$1 trillion if and the programme were to scale up co-investment and/or co-ownership of electric buses through the development of public EV bus and ancillary infrastructure; it would contribute to long-term sustainable urban transportation and climate resilience. However, the programme does not provide any clear guidance on how this can be achieved or financing a large-scale pilot in each city and the scaling up of 20 buses is alluded to but not defined. The programme also fails to present a strategy for leveraging co-investment from private sector actors as it does not define how AFD will work with other development partners to create a stronger paradigm shift, as well as developing a cohesive strategy for leveraging co-investment, from both public and private sectors.

16. The ITAP strongly recommends that the AE design co-investments with private local players, where possible, for the long-term sustainability of the sector and market creation.


### 1.4 Sustainable development potential

The programme contributes to key Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): SDG 3 (Good health and well-being), SDG 7 (Affordable and clean energy), SDG 8 (Industry, innovation and infrastructure) and SDG 9 (Build resilient infrastructure, promote inclusive and sustainable industrialization and foster innovation).

The main environmental co-benefits of the proposed programme are expected to be reduced emissions of pollutants and reduced noise emissions. The major concern is for air pollution in urban areas where there is a high concentration of vehicles.

The major social co-benefit would be improved air quality (plus reduced noise). Air pollution is a major problem in most Latin American cities, with levels seriously affecting human health. According to the World Health Organization (WHO), 100 million people live in and middle-income countries with more than 100,000 inhabitants do not meet World Health Organizations air quality guidelines. The World Health Organisation estimates that 42 nations


---
**Page 140**
---

deaths each year result from excessive exposure to fine particulate matter, equivalent to 7.6 per cent of all deaths.

The programme also has the potential to deliver economic co-benefits due to the monetary value of reduced air emissions (GHG and air pollutants). The economic cost of pollution is estimated at US$10 billion annually in terms of premature mortality from fine particulate matter and nitrogen oxides for each country based on local levels of pollution at the ground level, and the impact on health and lives caused by this type of pollution in all programmes countries.

## 1.5 Needs of the recipient

**Scope: Medium**

The majority of programme countries have included a mobility as their NDCs as a core mitigation strategy to reduce transport emissions and have formulated concrete decarbonisation targets through regulations, policies, or EV strategies. The programme's activities in areas that are centralised in the country plans, as further described in the funding proposal and the feasibility study.

The accredited entity (AE), has also followed a robust stakeholder consultation process, which includes representatives from government agencies, development partners, multilateral banks, private banks, municipalities, and regional states, power utilities, transport operators, etc.

A detailed power-sector assessment has been made per country as separate report. See new report attached as additional report under annex 2. This includes power availability, electricity prices, grid capacity, transmission lines, and other relevant infrastructure, which anchors the important paradigm shift argument that electrification of transport systems in countries with high renewable energy will lead to lower emissions.

However, the AE has not yet identified which specific investment projects is still undefined. The country-specific mobility ecosystem constraints and respective solutions are not formally documented or communicated to the AE. This is a major concern for recipient governments on which the modality of public or private-public or mostly private sector led implementation of the programme. This lack of country/city-specific design - even at a broader level, may result in an inefficient use of resources.

The IEAP understands that due to the programmatic approach of this proposal, there is a degree of flexibility for the AE to determine whether concessionary funding is needed by the recipient or even whether concessionality is needed without understanding the specific needs and market bottlenecks of the cities where the GCF capital will flow.

The AE has also not yet held any formal dialogue or in-depth conversation on the questions-and-answers call, on the relative importance of e-mobility policies, enabling environmental regulations, and other relevant factors that may influence the selection of underlying conditions versus intervention projects. The AE argued that the intervention strategy is to use concrete implementation projects to foster policies, regulations, and frameworks conducive to the wider adoption of electric vehicles in the country. The IEAP team has identified several workable implementation cases for, e.g., concession contracts or business models such as the operation of public transport systems by private operators. These cases can serve as templates together with high-quality bus-system design, enable e-buses to be commercially competitive in final markets and provide a basis for scaling up the intervention strategy. The IEAP has also comparable total costs (to diesel buses) will governments, city authorities, transport operators, and other stakeholders. This is particularly important when considering the transition away from fuel buses. Stakeholders consulted by the programme team have repeatedly stressed the point that investment projects involving significant fleets (not pilot projects) need to be implemented prior to taking critical regulatory decisions, which affect public transport services. The proof of


---
**Page 141**
---

1.6 Country ownership

- Scale: High

The programme is well-aligned with the programme countries' policy objectives on low-emission transport, urban development, climate-change commitments in NDCs and sectoral plans, which include concrete e-mobility targets. All seven countries have also established/are formulating national policies and strategies for electric vehicles (EVs) and have set specific targets for the EV sector, to which this programme proposed will contribute.

The design of the project builds on the concept one stage ahead of funding principal development partners have adopted a detailed stakeholder engagement approach including meetings with the national designated authorities and stakeholders in the public and private sector representatives from government, as well as with financial institutions and market studies.


1.7 Efficiency and effectiveness

- Scale: Medium to high

Proponents of the proposed programme target a high co-finance ratio of 61 per cent (leverage factor of 1.6). Future investments are not expected to require further GCF finance due to cost-effective technology, competitive procurement processes in an appropriate EV-mobility projects for public transit (Inter-American Development Bank, Asian Development Bank and Andean Development Corporation and Asian Development Bank programmes).

The required concessionality for kick-starting the EV public transport mobility has been clearly measured, thought through, and justified. Most impressive is the ability of the AE to draw on existing resources and expertise in the country to ensure the long-term financial viability of the right-country-specific projects are designed and implemented. The benchmark report (Lazard 2018) shows that the cost of battery-electric vehicles (BEVs) and plug-in hybrid electric commercial EVs (140+ units) have used, at least initially, financial-support instruments in the form of grants to kick-start EV deployment. The market studies realized in all countries also show that the cost of BEVs is competitive with conventional vehicles for public transport use. The market studies, however, also reveal that in the medium term due to price decreases, a commercial uptake of EVs is viable.


---
**Page 142**
---

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The programme is trying to create scale in a sector which requires it for two reasons: (i) in order for the cost of capital (related to perception of risk) to come down and (ii) the cost of the infrastructure itself. The programme has chosen to focus on bus fleets, which are well-designed from an impact, country ownership, and efficiency and effectiveness point of view. The main challenge is that it will be difficult to scale up the programme in a way that is inevitable but by creating scale, the programme will accelerate these positive transformations for the BOP sector in the pilot cities and countries.

The TAP has not provided details on which mode of implementation it will use to carry out the EV bus fleet investment and necessary infrastructure (localized grid upgrades, etc.). The TAP has also not provided any information about whether it is private-led. However, it has selected suitable partners in AFD, Proparco and Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) with significant responsibility for planning to assess, evaluate, design, and implement which design model makes sense for each location.

The main gap that the TAP finds in this programme is a lack of focus on mobilizing the billions of dollars needed to finance the scale-up. The TAP has not provided details about the current programme or as future investors in the space. Latin America has deep capital markets: the region’s financial markets have substantial liquidity, a broad range of funding instruments, and significant participants. Sovereigns are the right partners for long-term financing sustainability of the targeted sector. In addition, if they were to invest in this sector, they would be able to diversify their portfolios while taking on risk against mitigant currency depreciation and foreign shocks. Large scale infrastructure in Latin America is typically financed by loans to be invested in hard currency. Development Financial Institutions (DFIs) must do more.

The TAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal with the following recommendations:

(a) AFD, Proparco and GIZ co-Design with local stakeholders of an investment strategy to attract local investors in this space either through inter alia co-investment, exits, refinancing, etc. The TAP has suggested that the programme should be designed such that they allocate a significant portion of the technical assistance AND the GCF capital to enable the development of a robust and sustainable private sector in Latin America. Local capital – the TAP suggests for the private sector investments of this programme, that e.g., sovereigns or other large institutional investors should be allocated to take on more risk averse senior positions in the capital stack – in order to ensure that after the


---
**Page 143**
---

AFD programme is over, the local financial markets take up the baton of financing and scaling up this important sector for their economies, the health of their people, and the planet.

## Proposal name: E-Motion: E-Mobility and Low Carbon Transportation

### Accredited entity: Agence Française de Développement (AFD) (French Development Agency)

### Countries: Argentina, Brazil, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Mexico, Peru

### Programme size: Large

## Impact potential

**ITAP Scale:** High

# Introduction

The Green Climate Fund acknowledges in its Gender policy that its initiatives to combat climate change are more sustainable, equitable and have more probabilities of reaching their objectives when gender equality and women empowerment questions are included in designing and implementing its programs and projects.

## The Role of the E-mobility and Low Carbon Transportation Multi-Country Facility

The role of the E-mobility and low carbon transportation multi-country facility to support innovation and implementation of policies, technologies, and practices that will help address climate change while closing the gender gap, and to prevent and minimize harm is three-folded. First, the transport sector is a catalyst to change social norms and attitudes towards women in the transport sector by reducing violence against women and girls, by providing visibility to women working in non-traditional roles in the EV (electric vehicle) markets, and by considering the mobility of care when designing policies and programs that will help women access decent work or improve their access to jobs in traditionally sex-segregated occupations or improve access to investment loans for female entrepreneurs. Third, the capacity building, training, and sharing of know-how to women and men designing the investments plans in the program will systematize gender mainstreaming in the transport sector.

The E-mobility and low carbon transportation multi-country facility seeks to both directly and indirectly reduce Green House Gas (GHG) emissions from the transport sector in Latin America through electrifying intensive urban public transport systems, promoting electric vehicle adoption, and increasing battery EV adoption (and therefore higher GHG reductions and social co-benefits). The program is split in three components:

- **Component 1:** Establishment of a sustainable mobility ecosystem encouraging electrification and shift to public transport (TA)
- **Component 2:** Incentive measures for PT mode shift (FA)
- **Component 3:** Deployment of EV fleets (FA)

## Gender Assessment E-Mobility Program

GRUTTER CONSULTING


---
**Page 150**
---

This gender assessment provides information about gender equality and the empowerment of women as well as its links with the transport sector in the following countries: Argentina, Brazil, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Mexico, and Peru. The assessment is organized into two sections that feed the recommendations that will design the proposed Gender Action Plan.

The first part covers data on relevant indicators to offer a snapshot of women's unique situation in each of the selected countries. A selection was made from the indicators related to gender equality and women’s empowerment prompted a selection of those indicators with available and updated data that were reliable for all seven countries. The indicators are drawn from the World Bank, UNICEF, ILO, and ad hoc country studies on gender or data sources from reliable international entities. The indicators selected reveal gender gaps in education, labor force, and political participation and furnish data on maternal mortality, access to health services, and violence against women. Data is available for all countries at the national average or referred to urban women. The unique multidimensional and overlapping inequalities of each country has been included slightly because of the complexity of an assessment for seven countries, and only a few indicators are presented in this document.

The emphasis on displaying the most recent county data available derives from the current COVID-19 pandemic situation. The numbers are changing and proposing the most recent data will allow us to better know how women have been affected by the pandemic before the health crisis. Nevertheless, the first section begins with a small summary that offers available information about the pandemic’s impact on the situation of women in the Latin American and the Caribbean region.

The second section presents initiatives and best practices carried out on these countries and the region on gender equality and urban transportation. Given the large huge number of indicators for gender and urban transport, this section is divided into three subsections. The first part of the report is presenting best practices grouped by topic and activities to facilitate the reading. This second section is structured in three interrelated topics: education, health, and jobs. After the sub-section about jobs, there is a specific subsection on the impact of the pandemic on women’s employment, which has used the available data at the time of drafting the assessment.

Following, a third section describes the capacity of the AFD, PROPARCO and GIZ to implement, monitor and evaluate the gender actions proposed in the GAP

Finally, the assessment concludes with the recommendations drawn to guide the Gender Action Plan.

# Section 1: Gender assessment by country

## Summary

The last two decades brought significant advances for women in Latin American Countries. All of the countries in this program ratified the Convention Belon do Pará, whose main achievement was to incorporate specific legislation to include "femicide" as a specific aggravated crime. Since its ratification in 1994, countries have been advancing to prevent, sanction, and eradicate violence against women. In general, Latin American countries have made progress on gender equality, but the pace has been uneven and underreported, and before 2006 no country in LAC established femicide as a crime; while by 2015 16 countries already did. Likewise, in many parts of the World, civil society has raised strongly against GBV and Femicide in Bolivia, Mexico, Peru, and Uruguay have marched under the motto: "No una menos" (Not one woman less).

The seven countries have included quotas of around 30 percent for political representation, and many countries have reached at the international level on this objective, lagging the others. These countries have advanced in terms of gender health, but not every country, lagging behind, and by ethnic group or race. Parity in education is mostly reached in all countries at the primary and secondary levels, and in tertiary levels in some countries. The situation varies significantly between countries by country, and careers in STEM (Science, Technology, Engineering and Maths) remain predominantly masculine. Occupational segregation persists across countries and women's careers concentrates in low paid jobs, which are more vulnerable to the effects of COVID-19 on their income, health, safety, and well-being. Despite many laws, gender wage gaps persist across sectors and gender roles impede women’s access to better economic opportunities since domestic responsibilities are mostly in their hands.

Education in girls is affected by adolescent pregnancy, which is still high and despite the widespread regional adoption of policies to reduce it, a serious challenge remains. Child marriage is also more illegal for minors in all these countries; yet teenage pregnancies and minors living in union occur. Plans, strategies, and entities (Ministries or agencies) have been given more power or have been recently created, but entities are not funded enough to carry on, enforce, and follow up with all the gender equality agenda.

The outlook to both keeping the progress and continue reducing the gaps in gender inequality is challenging after the COVID-19 pandemic. UN Women and other international organizations fear that recent gains in gender equality could be reversed by the pandemic. The situation of women varies across countries but has aggravated differences amongst groups. In Brazil, the maternal death rate due to COVID-19 is twice as higher for black women than white women. In Argentina, 20 percent of domestic violence cases are in lockdowns living with their aggressor under the same roof and can’t access support networks. The figure below shows how now only in Brazil, from March to April 2020 compared to the same period in 2019, there has been a significant increase in calls for help against domestic violence (from 35 percent to 68 percent), and an increase of 47 percent in complaints of physical aggression. In Chile, the number of cases of rape. Complaints increased 27 percent and reports for physical aggression cases decreased by 25.5 percent. In Peru, there has been a significant increase in calls for help against domestic violence (from 30 to 68 percent), in Argentina by 25 percent (out of 10 murders happen at home). It is estimated that less than 40 percent of domestic violence survivors report it; adding to that, figures of actual gender-based-violence


---
**Page 152**
---

7

are worse during COVID-19 because the fear of getting infected prevents women from seeking help in health services. Lack of critical services and access to key healthcare and health services due to stay-at-home orders might have increased teenage pregnancies and maternal mortality. There is evidence that other forms of violence, such as sexual exploitation and abuse have increased in others health crises1.

Many women are experiencing a double crisis: the economic crisis and the pandemic, with severe lockdowns in most countries in the region and schools and workplaces closed, while access to decent work has deteriorated for all. More information about the impact on women's jobs is included later at the end of Section One.

Following, the report presents women's unique situation by country to allow the reader to understand the realities of women in each of the seven countries. As mentioned before, analyses from country gender profiles are based on a combination of qualitative and quantitative data sources as well as other reliable statistical data sources. It is not intended to be exhaustive, but given the number of countries and difficulties to compare them, this part of the report seeks to provide the panorama of each country presenting the same indicators and trends in order to allow for comparison between countries. The report also includes an analysis on the laws and regulations that limit or foster economic opportunities for women in these countries.

Argentina<sup>1</sup>

Maternal mortality rate is low: 39 per 100,000 births in 2017 (CEPAL), and all Argentinian pregnancies received professional care.

13 percent was the adolescent pregnancy ratio<sup>2</sup> in 2010. In 2015, adolescent pregnancy was 63 per 1,000 women. This rate can be related to the rate of young adults (1 of 5) who are not in employment, education and training, which amounts to 25 percent<sup>3</sup> (INEGI, 2018).

252 women died for domestic violence; 179 women of them were by an intimate partner in 2016 (0.4 per 100,000 women) (CEPAL). In 2016, 30,000 cases of gender-based violence were registered, where the perpetrator was mostly an intimate partner. Data is scarce, often underreported and data collection presents challenges. The majority of victims are young women aged between 15-49 years old. Women who are not harmonized, victims lack confidence and economic autonomy, mistrust the authorities or are unfamiliar with the processes. In its first two years of operation, the free phone line for women victims of gender-based violence received 203 calls (CEPAL).

Partly is reached in secondary education enrollment; and compulsion and girls slightly surpass boys in secondary enrollment (92 and 87 percent respectively) in 2017), but non-indigenous women are four times more likely to finish secondary school than rural indigenous women. Argentinian girls drop out of secondary school at a rate of 35 percent, compared with 46 percent for boys. In contrast, 42 percent of boys left school to work, and 24 percent reported they did not like school.

49 percent of women participate in the labor force, compared to 73 percent of men, and for younger adults (18-25 years old) this rate is even lower at 30 percent. Women’s domestic responsibilities and high teenage pregnancies contribute to the low integration of women into the Argentinian job market in terms of quantity and quality. Informality represents 30 percent of wages earners, and more than half of women are employed informally (INEGI). The rate of female unemployment is 12.5 percent, a proportion that reaches 70 percent in the northern regions of the country (WB 2018). This undermines the acquisition of skills for women to enter the labor force while women’s high domestic responsibilities prevent their integration into labor force (IADB 2016).

39 seats are held by women in the national parliament in 2018 and the female rate for ministers is 17 percent. The previous 30 percent quota for women in candidate lists was increased to 50 percent in 2017.

<sup>2</sup> Adolescent pregnancy ratio is defined as the number of women who secure to have had a living baby divided by the total number of women in the age group, multiplied by 100 (CEPAL)

Brazil

Likewise, in other countries in LAC the Brazilian poorest live in rural areas, but 60 percent of Brazilians poor are urban people. The larger group affected by poverty are Afro-descendants, and extreme poverty hits indigenous people most. Moreover, the multidimensional face of inequality in Brazil is female as well, since women are more likely to be poor than men. In addition, a wide range of organizations—women, feminists, lesbians, domestic workers, indigenous women and more women’s groups have shaped the women’s rights agenda in Brazil. The gender gap in Brazil has been analyzed by researchers almost entirely from the WB Snapshot of Brazil in 2016, which was marked by race and geography.

44 women die for 100,000 births in Brazil in 2017. In 2016, 45 percent of maternal deaths were Afro- Brazilian women and 39 percent were white. Almost all Brazilian women receive prenatal care, but access for afro-descendant and indigenous peoples is different than that for white women. 73 percent of white women get seven or more prenatal visits compared to roughly 50 percent of afro-descendant women and 25 percent of indigenous people’s women.

Adolescent pregnancy rate remains high despite the downward trend since the year 2000. In 2017, the rate was 62 births per 1,000 women aged 15-19.

Femicides reached 1,941 women in 2019. Rate of violence against women by intimate partner in Brazil is 85.3 percent, and 10.7 percent of women have to suffer physical, sexual or psychological gender-based violence. Indigenous women almost double non-indigenous gender-based violence average. Domestic and Family Violence’s Law in Brazil, known as Maria da Penha’s Law, is worldwide known for its comprehensive approach to domestic violence prevention, protection, and enforcement at the sub-national level. Men suffer heavily gang violence and homicide in Brazil.

Partly is achieved in elementary and secondary education while women exceed men in tertiary education enrollment rates in 2017, with 59 percent and 43 percent respectively. Women expect years of schooling to be completed by age 26 (18.5 years) compared to men’s 14.5 years; women are 10.1 years for men. In contrast, when analyzing racial and geographic inequalities, 13-16 years old girls in the Southern region have a higher school attendance rate than their Afro-descendant counterparts while boys are more likely to attend school than Afro-descendants. At tertiary level less than 50 percent of Afro-descendants attend school compared to whites. Same for women studying Science, Technology, Engineering and Mathematics in tertiary education and represent only 24 percent graduates compared to 62.4 percent men.

Female labor participation hasn’t changed much over the last decade and remains low (29 percent in 2014). Gender wage gaps are exacerbated by race and prise in most educated women. Afro-descendant women wages are less than half than white men’s wages.

Female candidates increased 47 percent for the 2018 general election thanks to the 30 percent quota imposed, but it didn’t translate into seats of Deputies with 9.9 percent of women seats (compared to 8.8 seats secured in the previous 2016-election) and 13.6 percent female senators, which is below the average of 24 percent politicians in LAC parliamentary. At the state level only 7 female governor

(WB 2016) A Snapshot of Gender in Brazil Today Institutions, Outcomes, and a Closer Look at Racial and Geographic Differences.

# Colombia

Women’s access to health services has brought numerous positive results in maternal health, but adolescent pregnancy rates* improvements are slow. Mothers’ mortality at birth or during pregnancy have dropped by more than half since 1990. In 2016, 85 percent of pregnant women had access to natal check-ups, and 70 percent of women had access to a skilled professional at delivery.

Teenage pregnancy ratio in 2018 was 11.8 percent. Increase in the use of contraceptives has caused adolescent pregnancy rates (15–19 years old) to fall from 80 births per 1,000 women in 1990 to 50 in 2016. From 2004 to 2017, the proportion of girls who have had sexual intercourse has fallen by more than half, from 30 percent to 14 percent. The trend is less obvious among urban and educated women. However, limited women’s agency* causes that 20 percent of women refuse to use contraception or abortion services because they fear their partners’ reaction.

Gender-based violence is endemic in Colombia. Data from 2015 shows that almost 78 percent of married women and 27 percent of women in union have reported physical and emotional violence by their partners; 27 percent reported having suffered physical violence while 18 percent reported being threatened with violence. The economic cost of domestic violence alone accounts for 4 percent of GDP (Londoño and Guerrero 1999) while the economic cost of domestic violence in general represents 11 percent of the economy (Londoño and Guerrero 1999).

*Girls’ education enrollment and attainment have reached equality, and Colombia is doing better, but women’s participation in tertiary* attains a gender gap. In the adverse pattern of marriage and *girls’ education rates have improved over time; in secondary education 82 percent of girls compared to 76 percent of boys are enrolled. In higher education, 50 percent of women were enrolled compared to 54 percent of men. However, patriarchal family dynamics, involvement in household responsibilities and social norms endanger Colombian girls’ education as absenteeism and dropout rates start at a young age. For example, 28 percent of girls aged 10–14 were not in education, training or employment in 2017.

Economic opportunities have grown for women but the economy of care falls disproportionately in their hands since 37 percent of urban women report to be engaged only on domestic tasks. In 2017, 57.7 percent of women worked part-time and earned less than 60 percent of men’s wages compared with 80.8 percent Colombian men. Moreover, when Colombian women find jobs, these are usually poorly paid and of low quality. Only one third of women contribute to a pension fund compared to more than fifty percent of men.

*Data from July 2015. *Gender-based Gender Assessment

**Agency is the capacity to make decisions about one’s own life and act on them to achieve the desired outcome, free of interference, restrictions or coercion.

*Reyes, R., & Sánchez, F. (2005). Determinants, effects and costs of domestic violence. Documento CEDD Edición electrónica.

*Londoño, A., & Guerrero, M. (1999). Violencia en América Latina - Epidemiología y Límites. Inter-American Development Bank. Documento de Trabajo No 375.

# Costa Rica

27 mothers per 100,000 births died in 2019, since the year 2000 (74 deaths per 100,000) the rate is dropping but regional differences persist.

Adolescent pregnancy has been dropping in the last years, from 18.7 percent in 2010 to 13.2 percent births between women aged 15 and 19 years old in 2019. Adolescent pregnancy is associated with the beginning of sexual relationships and despite being illegal, adolescent-adult sexual relationships occur. 56.7 percent of girls who report having had sex before age 18 say that their partners were below age 14 years old; report that their partners were 3 years older than them. Whereas 8.5 percent of women older than twenty-two years old report that their partners had between 5 and 10 years less than them, which means that when sexual intercourse occurs, the woman is usually younger than her partner.

Women who report living or having lived under some kind of conjugal bond do not attend school, compared to those who are not (17 percent). Moreover, 88.3 percent of the girls reporting living or have lived under conjugal bond have had sexual intercourse before age 15 years old (UNICEF, National Institute of Statistics and Population Fund).

14 women died for gender reasons (homicides) in 2019 at a rate per 100,000 women. 3 of which (0.4 percent of the total) were killed by their intimate partner or ex (EFEPA, 2020). 57.7 percent of women surveyed in 2004 were victims of at least one incident of gender-based violence sometime after they turned 16, whereas 24.1 percent experienced it more than once. Older women, between 25 and 49 years old, reported having been raped (32 percent) and 48 percent reported being victims before turning 15 years old (ENVMC, Household Survey Costa Rica 2004).

Only around 50 percent of working age women are included in the Costa Rican labor force, which in 2019 was 54.3 percent. 76 percent of them work in low productivity sectors. The Latinobarómetro 2013 found that 43 percent of women surveyed in Costa Rica believe that their partner earns more than they do and that their partner is not earning enough (Only 20 percent in Chile citizens think the same). 32.7 percent of women in Costa Rica earn less than the average (in Latin countries) don't have their own income compared to 18.5 percent of men. Besides, women spend more than two-thirds of their time in unpaid domestic work (33:18 per week vs 12:30 per week).**

The education gender gap is closed and near all the population is literate. Girls school attendance is similar to boys in elementary and secondary school. While in tertiary education women's attendance rate is superior than men (67.1 percent vs 50 percent), this does not result in more women participating in productive careers or jobs.

55.17 percent of ministries are held by women since 2018, and 45.5 percent of the seats in the congress are occupied by women at the local level, 14.8 percent of majors and were women and 45.6% of city councilwomen in 2018. Costa Rica is one of the best performing countries in gender equality politics not only in the region but in the World.

The Dominican Republic

The poverty gender gap in the Dominican Republic is the largest in the region[1]. At the end of 2015, the poverty gap was 6.4 percentage points. In the case of women head of household, the poverty gap rises to 7.1 percentage points. The highest rates are for non-educated women (8.6%). People with Haitian origins have worse poverty indicators than the 20 percent poorest of the population[2]. 83 percent of the country's population are Haitian women.

95 Dominican mothers died per 100,000 live births in 2018 [92 vs. 74], while the rate for Haiti was 74. Around 60% of pregnant women have never been to a health facility and only 30% had skilled professionals at delivery. Still, the quality of pregnancy and delivery supervision seems to be the leading cause of the high mortality rates. The mortality rate for sexual and reproductive diseases (other than maternal death) was 50.1 per 100,000 live births.

Dominican Republic ranks first in adolescent pregnancy in the region: 93 birth per 1,000 women, while LAC average is 62, and the average fertility rate for the country is 2.3 children per woman (World Bank 2018). Low educated and poor teens are more affected [50.4 percent women with 1-4 years of education, and 35.3 percent women in poverty] than those who have higher levels of education or wealth.

The probability of getting pregnant before 18 years old for women who have experienced intimate partner violence is 2.6 times higher than women who haven't had this experience. Early pregnancy's probability is 3.0 times higher among women with a history of IPV. Women who have experienced IPV are more likely to start sexual life early and often with older men, and for women who had two or more intimate partners only one in four used contraception at the time of conception. Only 48 percent of women use a modern contraception method (similar to the regional average in 2016).

148 Dominican Republic women died in 2019 victim of gender-based homicides [CEPAL] and the country ranks first in intimate femicides in the LAC region with 1.5 cases for 100,000 women (CEPAL). Intimate partner violence is a major risk factor for intimate femicide. Women who suffered intimate partner violence reported that their partner got drunk regularly compared to 12.5 percent of women whose partner consumes moderately or never. Controlling behaviors produce similar data [46% vs. 37%]. The probability of being beaten by the partner is also higher among IPV victims and GBV are tightly related since prevalence is 2.6 times higher among women who have experienced violence compared to women who haven't (CONAVIHISDA, 2014). Every day four women died due to HIV/AIDS.

[1] World Economic Forum 2020 Global Gender Gap Report 2020

six times the regional average, while mobility situates the Dominican Republic the second in the Caribbean. In 2015 there was no gender gap in HIV/AIDS data.

Girls' enrollment rate equals boys for primary education at 93 percent in 2017 (85 and 84 percent respectively). For secondary school girls' completion is 68 percent compared to 66 percent. The elementary completion rate is similar for both sexes, 94 percent of girls and 96 percent for boys. It exceeds slightly for girls in secondary education at 88 percent compared to 82 percent of boys completing secondary school. Women's graduation rate almost doubles men, with 10 percent and 5 percent respectively.

(WB 2017)

Labor force participation hasn't grown much for women or men in the last two decades. In 2018, the female labor force participation rate was 57 percent women and 82 for men. Vulnerable employment continues to be in the hands of women, who holds 31 percent of all vulnerable employment (39 percent is men's).

Women have doubled their presence in the public sphere in the last years: 27 percent of women occupy the parliament in 2017 (16 in the year 2000). A similar figure, 28 percent of women, have been elected in local government. Women hold 35 percent of senior management employment and middle management is 50 percent of the total, compares to 37 percent in 2000. Twenty-three firms have a top female manager and 70 firms have female participation in ownership.

Mexico18

Poverty in Mexico is feminine, indigenous and rural. Mexico ranks 1st in indigenous people population. 17 percent of population lives in four states with the higher level of indigenous peoples (Chiapas, Oaxaca, Guerrero, and Veracruz) and accounts for 37.5 percent of the population living in poverty (SADIN,2019). For the purpose of this assessment, differences within regions won't be included and data will include the country's average.

Women access to maternal health has improved in the last decade. Maternal deaths have decreased from 86 deaths per 100,000 live births in 1995 to 38 in 2015. Mexico's social inequalities produces different levels of access to maternal care: indigenous women are twice as likely to die during childbirth and their lower access. Maternal mortality rates for indigenous women doubles those of non-indigenous women.

Adolescent pregnancy rate remains high. Fertility rate has been decreasing from 157 births per 100,000 adolescents in 1997 to 74 births per 100,000 adolescents in 2015. Knowledge of contraceptive methods is high in Mexico (86 percent). However, only 35 percent of women who have ever used or tried contraceptive methods reported they wanted to get pregnant. Contraceptive use is low on average, but their annual rate has increased from 47 per thousand women in 2010 to 59 per thousand women in 2016. Women did not know any method compared 0.6 percent with completed secondary education. Studies show that teenage pregnancy's impact on girls reduces in 0.8 years school attainment in the short run and they have one year less of education in the long run.

Urgent action needs to be taken to prevent gender-based violence: seven Mexican women per day were killed in 2019[1], or about one every 384 hours (UNODC 2019). Likewise, in other countries the region advances in legislation have been translated into few actions to protect women. In the case of Mexico, men and women’s homicides have followed the same pattern since they started being recorded: men are killed more often than women. This means that gender-based violence is overlooked and women don’t seek for help probably because of the social acceptability of gender-based violence. Mexican women older than 15 have experienced at least one violent incident (66 percent) or sexual violence (42 percent), and 30 percent of them have been raped (UNODC, 2019[1]). Despite intimate partner violence is the most common in Mexico, violence from aggressors other than intimate partners is increasing.

Prevalence in diabetes mellitus is related to the rates in female obesity, especially in urban areas.7% of DM patients above 18 years old are considered overweight, and 35 percent of them are obese compared to 24 percent of men. There is evidence of gender discrimination against obese women and a study highlighted that the probability of being called back after an interview is low for obese women compared with non-obese women (Bosworth et al., 2018[2]).

Education gender gaps are closed, less girls than boys are out of school but the gap is reversing for boys. On average, Mexican women have 9 years of education compared to 9.53 for men. In 2016, 92 percent of girls graduated from secondary education compared to 86 percent of boys, whereas 28 percent of female students completed tertiary education in Mexico (INEGI, 2017[3]). The gender gap in the tertiary level represented 50.3 percent, 55.2 percent of graduates, and 48.8 percent of graduates[4] in PhD. However, data differs across regions: there are large gaps in some region such as in Chiapas where girls have almost one year less of education than men. Primary and secondary attainment gap is closed, but at the tertiary level women’s attainment is lower than men.

Female and youth labor force participation is low and the care economy has a strong female labor force presence: 45 percent for Mexico, 45.5 percent, and there is an increase in women of 13.5 percent in terms of per capita GDP[5]. Mexico ranks last among OECD countries but according to ILO’s Better Jobs Index Mexico ranks 13 out of 17 countries in the region. The gender gaps in labor participation are due to: (a) a greater concentration of women in low paid jobs, (b) differences in education and training, (c) gender preferences (and/or limitations) in the case of longer or shorter remunerated working hours, (iii) employer discrimination, and (iv) women’s responsibilities in the household, which limit the time dedicated to remuneration work. Women are more likely than men to be employed in informal sectors, especially related to two of men in unpaid households. 30 percent of employees in Mexico work 40 hours per week, which constitutes a barrier for women seeking a paid job. Lack of reliable childcare is another barrier for women seeking a job.

Women’s public representation almost reach parity at federal level and ranks the 4th country on the world with most women in parliament, but numbers remain low at local levels. A quota requires 50 percent of women on candidate list for elections for national and state congress as well as municipal councils. In 2019[6], 48.2 percent of deputies of the federal parliament and 49.2 percent of senators are women.

### Peru

**90 women die during pregnancy or 42 days after delivery in Peru (PAHO, 2017). Maternal mortality is now similar to the regional average [74]. All pregnancies receive follow up care in Peru and almost all deliveries [94 percent] are attended by professional staff. Data for rural, mostly indigenous people women, are not available.** The government has been working on respecting indigenous people's preferences in order to increase maternal care access among this group.

**48.5 births occurred over 1000 women age 15-19 in Peru (WB 2018). This represents about 10 percent of adolescent girls in Peru [Espal 2017]. Even though it is lower than the regional average, it differs among groups; for example, 63.4 percent of rural women have had at least one pregnancy while rural women reached 12.5 (compared to 10.8 percent in urban areas) in 2015.**

**128 women died in femicides in 2019 which represents a 0.8 per 100,000 women; 98 are perpetrated by intimate partners or ex-partners (0.6 per 100,000 women) [CEPAL 2019]. It shows a decreasing tendency over the last five years.** The most common age group for victims is between 35 and 44 years old; 84.6 percent of victims were between 18-34 years and half of the cases occurred inside the home [between 2009-2015] (Instituto Nacional de Estadística del Perú). In 2018, 7.8 percent of women aged 15 to 49 having suffered violence in their lifetime reported that they had experienced psychological (67.4 percent), 32 percent physical and 7.9 percent sexual violence. Even though reporting has increased, most victims do not search for help because they don't trust the institutions and social barriers impede them to have abusive relationships [WB 2018].

**Part is reached in primary and secondary education enrollment, although women in the poorest quintile are less likely to enroll in secondary education. Completion is higher for girls who outperform boys in Peru. The gender gap between men and women aged 45 and for men, this gap has been increasing since 2017 at the advantage of women [WB 2018].**

**Female labor force participation rate was 70 percent for women and 65 percent for men in 2018 (Little data book on gender) [2019]. Larger gaps in labor force participation are found in married women, or women with children. The female labor force participation rate is also lower than the male one; it is high in female labor force participation in the region. Between 1993 and 2007 out of the total increase in the women's employment rate, 27 percent can be attributed to the drop in Peru's women fertility rates, which reached its lowest level at 2.4 children per woman [WB 2018].** In 2018, only 35 percent of Peruvian women aged 15-64 were employed; this is lower than the regional average (47 percent). The employment rate for women in Peru is also lower than that of men. Among women aged 15 to 29 years old are not working or studying (n=0) compared to men, but the share is lower than the regional average. This share seems related to gender roles and the use of time.

**36.2 percent of women are represented in the Low Chamber [CEPAL 2019]. The 30 percent gender quota was established in 1997 and in 2017 women occupied 31.6 percent of ministers and 37 in 2019, although most occupy positions in social areas. Only 2.9% of mayors are women; this percentage has not changed in Peru since 1998 [WB 2018]. Women's representation is also low at the local level. The lack of representation impacts the local government which many do not have representation from the larger political parties, and is likely responsible of the lack of trust by the population in institutions.**

# Laws and Regulations on Economic Opportunities for women

Countries have laws and regulations that foster or impede women’s access to economic opportunities.

The report “Women, Business and the Law 2020” illustrates with seven indicators the legal differences between men and women as they transition through different stages of working life. The table below collects data from seven countries: Argentina, Brazil, Chile, Colombia, Costa Rica, Ecuador, and Peru. A score greater than or equal to 100 indicates that at least one law constrains norms and practices on women’s freedom in a particular indicator, whereas a score less than 100 indicates that only laws and regulations are considered.

| Mobility | Pay | Parenthood | Assets |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Argentina | 95 | 87 | 63 |
| Brazil | 100 | 100 | 100 |
| Chile | 100 | 100 | 100 |
| Colombia | 100 | 100 | 100 |
| Costa Rica | 95 | 72 | 46 |
| Ecuador | 100 | 83 | 64 |
| Peru | 100 | 100 | 100 |

The table reveals that in the 7 Program countries, women do not have legal constraints for movement. The mobility indicator is based on four questions: Can a woman choose where to live in the same way as a man? Can she travel by train or bus without needing an escort? Can she obtain a driver’s license and drive a car in the same way as a man? Can a woman travel outside the country in the same way as a man?

Laws and regulations on inheritance and are not discriminatory for women in any of these countries either.

The best score as a country is Peru, with the only restrictive laws are in marriage and parenthood, while the worse score is for Argentina. In Argentina, a law states that women are not allowed to work at night (nocturnal work), which is considered dangerous. Women can be hired as a cleaner or a worker in cleaning of moving machinery, nor can they be an engine driver stoker. The former is in line with the differential in the pay indicator: women in Argentina cannot work in specific tasks (deemed dangerous) thus, they can be awarded lower wages and higher paid jobs. Similarly, in Costa Rica, laws do not mandate equal remuneration for work of equal value and women are not allowed to work in jobs deemed hazardous.

**Section 2: Overview of Gender Equality and Urban Transport links in these countries**

According to UN Habitat, two-thirds of the population will be living in cities by 2050 (UN Habitat, 2018). Together with overcrowding, overclimbing, lack of modal integration, are some of the problems that the increase of urban population is exacerbating. 46.2% of surveyed people in Peru responded that Transportation is the second most important problem affecting their quality of life (first is insecurity) (UN Habitat, 2018). Women face more difficulties than men to access public transport and to make full use of public transport systems (WB, 2020). Lack of access to transportation and safety issues limits women’s participation in the labor market and reduces their participation in the economy by 36.5 percent. The lack of safe and affordable public transport also affects women’s ability to reach work station (time spent, return trip, timing, expenses). Besides providing access to education, health and jobs, the sector generates jobs itself but traditional employment opportunities are likely to remain men's hands.

Hence, it is important to start by analyzing urban transportation systems that meet the conditions, priorities, roles and beliefs about urban transport infrastructure and services vary between women and men and also influence decisions about mobility are constrains. The lack of certain safety features in the design of the transport system can constrain a woman’s decision to choose a certain mode of

# Mobility in Urban Transport

Female urban transport ridership characteristics differ from that of men. Most women are captives of public transportation (i.e., they don't own a private vehicle as they don't have the means to use one (gas, insurance). For long distances, they become dependents on public transportation (AIFD 2015). Women rely more on public transportation than men, especially buses; women chain different destinations together and travel longer distances. They often travel alone or with a friend, partner, and many times they travel off-peak hours. They are often accompanied by others (Ex: children, elderly) and carry bags. Long-distance trips are often made in the morning and evening rush hour when women combine multiple wage jobs and care responsibilities (family earnings, care for the elderly, the ill, the kids, and their community) in one trip. Sometimes, the nature of the wage work (Ex., as part-time nannies, providers of cleaning services, etc.) makes it difficult to travel during peak hours. If there is no public transport in place, they have to buy a new ticket every time or just walk more. If there is not public transportation available, they just walk but decide or not to travel. Women in these countries, use officially official taxes when traveling by bus and are subject to the same fares as men. In some cities, they use collective taxis ("colectivos" in Buenos Aires and Lima) which have a number of benefits for women: they fill route maps, they provide a safe space where women can talk freely with other women, they allow women taxi drivers discriminate against women with bags, and some cities have subsidies to reduce "taxes" fare for certain passengers (women, students, or elderly). In Lima, women who live in the high hills feel discriminated by the collective taxis because they are not safe. The same thing happens when hailing is being frequently used by women and sometimes it brings cheaper fares to women (WB 2020)

The graph 1 below shows the differences in percentage female-male of their mobility patterns (motorized trips, public transportation, and walking) in seven LAC countries compared to the EU27. Common mobility patterns are observed between men and women in all seven LAC countries except for Mexico where women do more trips by foot than men, they use more public transportation and they less motorized trips compare to men. However, when women are in higher income groups, they show similar mobility patterns as men (i.e., they use more private vehicles). Women's mobility patterns can be a gradual change to individual modes of transportation if perceived safer, or they can choose not to travel if not


---

The growth of motorization rates in the LAC region and its negative consequences on public transportation have a direct impact on women’s mobility. Women use significantly more public transportation than walk, while men prefer to drive. This is reflected in the gender gap of drivers’ licenses by gender, where female drivers' licenses represent nearly 30 percent of the total licenses in the region. In contrast, some countries in Latin America and the Caribbean have a gender gap as high as 50 percent for taxis. In Costa Rica, driving licenses for men double those of women, but when we analyze the type of license the gender gap is even bigger for productive activities. Only 10 percent are authorized bus drivers, and between 20% to 30% are authorized taxi drivers. Most airports and regular taxis are owned by women (it might not reflect women taxi drivers, only ownership).

The mobility of care is simplified by data collection methods. For the purpose of designing and calculating transport, data collection methods focus traditionally in one trip pattern (Ex: travel to and from work or to and from study) while the mobility related to care responsibilities is overlooked because it is fractured in multitude purposes and fall in different categories (Ex: shopping, or escorting). The graph 1 below, shows the distribution of trips left and right new data. It shows that women have more activities related to care on the right (maintenance of the household and care for others). The number of trips for care work is higher than those for commuting. This gender bias has led researchers and practitioners to have undervalued the "reproductive" trips, considering only the "productive" trips for the design of transport systems. This gender bias underestimates others’ ridership than commuting, and a poor urban transport design has negative consequences for the agency of women.

## To give visibility to mobility patterns, the IDB has a Client Map Methodology[2] which breaks into 12 segments a trip's experience in Bogotá, Santiago and Medellín. Then different profiles of riders (women, elderly, people with disabilities) are assessed and the results help to design better urban transport.

Fare schemes can foster women’s use of public transportation. Women have more price sensitivity than men because they are responsible for household expenses. The poor pay more for fares while the rich and the poor suffer the most with unregulated tariffs (CAF 2020). Urban transportation must promote a fare structure that subsidizes short trips or propose an integrated fare system. Women’s value of time differs from men, as women have to balance their work responsibilities with household duties. Women are very concerned about the time of their return home, so besides safety, waiting time and fare are key to their decisions. Further, because most women's trips are not related to income generating activities, women might prefer to use public transportation at night when it is cheaper.

The IDB has supported several initiatives in Latin America aimed at increasing transport tariff in 2016 and la Defensoría del Pueblo[3] (Office of the Ombudsman) received considerable feedback from vulnerable riders, which all percent women with small kids, and specifically domestic workers.

Ride-hailing has become an alternative for women to increase their urban mobility in safer conditions than other modes. The main characteristics of ride-hailing, many times match women’s unique needs. Its on-demand nature allows women to plan trips according to their schedule, the flexibility it offers in public transportation given that public transportation mostly covers mass-based trips (from home to work/child centers), and women's perception of security augments when ride-hailing at night, especially when the

Gender-segregated ride-hailing benefits female passengers and drivers. Safety and security are the most limiting factors and a major factor for female (and men) in mobility and access to jobs. In 2018, a survey carried out in 6 countries (Mexico being one of them) for the IFC® found that 20 percent of women riders reported that the lack of women drivers reduces the trip they would make, whereas 44 percent said that they would not take a ride-hailing service if it was only available to men. In Mexico City, 51% of women said that ride-hailing is an alternative for women when there are gaps in the public transport system since women’s trips are channeled, because it has become a safer mode of transportation thanks to technology. At Uber Brazil, more than half of the women surveyed (54%) said they would not use the service if it was only available to men.

## Safety in Urban Transport

Six out of 10 women reported to have been harassed in public transportation in Latin American countries. In 2014, a “survey” in 35 out of the 20 largest cities in the World, asked 6550 women and experts in urban planning, about their perceptions when traveling in public transportation. Women’s perceptions were collected through an online survey that included questions on whether they had been verbally or physically harassed, the likelihood that other passengers would come to their assistance, the truth of the statements “I would investigate reports of harassment or violence” and the availability of safe public transport. The graph 4 below reveals the ranking of the most dangerous transport systems for women found by this study.

# Gender Assessment E-Mobility Program

GRUTTER CONSULTING


Bogotá, the world’s 16th largest capital city, was ranked as the most unsafe city for women to travel alone at night. More than one out of five (20 percent), in Mexico City, 45 percent, and in Santiago by YouGov said they had been groped or experienced physical harassment on public transport. The same study found that women felt more dangerous as women riders, and Buenos Aires came as 46th. The 2018 study “Ella se mueve segura” (‘She moves safely’) found that 57 percent of women reported being afraid of suffering a sexual attack in the transport systems of Latin America. In Guadalajara 62 percent of women reported being afraid of suffering a sexual attack, and 60 percent and 64 percent of women reported being victims of sexual harassment in Lima and Bogotá respectively. Women are also more likely to be harassed by strangers than by acquaintances or friends, and many times happens traveling from and to the transport systems[1]. In Costa Rica, 80 percent of women report being victims of violent situation in public spaces[2].

Perception of transport systems safety shapes women’s agency in mobility in such a way that they rather pay higher fares for safer modes than accept unsafe risks. In Rio de Janeiro, minivans are more expensive, their trips are more delayed and queues are longer than trains for long distance commutes. Nonetheless, young women choose to travel in minivans because they feel protected from harassment[3]. Women’s perception of safety is also influenced by the quality of urban transport infrastructure.

Adequate urban transport infrastructure can boost women’s participation in the sector, both as passengers and providers. Good lighting and vigilance cameras can transform women’s perception of safety and discourage perpetrators from committing crimes. Both simple solutions prevent crimes, not only criminalizing them. Cameras are a cost-effective solution that is easy to implement and recently they are installed in buses to protect both passengers and drivers. Installation, operation, and maintenance costs for cameras are low compared with other security measures[4]. Lighting and cameras is the most effective solution[5]. Since 2014, Buenos Aires installed cameras and GPS in public transport units to monitor traffic registered inside and outside the bus. These kinds of tools can potentially reduce crime rates by making it more difficult for perpetrators to commit crimes in poorly crowded buses, poorly lighted park-and-ride lots represent stressful settings for women that limit their mobility agency. The surroundings of the transport infrastructure play a role in creating traveling stress for women[6]. Women feel safer when they travel on well-lit streets and when there are no stops between bus stop at night. These random bus stop locations might be better lit or be closer to women’s destination, which can improve their perception of safety at night. In Montreal and Toronto (Canada) these stops are permitted (CAF 2020).

Women adopt specific strategies, which can work or not, but make women feel safer in the public transport system. Sexual harassment in public transport includes leering, winking, offensive gestures, unwanted touching, verbal abuse, and even rape[7]. Women’s perception of safety is influenced by their physical environment (e.g., lighting, cleanliness) as well as by social factors such as gender norms (WIEC, IADB, ICRI, 2015). Women change clothing, take long detours to avoid unsafe places, board crowed buses or stay near the walls, and even carry non-lethal weapons. They feel safer when traveling in a group of women[8]. In Mexico City, women who travel with other women report having developed friendly relationships with drivers. In Manizales (Colombia), a medium-sized city where trips usually need only one mode of transportation, 73 percent of women have suffered at least one incident of harassment, sexual violence, or street abuse, and 41 percent changed their routines because of them. These women

# Campaigns to raise awareness about harassment together with safer protocols to report abuses can protect women and girls in public transport

### Mobile applications facilitate women protection and contribute to gathering data, which supply information to design safer transport infrastructure and raise awareness on harassment.

Digital technology helps women track and share their location with family and friends. Ride-hailing companies have developed apps that allow passengers to rate the driver and the car's safety features. Passengers can also document the driver's professionalism, cleanliness of the vehicle, and quality of their trip in Brazil. "Chega de Frustrar" (Enough whistling) is a mobile application that allows women to report harassment on public transport.

### SafetyNet

SafetyNet is an app developed by the World Bank Group that uses machine learning algorithms to identify different routes and places by using data. The app also documents the woman's regular interaction when walking and using public transportation to raise awareness of the harassment that women suffer routinely. In 2018, Bogotá used the "Me Muero Seguro" study, which documented the frequency with which women are harassed on public transport in different areas.

### SafetyNet

SafetyNet is based on nine variables to obtain a safety score: Level of lighting, number of people per square meter, presence or absence of security, Emergency (agits), Walk/footability, Availability of public transport. Usage of the public space (for instance it is male-dominated or mixed) and Feeling emotional response to the scene (sometimes also called "fear factor"). The app can be used in different cities around the world.

### Study participants

The study participants were below 35 years old and 78 percent work or study, and 50 percent finished tertiary education. This is important information when considering when implement technology to protect women in public transport since affordability (costs of telephones or internet) and user knowledge can be barriers for many women**, especially the poorest.

### The transport sector can serve as a catalyst to teach women their rights

The Via Lútea program was launched in 2015 in Rio de Janeiro as part of the implementation of the Maria da Penha's law on gender-based violence. Electronic kiosks, "tornos" were installed in the Supera Río Suburban rail line, which included:

- **AID mobilizing (Ag 2019): La movilidad sostenible como herramienta para el desarrollo local y regional para las mujeres**
- **AID mobilizing (Unidades de en el mundo digital): Brecha de género en el uso de las TIC, Washington, D.C.**

**Source:** https://blogs.worldbank.org/transport/more-inclusive-use-transport-infrastructure-help-reduce-gender-based-violence

serve around 700,000 daily users. Anonymously, women seek data on the health network, women's rights laws, and gender-based violence protocols.

Sensitization and prevention campaigns on Gender-Based Violence can equip public transport workers with tools to combat it. In the Dominican Republic, a gender-based violence campaign delivered workshops to drivers' trade unions working in buses and "motonchistas" (drivers of private cars that operate as public transport). The workshops included topics such as new masculinities and gender-based violence in bus conductors' spaces and with passengers in the transport environment. (ILO Gender Lab 2019)

Feedback and grievance mechanisms located in the transport systems help women and girls to inform authorities when they are victims of violence. Capacity building and coordination among different bodies (police, prosecutors, judges) can also be strengthened by involving women's organizations, ombuds, survivors of abuse perceive authorities in charge of taking care of them as lacking credibility (WB 2020).

Lack of trust in authorities is common: women prevent from reporting abuses. In a survey in 2016 in Lima, 35 percent of public transport users don't trust police in arriving at the scene when reporting the rape of a woman on the public transport system. For 41.5 percent their trust is low, 18.9 percent reported some trust, and 3.9 percent said that "police will arrive."

Jobs and Urban Transport

The transport sector provides more men women access to education, health and economic opportunities. In this context, focusing only on the jobs that the sector generates by itself, jobs can be generated during the design and at construction of the transport infrastructure, and during its operations and maintenance. In this program, most jobs will be generated during the operation and maintenance of the e-buses, e-taxis, and trains in the public transport system. The creation of new infrastructure are common to boost the economy and it will be the case after this health crisis; thus, this is a unique opportunity to lever job potential.

The gender gap in jobs in the transport sector is notorious. Typically, women work in feminized and low-paid tasks such as cleaning or administration, while men work as drivers, mechanics, or supervisors. Thus, both the number of women hired or trained and the quality of their contracts are low. Specific efforts should be made to attract more women into these jobs, which are critical to attract, secure and maintain jobs with more vacancies (ex: commercial drivers), with a higher paycheck (ex: mechanics), or that are involved in decision making (investors).

The gender gap is also evident when looking at the proportion of women working in the care sector (traditionally dominated by women), compared to 6 percent of men. This reflects the regional wage gap in which women earn between 5 and 30 percent less than men with similar educational level and demographic characteristics[2].

Another call searched for women for the "Salvando Vidas" (Saving lives) program whose focus was on improving women's perception of the police as a transparent department that applies law to everyone.

# Women, Jobs and COVID-19

Women’s labor force participation has been steadily rising in the last decade for each of the seven countries until the pandemic. The devastating impact of COVID-19 has affected disproportionately women's labor force participation. The large gender gaps in employment favor men, but these gaps were gradually narrowing over the last years for all countries. The COVID-19 pandemic has left women in a worse situation than before.

The sex-disaggregated table 1 below provides a glimpse of the labor supply for the last years, which in the second quarter of 2020 has dropped around 10 points in Argentina, Colombia, Dominican Republic and Mexico. In Costa Rica, women's rate is almost at its lowest level since 2011, while the gap between men and women’s labor force participation was narrowing from around 20-25 difference points to 20 in the first quarter of 2020. In Peru, women’s rate has dropped by more than 30 points, falling below 70 points. In Costa Rica women's rate in the 2nd quarter of 2020 is almost the same that that of 2011.

In contrast with these dropping numbers globally, 70% percent of nurses, front-line care workers, midwives, and community health workers are women, who are more likely to be at risk during this pandemic crisis. Women working in the health sector are more affected by COVID-19, and studies from the USA and European countries have revealed that positive COVID-19 cases are two to three times higher for women than for their male colleagues.

2) Solidarity Center 2020: “Domestic workers left out in the cold.” Accessed 10 August 2020.
